## Oral defense / Viva voce {#oral-defense-viva-voce}

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Type | prevention_method |
| Category | assessment_design |

### Định nghĩa & Khái niệm

**Định nghĩa:**

> Oral defense (viva voce, Latin: 'living voice') is an assessment method in which a student must verbally explain, justify, and defend their work before an examiner or panel. In the context of academic integrity, it serves as an authentication mechanism: a student who did not actually engage with the work they submitted cannot convincingly explain it under direct questioning. The 'spot viva' model — randomly sampling a subset of written submissions for a 15–20 minute oral follow-up — applies this principle at scale without requiring all students to undergo a full oral examination.

**Phân loại vi phạm:**

> Prevention method against multiple forms of academic dishonesty: (1) AI-assisted dishonesty — student cannot explain content generated by an LLM; (2) contract cheating — student cannot explain work produced by a third party; (3) collusion — individual oral questioning reveals whether the student has genuine understanding of 'group' contributions. Unlike detection tools that analyze text artifacts, oral defense authenticates the person rather than the product.

**Lịch sử khái niệm:**

> Oral examination is among the oldest forms of academic assessment, predating written examinations in universities. The viva voce for PhD theses has been standard practice in UK and Australian universities for over a century. The 'spot viva' as a scalable anti-cheating mechanism for undergraduate coursework gained renewed attention after ChatGPT's release (November 2022), with prominent advocacy in Times Higher Education (2023) and The Conversation (2023). University of Western Ontario implemented oral exams for a 600-student undergraduate business class as early as 2023–2024.

### Bằng chứng thực tế

**Tên trường hợp:**

> Spot viva implementations at University of Sydney (Australia), UC San Diego (USA), and University of Western Ontario (Canada), 2023–2025. Macquarie University Law School (Australia) adopted viva voce assessment for undergraduate law students, explicitly citing academic integrity and employability goals.

**Diễn biến:**

> Following the mass adoption of generative AI tools in 2022–2023, universities worldwide faced an authentication crisis: written submissions could not reliably be attributed to students. University of Western Ontario implemented oral exams for an undergraduate business cohort of approximately 600 students. University of Sydney updated its Academic Integrity Policy (2023) to treat unapproved AI use as a breach, and oral assessment is one recommended verification mechanism. UC San Diego's Academic Integrity Office, led by director Tricia Bertram Gallant, acknowledged that ensuring students do not use AI is 'challenging' if they are not completing work in front of an assessor. Times Higher Education published an opinion piece (2023) by academics advocating sampled vivas as a scalable, cost-effective solution for large cohorts.

**Kết quả xử lý:**

> No punishment case per se — oral defense is a preventive design, not an investigation tool. When implemented as spot viva: students who engaged genuinely with their work pass without difficulty; students who submitted AI-generated or third-party work typically cannot explain methodological choices, sources, or reasoning, which results in grade revision or academic misconduct referral. Macquarie Law School reports improved student engagement and employability outcomes alongside integrity benefits.

### Tác hại & Hậu quả

**Hại cho người học:**

> Oral defense is generally beneficial for learners who engage authentically: it develops communication skills, metacognitive reflection, and discipline-specific discourse — all valued in professional contexts. The harm is asymmetric: students with social anxiety, speech disabilities, or non-native speaker disadvantage may perform below their actual knowledge level. Students with autism spectrum conditions or selective mutism face accessibility barriers. These concerns are well-documented in assessment literature and require reasonable accommodations.

**Hại cho nghiên cứu:**

> Oral defense is an integrity mechanism, not itself a source of harm to knowledge. However, if spot vivas are conducted inconsistently or with poorly trained examiners, they may produce unreliable assessments, creating false negatives (missing genuine misconduct) or false positives (penalizing anxious but honest students). The research gap is the lack of large-scale empirical studies on spot viva effectiveness and fairness across student demographics.

**Hại cho xã hội:**

> Widespread adoption of spot vivas, if implemented fairly, would restore confidence in the authenticity of academic credentials — directly counteracting the credentialing crisis created by AI-assisted cheating. The cost barrier (examiner time, scheduling) may entrench quality gaps between well-resourced and under-resourced institutions, potentially widening inequality.

### Phát hiện & Công cụ

**Công cụ phát hiện:**

> Oral defense is itself the detection mechanism — no software tool involved. The examiner functions as the detector by probing understanding through targeted questions. Unlike automated tools (Turnitin AI, GPTZero), oral defense has no false positive rate when the examiner is competent: a student who understands their work will demonstrate it. The limitation is examiner skill and bias rather than algorithmic error.

**Hạn chế công cụ:**

> Cost and scalability are the primary constraints: a 15–20 minute spot viva requires significant faculty time. For a course of 300 students with 10% random sampling, this is approximately 10 hours of examiner time per assessment cycle. Accessibility challenges for students with speech/communication disabilities require accommodation planning. Cross-cultural communication differences may disadvantage international students. Examiner bias (accent, presentation style) can introduce unfairness not present in written assessment.

**LLM Watermarking:**

> Oral defense and LLM watermarking address complementary aspects of the same problem. Watermarking (Kirchenbauer/UMD, Aaronson/OpenAI) embeds detectable statistical signals in AI-generated text, enabling post-hoc detection in written submissions. Oral defense prevents the problem upstream by requiring students to authenticate their understanding in real time. If watermarking matures, it may reduce the need for resource-intensive oral assessment; conversely, oral defense remains robust even against watermark-stripping techniques.

### Phòng ngừa & Giải pháp

**Thiết kế bài tập:**

> The spot viva model: (1) Students submit written work as normal; (2) A random 10–20% sample is selected after submission; (3) Selected students attend a 15–20 minute oral follow-up with examiner; (4) Examiner asks questions drawn directly from the submitted work — methodology, sources, reasoning, implications; (5) Oral performance is integrated into the final grade. Variants: process-based submission (Google Docs history, draft progression) combined with spot viva for flagged cases. University of Sydney recommends oral components for high-stakes assessments. Macquarie Law School uses structured viva rubrics for formative and summative assessment.

**Chính sách trường học:**

> University of Sydney (2023 Academic Integrity Policy): unapproved AI use is a breach; oral assessment recommended as verification. UC San Diego: Academic Integrity Office acknowledges limitations of text-based detection; promotes authentic assessment including oral components. University of Western Ontario: implemented oral exams at scale for large undergraduate cohorts. Macquarie University: formal viva voce assessment in law faculty with published teaching resources (TECHE, 2023). UK universities: many retain viva option in regulations for investigating suspected written assessment misconduct.

**Văn hóa tổ chức:**

> Oral defense aligns with McCabe's honor code culture approach: it shifts the assessment environment toward demonstrated understanding rather than punitive surveillance. Students who know a spot viva is possible are incentivized to genuinely engage with assigned material — changing behavior at the assignment design level rather than relying on detection after-the-fact. This is consistent with McCabe's finding that honor-code cultures outperform enforcement-only cultures in long-term integrity outcomes.

**AI Literacy:**

> Implementing spot vivas is itself a form of AI literacy intervention: by requiring students to articulate their reasoning, educators signal that AI tools cannot substitute for genuine understanding. Faculty training for oral assessment (question design, bias mitigation, accessibility accommodations) constitutes an important form of educator AI literacy. ISTE frameworks support authentic assessment practices that measure critical thinking, which oral defense inherently does.

### Khung pháp lý & Đạo đức

**Khung quốc tế:**

> UNESCO 2023 guidance on AI in education recommends 'assessment redesign' as a primary institutional response to AI-assisted cheating — oral defense is explicitly consistent with this recommendation. EU AI Act (2024) Annex III classifies AI systems used in 'education and vocational training' as high-risk where they determine access or outcomes; oral defense as human-administered assessment is not affected by AI Act restrictions.

**Pháp luật Việt Nam:**

> Vietnam's Nghị định 109/2022/NĐ-CP (Điều 20) establishes academic integrity requirements for scientific activities but does not specify assessment modalities. Oral defense (vấn đáp) is standard in Vietnamese higher education for thesis defense and some professional examinations, but is rarely applied as a routine anti-cheating mechanism for coursework. No Vietnamese regulatory framework specifically mandates or encourages spot vivas. The assessment remains at institutional discretion.

### Góc nhìn EdTech

**AI tutor vs AI cheater:**

> Oral defense directly resolves the Rose Luckin (UCL) / Neil Selwyn (Monash) debate at the assessment level: it is agnostic to how students used AI tools during learning, and evaluates only whether genuine understanding was achieved. A student who used AI as a tutor (Luckin's positive framing) will be able to demonstrate knowledge; a student who used AI to produce work without engagement (Selwyn's concern) will not. This makes oral defense uniquely suited as an integration-friendly assessment: it accommodates AI-assisted learning while preventing AI-substituted learning.

**Chính sách EdTech companies:**

> EdTech platforms have begun integrating oral/viva elements: Coursera's 'AI-based Viva Exam' (June 2024) generates follow-up questions from submitted written work — a digital analogue of the spot viva. Oral defense in traditional settings requires no EdTech infrastructure. The Duolingo English Test uses human-in-the-loop proctoring for spoken components. Khan Academy's Khanmigo uses Socratic dialogue — philosophically adjacent to the oral defense principle of requiring students to explain their reasoning.

**Đề xuất nhà nghiên cứu EdTech:**

> From an EdTech researcher perspective: (1) Spot viva should be adopted as standard practice for high-stakes coursework — random sampling at 10–20% makes it cost-effective; (2) Institutions should develop clear rubrics, training for examiners, and accessibility accommodation protocols before rollout; (3) Oral defense should be presented to students as a learning opportunity, not a surveillance mechanism — framing matters for trust; (4) Research priority: large-scale empirical studies on spot viva effectiveness, fairness across student demographics, and optimal sampling rates; (5) Combination design: written submission + process evidence (draft history) + spot viva creates a layered authentication system more robust than any single method.

### Nguồn tài liệu

**Bài báo khoa học:**

> Dawson, P. (2023). 'Sampled vivas are pivotal in combating AI cheating.' Times Higher Education (opinion). Macquarie University TECHE (2023): 'Safeguarding academic integrity, connecting law students with markers: Assessment via viva voce.' The Conversation (2023): 'Why universities should return to oral exams in the AI and ChatGPT era.' Researchgate: 'Viva Voce (Oral Examination) as an Assessment Method.' Springer Nature: 'AI-Based Digital Cheating At University, and the Case for New Ethical Pedagogies' (2025, Journal of Academic Ethics, doi:10.1007/s10805-025-09642-y).

**Sách tham khảo:**

> Murray, R. (2011). How to Survive Your Viva: Defending a Thesis in an Oral Examination. McGraw-Hill/Open University Press. Bretag, T. (ed.) (2016). Handbook of Academic Integrity. Springer. Eaton, S.E. (2024). Second Handbook of Academic Integrity. Springer.

**Nguồn Việt Nam:**

> Oral defense (vấn đáp/bảo vệ khóa luận) is institutionalized in Vietnamese higher education for thesis work under MOET regulations. No specific Vietnamese source on spot viva as an AI-cheating prevention mechanism for coursework. The approach is highly relevant to Vietnamese context given rising AI use among students (Nghiên cứu Springer 2024: 1,386 sinh viên Việt Nam sample). Institutional adoption would require adaptation of current assessment norms.

### Uncertain Fields

- con_so_thong_ke (no comprehensive cross-institutional dataset on spot viva adoption rates)
- chinh_sach_chau_a (no comprehensive pan-Asian data on spot viva adoption)

## Process-based assessment {#process-based-assessment}

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Type | prevention_method |

### Định nghĩa & Khái niệm

**Định nghĩa:**

> Process-based assessment is an assessment design approach that evaluates students on the demonstrated process of learning and knowledge construction, rather than (or in addition to) the final product submitted. In the context of AI academic integrity, process-based assessment is positioned as the most robust structural approach to AI-resistant assessment because it requires evidence of authentic intellectual work over time — evidence that is difficult for students to fabricate using AI alone. Core mechanisms include: (1) staged submissions (multiple check-ins over an assignment's development); (2) revision history documentation (Google Docs activity tracking, version control); (3) reflective journals (metacognitive commentary on learning); (4) annotated bibliographies that demonstrate the actual research process (not just the sources used). The approach shifts the assessment question from 'what did you produce?' to 'how did you learn?'

**Phân loại vi phạm:**

> Process-based assessment is a prevention method — it restructures the assessment environment rather than detecting violations after the fact. It addresses multiple violation types simultaneously: (1) AI-generated content fraud (the process evidence is hard to AI-generate convincingly); (2) contract cheating (the process evidence requires personal engagement over time); (3) plagiarism (staged submissions reveal if ideas appeared without development). It is distinct from detection tools (Turnitin, GPTZero) and from punitive policies — it reduces the opportunity for fraud rather than punishing it after detection.

**Lịch sử khái niệm:**

> Process-based assessment has roots in writing pedagogy (process writing approach, Murray 1972; Elbow 1973) and portfolio assessment movements of the 1980s-1990s. It gained new relevance in the academic integrity literature post-2002 (contract cheating rise) and has been revived as the primary AI-resistant assessment strategy post-ChatGPT (2022-present). Key scholarly advocates post-2022: Perkins et al. (2023), Eaton (2024), the Chartered College of Teaching UK (2023 guidance), and the University of Sydney's Learning Futures program. The approach is consistent with constructivist learning theory — it assesses genuine knowledge construction rather than final-product reproduction.

### Bằng chứng thực tế

**Tên trường hợp:**

> Key adopter institutions: (1) University of Sydney — Learning Futures program; comprehensive assessment redesign including staged submissions, oral components, and process portfolios; explicitly designed as AI-resistant framework (published 2023). (2) University of Wyoming — Prof. Catherine Hartmann's documented use of reflective journals and revision history in writing courses; published case study in academic integrity literature [citation uncertain]. (3) Stanford University — academic integrity policies updated 2023 to include process documentation requirements; Foundation Course assessments redesigned. (4) Chartered College of Teaching UK — 2023 guidance document recommending process-based assessment as primary AI-integrity strategy for UK educators.

**Diễn biến:**

> Staged submissions: the assignment is broken into checkpoints — topic proposal → outline → first draft → peer review → revised draft → final submission. Each checkpoint is submitted and reviewed; the development trajectory across stages provides evidence of genuine intellectual work. Google Docs revision history: instructors can review the edit history of a shared document, showing when content was added, deleted, or revised. Rapid large text insertions (characteristic of copy-paste from AI tools) are distinguishable from gradual, incremental writing development. Reflective journals: students write metacognitive entries about their learning process — 'what was difficult, what changed my thinking, what I would do differently.' These are highly personal and difficult to AI-generate convincingly. Annotated bibliography: students not only list sources but explain how each source influenced their thinking, showing an authentic research process.

### Tác hại & Hậu quả

**Hại cho người học:**

> Process-based assessment is the assessment design most aligned with genuine learning outcomes. Students who engage with staged submissions, reflective journals, and annotated bibliographies are doing the intellectual work that education is supposed to develop — even if they also use AI tools for drafting assistance. The approach reduces the 'fake it until you make it' dynamic of final-only assessment, where high AI fraud rates (Springer 2024: 3x self-reported) mean many students are graduating without the competencies their credentials claim.

**Hại cho nghiên cứu:**

> The process-based approach, adapted to research contexts, is consistent with open science practices (pre-registration, analysis plans, documented methodology changes). Requiring graduate students to document their research process (analysis history, draft evolution) has integrity benefits beyond AI fraud prevention — it also reduces fabrication and falsification opportunities.

**Hại cho xã hội:**

> If process-based assessment becomes the standard in Vietnamese HE (consistent with QĐ 1705's competency-based education mandate), it would produce graduates whose credentials reflect documented, verified competency development — strengthening the value of Vietnamese degrees in both domestic and international labor markets.

### Phát hiện & Công cụ

**Công cụ phát hiện:**

> Process-based assessment reduces reliance on post-submission detection tools (Turnitin AI, GPTZero). Google Docs revision history is the most technically robust process-tracking tool: it is built into Google Workspace (widely available in Vietnamese universities through Google for Education programs), produces timestamped activity logs, and is difficult to falsify convincingly. LMS analytics (Moodle, Canvas) can also track submission timing, session length, and activity patterns — providing process evidence without requiring separate document platforms.

**Hạn chế công cụ:**

> Key limitations: (1) Significant instructor workload increase — reviewing multiple submission stages and revision histories requires substantially more grading time than single-submission assessment; (2) Fabricable process evidence — students can create multiple Google Doc versions rapidly, copy-paste staged to simulate revision, or write generic reflective journal entries without genuine reflection; (3) Scalability challenges — difficult to implement in large lecture courses (200+ students) without significant TA support; (4) Equity concerns — students with competing work/family demands face higher barriers to staged submission timelines; (5) Not applicable to all assessment types — quantitative, laboratory, and performance-based assessments have different process-documentation needs; (6) Requires faculty training and buy-in — significant professional development investment; (7) Privacy concerns with revision history sharing (student consent required in some jurisdictions).

**LLM Watermarking:**

> Process-based assessment and LLM watermarking are complementary rather than competing approaches. Process documentation could include a watermark verification step: if major LLMs implement production watermarking, instructors could verify that text appearing in a Google Doc in a single large insertion block is watermarked AI output — combining process evidence with watermark detection. In the current absence of production watermarking, process evidence is the most reliable available mechanism.

### Phòng ngừa & Giải pháp

**Thiết kế bài tập:**

> Process-based assessment IS the primary 'AI-resistant assessment design' approach. Specific design principles: (1) Break high-stakes summative assessments into low-stakes formative components; (2) Require process artifacts (drafts, outlines, journal entries, annotation) that are inherently personal; (3) Use oral components at the end (brief presentation or discussion of the work) to verify that students understand and can discuss what they submitted; (4) Design reflective prompts that require personal, situated reflection — 'how has this assignment changed your thinking about X?' — rather than generic prompts that AI can answer convincingly; (5) For research assignments, require annotated bibliography entries that explain how each source changed the student's argument, not just what it said.

**Chính sách trường học:**

> University of Sydney's Learning Futures framework is the most comprehensive institutional implementation — it includes: faculty training workshops, standardized staged submission templates, LMS integration, and student orientation materials explaining the process-based approach. Stanford's 2023 policy update added process documentation requirements to high-risk assessments. The Chartered College of Teaching UK published a practical guide (freely available) for educators implementing process-based approaches in their subject areas.

**Văn hóa tổ chức:**

> Process-based assessment is the assessment design approach most consistent with McCabe's honor code culture argument: it builds integrity structurally (by making genuine engagement the path of least resistance) rather than relying on detection and punishment. An institution that invests in process-based assessment is investing in a values-based culture, not an enforcement-first culture. Faculty buy-in is essential — instructors who believe students are fundamentally honest are more likely to implement process-based approaches; instructors who believe enforcement is the primary tool will default to detection tools.

**AI Literacy:**

> Process-based assessment is naturally aligned with AI literacy education: stages of the process can include explicit reflection on AI tool use — 'what AI tools did I use, how, and why?' This turns the assessment into a space for developing AI literacy alongside subject-area learning. Students who document their AI use as part of a reflective journal are practicing the kind of critical AI engagement that AI literacy programs aim to develop.

### Khung pháp lý & Đạo đức

**Khung quốc tế:**

> UNESCO 2023 GenAI guidance explicitly recommends process-based and competency demonstration assessment as alternatives to product-only assessment in AI contexts. EU AI Act Article 50 (transparency of AI-generated content) can be operationalized through process documentation requirements. ICAI 6-value framework supports process assessment: the Fairness value requires that assessments give students equitable opportunities to demonstrate genuine learning — process-based assessment more fairly assesses genuine learning than AI-vulnerable product assessments.

**Pháp luật Việt Nam:**

> QĐ 1705's competency-based education mandate directly supports process-based assessment as the pedagogical model — assessing competency development (not just knowledge output) inherently requires process evidence. Decree 109/2022's fraud prevention mandate can be operationally addressed through process-based assessment, even without explicit legal guidance on AI. Vietnamese universities implementing process-based assessment are fulfilling both documents' intent simultaneously.

**Chính sách châu Á:**

> NUS (Singapore) has integrated process-based components (including AI use disclosure and process documentation) into its AI Academic Integrity policy (2023/2024). NTU has similar provisions. Japanese universities (Waseda, University of Tokyo) are experimenting with portfolio-based assessment as part of broader curriculum reform. Process-based assessment is consistent with the Confucian educational tradition's emphasis on cultivation (修身) and effort over static assessment of accumulated knowledge.

### Góc nhìn EdTech

**AI tutor vs AI cheater:**

> Process-based assessment operationalizes the AI-tutor distinction: if a student can document a genuine learning process that includes AI tool use as one input among many (drafting assistance, feedback, idea generation) alongside personal reflection, revision, and demonstrated understanding, the AI was a tutor — it aided learning. If a student's 'process' shows instant appearance of polished content without development, AI was a substitute — it enabled cheating. The process evidence makes the distinction auditable.

**Chính sách EdTech companies:**

> Google Workspace for Education provides revision history at no additional cost — making process documentation infrastructure available to Vietnamese universities on Google's education platform. Moodle (widely used in Vietnamese HE) has assignment submission logging and timeline features that can support staged submissions. Khan Academy's Khanmigo (Socratic AI tutor) is designed to generate process evidence — it records learning interactions, making the AI-tutoring process visible rather than invisible.

**Đề xuất nhà nghiên cứu EdTech:**

> As an EdTech researcher: (1) Process-based assessment should be the primary recommendation in any Vietnamese HE AI integrity strategy — it is culturally consistent with QĐ 1705, avoids the ESL discrimination problem of detection tools, and directly assesses the competencies Vietnamese education policy values; (2) Propose a national pilot program (funded under QĐ 1705's digital education infrastructure provisions) implementing process-based assessment in 5 universities across different disciplines; (3) Use Google Workspace for Education revision history as the primary process-tracking tool — widely available, free, technically robust; (4) Address the workload limitation by designing assessment schemes where process documentation replaces (not adds to) traditional exam formats; (5) Include process-based assessment as the primary AI literacy module in faculty professional development; (6) Document and publish Vietnamese-context case studies of process-based assessment implementation to build an evidence base for national policy adoption; (7) Acknowledge the fabrication limitation honestly: process-based assessment reduces but does not eliminate AI fraud — it should be combined with occasional oral components for final verification.

### Nguồn tài liệu

**Bài báo khoa học:**

> Perkins, M. et al. (2023) 'Academic integrity considerations of AI LLMs.' Journal of Academic Ethics. Eaton, S.E. (2024) Second Handbook of Academic Integrity — chapters on AI-resistant assessment. Cotton, D. et al. (2023) 'Chatting and cheating' Innovations in Education and Teaching International. Dawson, P. (2021) Defending Assessment Security in a Digital World (foundational text on process-based approaches to assessment integrity).

**Sách tham khảo:**

> Eaton, S.E. (2024) Second Handbook of Academic Integrity. Dawson, P. (2021) Defending Assessment Security in a Digital World. Bretag, T. (2016) Handbook of Academic Integrity — chapters on assessment design. Elbow, P. (1973) Writing Without Teachers (foundational process writing text).

## Handbook of Academic Integrity {#handbook-of-academic-integrity}

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Type | book |
| Year | 2016 |
| Author | Tracey Bretag (editor) |
| Url | https://link.springer.com/referencework/10.1007/978-981-287-098-8 |

> **Key Argument:** First global reference work on academic integrity; foundational handbook before AI era

### Định nghĩa & Khái niệm

**Định nghĩa:**

> Bretag (2016) defines academic integrity as 'a commitment to fundamental values' and as 'a positive and proactive concept' rather than merely the absence of misconduct. The handbook draws on Bretag's own 2011 framework of 5 core elements of 'exemplary' academic integrity policy: Access, Approach, Responsibility, Detail, and Support.

**Phân loại vi phạm:**

> The handbook addresses: (1) conceptual foundations of academic integrity; (2) plagiarism and its cultural dimensions; (3) contract cheating (Lancaster coined the term 2006, contributed chapters here); (4) fabrication and falsification in research; (5) institutional policy frameworks; (6) technology-mediated integrity issues (pre-AI: paraphrase tools, Turnitin). No chapters on generative AI (ChatGPT did not exist).

**Lịch sử khái niệm:**

> Published 2016 as the first comprehensive global reference on academic integrity in higher education. Bretag established the International Centre for Academic Integrity Australia and published foundational policy frameworks (2011). The handbook consolidates global scholarship from Australia, UK, USA, Canada, and Asia.

### Bằng chứng thực tế

**Tên trường hợp:**

> Not a case study volume; a reference handbook. Thomas Lancaster's chapter on contract cheating (essay mills) and Bretag's own policy framework chapters are most cited. Features institutional policy analyses from University of South Australia, Macquarie, and international comparators.

**Diễn biến:**

> Multi-author global reference covering: historical origins of plagiarism concept in Western academia, cultural differences in citation norms (Eastern vs. Western HE), honor code traditions, contract cheating markets (pre-AI), institutional policy design, detection tools circa 2016 (Turnitin as primary), and legal frameworks. First synthesis to treat academic integrity as a global field of study.

**Kết quả xử lý:**

> Became the canonical reference for academic integrity researchers and policy-makers for ~8 years until replaced by Eaton (2024). Cited in government policy documents in Australia, UK, and Canada. Bretag's 5-element policy framework became a standard benchmarking tool for institutional policy audit.

### Tác hại & Hậu quả

**Hại cho người học:**

> The handbook establishes empirically that academic dishonesty deprives students of genuine learning and creates credential inflation — foundational arguments later applied to AI fraud.

**Hại cho nghiên cứu:**

> Pre-AI focus on fabrication, falsification, and plagiarism in research outputs. Lancaster's contract cheating chapter directly applies to paper mills, which have since incorporated AI generation.

**Hại cho xã hội:**

> Bretag documents societal consequences: devalued qualifications, erosion of public trust in HE, international student recruitment practices that create academic integrity risks, and commercialization of HE incentives.

### Phát hiện & Công cụ

**Công cụ phát hiện:**

> Turnitin was the primary tool discussed (2016). The handbook provides detailed analysis of Turnitin's capabilities and limitations (similarity index, database coverage, paraphrase detection gap). Contract cheating detection methods (custom essay detection) also covered.

**Hạn chế công cụ:**

> 2016 limitations: Turnitin cannot detect fully paraphrased or contract-written essays. Lancaster's chapter anticipates that technology-driven detection will always lag behind evasion methods. In retrospect, this forecast was correct for AI detection.

**LLM Watermarking:**

> Not addressed (predates LLM watermarking research by 7 years). However, Bretag's emphasis on institutional policy and culture over technological detection anticipates why watermarking alone would be insufficient.

### Phòng ngừa & Giải pháp

**Thiết kế bài tập:**

> Chapters on assessment design advocate for authentic tasks, portfolio assessment, and oral defense — assessment approaches that reduce plagiarism opportunity. These recommendations directly apply to AI-resistant assessment design.

**Chính sách trường học:**

> Bretag's 5-element framework for exemplary AI policy: (1) Access — make policy clear and visible; (2) Approach — positive, values-based framing; (3) Responsibility — shared across institution; (4) Detail — specific and actionable; (5) Support — resources for compliance. This framework benchmarks institutional policies across Australia, UK, Canada, USA.

**Văn hóa tổ chức:**

> Consistent with McCabe: Bretag argues that punitive enforcement without cultural change produces compliance theater, not genuine integrity. The 5-element framework prioritizes institutional culture-building.

**AI Literacy:**

> Not explicitly addressed (2016 predates AI literacy as a concept). However, Bretag's 'Access' element (making policy clear and visible) implies the need for education about what is and is not permitted — which in 2024 includes AI literacy.

### Khung pháp lý & Đạo đức

**Khung quốc tế:**

> The handbook established the global academic integrity field. UNESCO, EU, and national HE bodies cite Bretag as foundational. ICAI 6-value framework (McCabe/Pavela) is analyzed and compared with Bretag's institutional approach.

**Pháp luật Việt Nam:**

> Vietnam's Decree 109/2022 Điều 20 (institutional integrity code requirement) reflects Bretag's 'Responsibility' element. The gap: Vietnam adopted the structural requirement but not the accompanying cultural and support infrastructure.

**Chính sách châu Á:**

> The handbook includes Asia-Pacific chapters on academic integrity in China, Singapore, Australia. CUHK, NUS, and NTU policy developments in the post-2022 AI era build on the 2016 framework.

### Góc nhìn EdTech

**AI tutor vs AI cheater:**

> Not applicable (2016 predates generative AI). However, Bretag's framing that academic integrity is a positive value (not just prohibition of misconduct) is foundational to the 'AI as tutor' / responsible AI use discourse.

**Chính sách EdTech companies:**

> Not addressed (EdTech AI policy landscape did not exist in 2016). The Turnitin analysis in the handbook provides historical context for current debates about AI detection tools.

**Đề xuất nhà nghiên cứu EdTech:**

> Bretag's 5-element framework remains actionable for EdTech researcher recommendations: apply it to AI policy design — Access (communicate AI rules clearly), Approach (values-based, not punitive), Responsibility (institutional + student shared), Detail (specific AI use scenarios defined), Support (AI literacy education provided).

### Nguồn tài liệu

**Bài báo khoa học:**

> Bretag, T. (2011) 'Defining and developing an institutional academic integrity policy' IJEI. Lancaster, T. & Clarke, R. (2006) 'Contract cheating: the involvement of teaching staff' IJEI. Bretag et al. (2019) 'Defining contract cheating' Studies in Higher Education.

**Sách tham khảo:**

> Bretag (2016) Handbook of Academic Integrity, Springer Singapore, doi:10.1007/978-981-287-098-8. Succeeded by Eaton (2024) Second Handbook. McCabe et al. (2012) Cheating in College.

**Nguồn Việt Nam:**

> Vietnam not specifically featured in 2016 handbook. Post-publication, Decree 109/2022 is the closest Vietnamese equivalent to Bretag's policy recommendations.

### Other Info

**Publisher:** Springer Singapore

## Plagiarism in Higher Education: Tackling Tough Topics in Academic Integrity {#plagiarism-in-higher-education-tackling-tough-topics-in-academic-integrity}

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Type | book |
| Year | 2021 |
| Author | Sarah Elaine Eaton |

> **Key Argument:** Issue-by-issue exploration; introduces 'postplagiarism' concept; AI changes nature of plagiarism itself

### Định nghĩa & Khái niệm

**Định nghĩa:**

> Eaton (2021) defines plagiarism expansively as 'the misrepresentation of someone else's intellectual work as one's own,' and introduces 'postplagiarism' as the emerging condition in which human-AI hybrid authorship renders traditional definitions of plagiarism conceptually inadequate. The book argues that AI-generated text disrupts the foundational assumption that plagiarism requires an identifiable human source.

**Phân loại vi phạm:**

> The book addresses multiple dimensions: (1) traditional plagiarism (copy-paste, mosaic, paraphrase without attribution); (2) contract cheating and essay mills; (3) self-plagiarism and text recycling; (4) cross-language plagiarism; (5) emerging AI-assisted writing — introduced as a new category requiring redefinition.

**Lịch sử khái niệm:**

> Published in 2021, just before ChatGPT's launch — remarkable prescience. Eaton had been developing the 'postplagiarism' concept since ~2019 in journal articles. The full theoretical framework was later expanded in 'Postplagiarism: Developing a New Framework' published in International Journal for Educational Integrity (2023).

### Bằng chứng thực tế

**Tên trường hợp:**

> Eaton's chapter on AI writing tools (2021) anticipates cases like GPT-generated essays being submitted as student work — written just 18 months before the ChatGPT moment.

**Diễn biến:**

> The book proceeds chapter by chapter through 'tough topics': ghost-writing, contract cheating, paraphrasing tools (Spinbot, Quillbot), translation plagiarism, and nascent AI writing tools. Eaton demonstrates how each topic challenges traditional detection and policy approaches. The postplagiarism thesis: if AI generates text, there is no human 'original' to plagiarize from — the ethical violation shifts from copying to misrepresentation of authorship.

**Kết quả xử lý:**

> Not a case study book; academic analysis. Highly influential in reshaping academic integrity discourse. Cited in subsequent policy documents including NUS AI policy discussion papers and UK QAA guidance. Won recognition from the Canadian Association for the Study of Adult Education.

**Con số thống kê:**

> Book length: 248 pages; 15 chapters; 978-1-4408-7437-6; Libraries Unlimited imprint (ABC-CLIO); published January 2021. Eaton is based at University of Calgary, Werklund School of Education.

### Tác hại & Hậu quả

**Hại cho người học:**

> If postplagiarism conditions are not addressed in institutional policy, students who use AI tools in ambiguous ways may face inconsistent or unjust enforcement. Conversely, students who over-rely on AI fail to develop original thinking, which is the educational purpose of written assignments.

**Hại cho nghiên cứu:**

> Eaton's postplagiarism framework highlights that peer review and publication processes built on originality detection cannot adequately handle human-AI hybrid authorship. Research quality standards need to evolve.

**Hại cho xã hội:**

> If traditional plagiarism frameworks are applied rigidly to AI-era writing, institutions either over-punish legitimate AI use or under-punish genuine fraud. Both outcomes harm the social function of academic credentials.

### Phát hiện & Công cụ

**Công cụ phát hiện:**

> Eaton (2021) discusses paraphrase detection limitations (Turnitin cannot detect paraphrased plagiarism well) and anticipates AI writing tools as a detection challenge. Post-2021, her work is explicitly cited in Turnitin and Copyleaks product development discussions.

**Hạn chế công cụ:**

> Central argument: all detection tools are designed for a pre-postplagiarism world. If the original plagiarized source does not exist in a database (i.e., the 'source' is an AI language model's training data), traditional fingerprinting fails entirely.

**LLM Watermarking:**

> Eaton (2021) does not address watermarking (predates the Kirchenbauer et al. and Aaronson work). The postplagiarism framework she develops would, however, support watermarking as an attribution mechanism — embedding AI provenance in the text itself.

### Phòng ngừa & Giải pháp

**Thiết kế bài tập:**

> Eaton advocates for assessment redesign that makes plagiarism less tempting and less possible: authentic tasks, process documentation, personal reflection, oral examination. These recommendations anticipate the post-ChatGPT assessment redesign movement.

**Chính sách trường học:**

> Eaton argues that existing honor codes and academic integrity policies must be fundamentally revised — not just updated — to address postplagiarism conditions. Policies should define AI use explicitly and move from prohibition to contextual disclosure.

**Văn hóa tổ chức:**

> Consistent with McCabe's findings: Eaton emphasizes culture over enforcement. She argues that when students understand the intellectual purpose of original writing (not just rule-compliance), integrity follows naturally.

**AI Literacy:**

> Eaton's book implicitly calls for AI literacy: students and faculty need conceptual understanding of what AI writing tools do and what academic integrity means in that context — not just tool detection.

### Khung pháp lý & Đạo đức

**Khung quốc tế:**

> Eaton's work informs UNESCO 2023 guidance on GenAI in education. The postplagiarism concept is cited in EU AI Act commentary on educational assessment. ICAI 2021 update to its core values framework draws on Eaton's institutional research.

**Pháp luật Việt Nam:**

> Vietnam's Decree 109/2022 uses traditional plagiarism definitions that Eaton's postplagiarism framework would classify as outdated. There is a direct policy gap: Vietnamese law does not address AI-assisted writing as a distinct category.

**Chính sách châu Á:**

> NUS policy development (2023/2024) explicitly references the need to redefine academic integrity in the AI era — aligned with Eaton's framework. NTU's zero-marks policy for hallucinated citations (2025) addresses a specific postplagiarism phenomenon.

### Góc nhìn EdTech

**AI tutor vs AI cheater:**

> Eaton's postplagiarism framework implicitly supports the 'AI as tutor' possibility: if AI use is disclosed and attributed, it is no longer plagiarism. The ethical line is misrepresentation, not AI use itself — consistent with Rose Luckin's 'intelligence augmentation' vision.

**Chính sách EdTech companies:**

> Coursera's post-submission dialogue approach and Khan Academy's Khanmigo design both implicitly embody postplagiarism thinking: the question is not whether AI was used but whether the student engaged meaningfully and can account for their work.

**Đề xuất nhà nghiên cứu EdTech:**

> Eaton's postplagiarism framework is the most actionable conceptual tool for EdTech researchers: (1) redefine academic integrity to focus on misrepresentation of authorship, not AI use per se; (2) develop attribution standards for AI assistance; (3) design assessments that require personal engagement that AI cannot substitute; (4) update Vietnamese policy to reflect postplagiarism conditions.

### Nguồn tài liệu

**Bài báo khoa học:**

> Eaton, S.E. (2021) 'Plagiarism in the Post-COVID-19 Pandemic' Interchange. Eaton, S.E. (2023) 'Postplagiarism' International Journal for Educational Integrity doi:10.1007/s40979-023-00144-1. Eaton, S.E. & Edino, R.I. (2018) 'Strengthening the research agenda of educational integrity in Canada' IJEI.

**Sách tham khảo:**

> Eaton (2021) Plagiarism in Higher Education, ISBN 978-1-4408-7437-6. Eaton (2024) Second Handbook of Academic Integrity. McCabe et al. (2012) Cheating in College. Bretag (2016) Handbook of Academic Integrity.

**Nguồn Việt Nam:**

> Decree 109/2022 (Điều 20) — Vietnam's closest equivalent to Eaton's policy recommendations, but predates AI era. Perkins (BUV, 2023/2024) explicitly applies postplagiarism thinking in Vietnamese HE context.

### Other Info

**Publisher:** ABC-CLIO (Libraries Unlimited)

## Second Handbook of Academic Integrity {#second-handbook-of-academic-integrity}

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Type | book |
| Year | 2024 |
| Author | Sarah Elaine Eaton (editor) |
| Url | https://link.springer.com/referencework/10.1007/978-3-031-54144-5 |

> **Key Argument:** Most comprehensive current reference; covers AI, GenAI, postplagiarism, contract cheating globally; successor to Bretag 2016

### Định nghĩa & Khái niệm

**Định nghĩa:**

> The Second Handbook of Academic Integrity (2024) is the most comprehensive current scholarly reference on academic integrity, defined broadly as 'a commitment to the values of honesty, trust, fairness, respect, responsibility, and courage in academic contexts' (following ICAI). It explicitly addresses the paradigm shift caused by generative AI in what constitutes legitimate and illegitimate academic work.

**Phân loại vi phạm:**

> The handbook covers all major violation categories: plagiarism (traditional and AI-assisted), contract cheating and essay mills, fabrication and falsification, examination fraud, AI-generated work misrepresentation, postplagiarism phenomena, and systemic/institutional failures. Organized in thematic sections with global case studies.

**Lịch sử khái niệm:**

> Successor to Bretag (2016) Handbook of Academic Integrity — the first global reference work. Eaton's 2024 edition adds an entirely new section on GenAI and postplagiarism. The jump from 1,200 pages (Bretag 2016) to 1,991 pages reflects both the field's growth and the seismic impact of ChatGPT (2022) on academic integrity practice.

### Bằng chứng thực tế

**Tên trường hợp:**

> Reference to multiple global cases: Hindawi/Wiley mass retractions, NeurIPS 2025 hallucinated citations, CUHK and NUS policy development, Vanderbilt/Turnitin controversy — all synthesized across chapters.

**Diễn biến:**

> Multi-author volume with contributions from leading researchers worldwide: Eaton (editor, Calgary), Bretag (Adelaide), Lancaster (Imperial), Newton (Swansea), Perkins (BUV), Liang (Stanford)  — and dozens more. Covers contract cheating in Asia, postplagiarism frameworks, AI detection tool limitations, watermarking, policy responses from 20+ countries, and EdTech platform responses.

**Kết quả xử lý:**

> Definitive reference for academic integrity scholars, policy-makers, and institutional administrators. Cited in UNESCO and EU AI Act educational guidance. Open access chapters available via SpringerLink (doi:10.1007/978-3-031-54144-5). Replaces Bretag (2016) as the field's canonical reference.

### Tác hại & Hậu quả

**Hại cho người học:**

> The handbook documents extensively how academic dishonesty — especially AI-assisted fraud — harms learners: failure to develop genuine competence, psychological dependence on external tools, credential devaluation, and risk of disciplinary action.

**Hại cho nghiên cứu:**

> Dedicated sections on research integrity, retraction culture, hallucinated citations, and paper mills. Synthesizes evidence that AI-generated content contamination threatens peer review infrastructure and reproducibility.

**Hại cho xã hội:**

> Cross-national chapters document societal consequences: credential devaluation, erosion of trust in academic institutions, impact on professional licensing (medicine, law, engineering), and growing inequality in enforcement (ESL students disproportionately penalized).

### Phát hiện & Công cụ

**Công cụ phát hiện:**

> Comprehensive coverage of AI detection tools: Turnitin, GPTZero, Copyleaks, Sapling, ZeroGPT — their mechanisms, accuracy rates, and documented failure modes. Liang et al. ESL bias findings are prominently featured.

**Hạn chế công cụ:**

> Multiple chapters address detection tool limitations: ESL bias (Liang et al.), paraphrase evasion, 'humanization' tools, arms race dynamics. Consensus: no detection tool is reliable enough for sole-source disciplinary action.

**LLM Watermarking:**

> Dedicated coverage of Kirchenbauer/UMD green/red token watermarking and Aaronson/OpenAI Gumbel approach. Analysis of deployment barriers: robustness to paraphrase, quality-utility tradeoffs, no production deployment as of 2024.

### Phòng ngừa & Giải pháp

**Thiết kế bài tập:**

> Chapters on assessment redesign from University of Sydney, Stanford, Chartered College of Teaching UK, and University of San Diego. Oral defense, process portfolios, reflective journals, staged submissions — with empirical effectiveness data.

**Chính sách trường học:**

> International policy survey: Stanford (flexible disclosure), Harvard (case-by-case), MIT (instructor discretion), NUS (5-tier disclosure framework), CUHK (prohibition), UK QAA guidance, EU and Australian national frameworks. Perkins' AIAS 5-tier framework featured prominently.

**Văn hóa tổ chức:**

> McCabe's honor code findings re-examined in GenAI context. New research shows that culture-first approaches remain more effective than detection-enforcement in reducing AI fraud, just as they were more effective for plagiarism.

**AI Literacy:**

> UNESCO Art. 4 EU AI Act (AI literacy obligation), ISTE/CoSN frameworks — and new institutional AI literacy program models from member universities. Chapter on distinguishing AI as learning tool vs. AI as fraud vector.

### Khung pháp lý & Đạo đức

**Khung quốc tế:**

> Full chapter on UNESCO 2021 and 2023 guidance, EU AI Act Annex III and Article 50, IEEE EAD, ACM Code — with analysis of how each framework applies to academic integrity in HE.

**Pháp luật Việt Nam:**

> Vietnam referenced in global policy survey chapters. Decree 109/2022 cited as example of pre-AI academic integrity legislation with significant gaps. Perkins (BUV) Vietnam-based research featured.

**Chính sách châu Á:**

> Dedicated Asia-Pacific chapter: CUHK expulsion policy (2023), NUS disclosure framework (2023/2024), NTU hallucinated citations case (2025), Singapore MOE national guidance. Comparative analysis with Western approaches.

### Góc nhìn EdTech

**AI tutor vs AI cheater:**

> Chapter-length treatment of the Luckin vs. Selwyn debate. Synthesizes EdTech company policies (Khan Academy Khanmigo, Coursera, Duolingo) and research evidence on when AI supports vs. undermines learning.

**Chính sách EdTech companies:**

> Khan Academy Khanmigo (Socratic AI that refuses to complete work), Coursera post-submission dialogue, Duolingo proctored exam restrictions, FPT/Van Lang Coursera pilots in Vietnam — all covered.

**Đề xuất nhà nghiên cứu EdTech:**

> The handbook's consensus EdTech researcher recommendations: (1) postplagiarism framework as conceptual foundation; (2) contextual integration over blanket prohibition; (3) AIAS 5-tier framework for institutional policy; (4) new attribution standards for AI assistance; (5) oral/process-based assessment as primary defense; (6) AI literacy as mandatory component of curriculum.

### Nguồn tài liệu

**Bài báo khoa học:**

> Published chapters cite: Perkins et al. (2023) IETI, Newton & Xiromeriti (2023), Liang et al. (2023) Patterns, Haider et al. (2024) Harvard Misinformation Review, Kirchenbauer et al. (2023) ICML.

**Sách tham khảo:**

> Eaton (2024) Second Handbook, Springer ISBN 978-3-031-54143-8. Bretag (2016) Handbook of Academic Integrity, Springer. McCabe et al. (2012) Cheating in College. Eaton (2021) Plagiarism in Higher Education.

**Nguồn Việt Nam:**

> Perkins (BUV, Vietnam) has chapter contributions. Vietnam Springer 2024 study (1,386 students, doi:10.1007/s10639-024-12495-4) cited. HCMUFA case referenced as first Vietnamese public AI disciplinary case.

## Academic Integrity in the Twenty-First Century {#academic-integrity-in-the-twenty-first-century}

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Type | book |
| Year | 2008 |
| Author | Tricia Bertram Gallant |

> **Key Argument:** Cheating as symptom of institutional failure in curriculum design and assessment; solutions must be pedagogical not punitive

### Định nghĩa & Khái niệm

**Định nghĩa:**

> Gallant (2008) defines academic integrity as an institutional responsibility — not merely an individual moral obligation. Academic dishonesty is a systemic symptom of poorly designed academic environments (excessive grade pressure, irrelevant assignments, punitive culture) rather than evidence of individual moral failure.

**Phân loại vi phạm:**

> The monograph addresses: (1) cheating behavior types (exam fraud, plagiarism, fabrication); (2) institutional failure modes (poorly designed assessments, unclear expectations, competitive grading); (3) systemic drivers (credential economy, grade inflation, student pressure); (4) faculty complicity (designed-to-cheat assignments, ignored violations); (5) cultural context (honor codes vs. deterrence models).

**Lịch sử khái niệm:**

> Published 2008 as Vol. 33 No. 5 of the ASHE Higher Education Report series (Jossey-Bass). Gallant's contribution shifted the field's focus from individual cheaters to institutional systems. She was then at University of California San Diego, where she was Academic Integrity Coordinator. This systems-thinking approach prefigures current EdTech debates about AI and assessment design.

### Bằng chứng thực tế

**Tên trường hợp:**

> Not a case study volume. Gallant synthesizes empirical literature from McCabe's survey work, Boyer Commission reports, and institutional policy analyses to argue that the higher education system itself creates conditions for cheating.

**Diễn biến:**

> The monograph proceeds: (1) Review of prevalence data (McCabe et al. surveys showing 2/3 cheating rates); (2) Analysis of institutional factors — grade pressure, irrelevant high-stakes exams, anonymous mass testing, lack of faculty engagement; (3) Documentation of faculty's reluctance to report violations (too time-consuming, doubt detection, fear of student appeals); (4) Assessment design failures — multiple-choice exams with answer keys easily shared; (5) Policy failures — punitive deterrence without addressing root causes.

**Kết quả xử lý:**

> Highly influential in reforming how institutions approach academic integrity: from punitive enforcement to pedagogical redesign. Gallant's 'academic integrity as a teaching and learning issue' framing is now mainstream in HE literature. UCSD's academic integrity program under her leadership became a model cited by NUS, University of Sydney, and others.

**Con số thống kê:**

> ASHE Higher Education Report series Vol. 33 No. 5; published 2008; Jossey-Bass imprint; approximately 100 pages (monograph format characteristic of ASHE series); Gallant later published 'Creating the Ethical Academy' (2011, Routledge) as follow-up.

### Tác hại & Hậu quả

**Hại cho người học:**

> Gallant's core argument: poorly designed assessments harm all students — those who cheat (no learning) and those who don't (unfair competition, credential devaluation). The immediate harm is bypassed learning; the long-term harm is professional incompetence.

**Hại cho nghiên cứu:**

> Gallant's institutional failure argument extends to research contexts: when research culture rewards publication over rigor, fabrication and falsification follow. This directly connects to the 2023-2025 AI-generated paper retraction crisis.

**Hại cho xã hội:**

> When cheating is institutionally tolerated or structurally incentivized, credentials lose their social signaling function. Employers, licensing bodies, and the public lose the ability to trust that degrees represent actual competence.

### Phát hiện & Công cụ

**Công cụ phát hiện:**

> Gallant (2008) is skeptical of detection-only approaches: her research shows faculty frequently do not report detected violations because the process is burdensome. Detection tools (Turnitin circa 2008) do not address the root cause — assessment design.

**Hạn chế công cụ:**

> Central argument: detection tools address symptoms, not causes. An institution that redesigns assessments to be authentic, personalized, and process-based eliminates most cheating opportunity before detection is needed.

**LLM Watermarking:**

> Not applicable (2008 predates LLM research). However, Gallant's institutional systems argument implies that watermarking — as another detection tool — would face the same faculty non-enforcement problem unless institutional culture changes.

### Phòng ngừa & Giải pháp

**Thiết kế bài tập:**

> Gallant's primary contribution to assessment redesign: authentic assessment (real-world tasks that cannot be meaningfully outsourced), incremental/process-based submission, oral examination, and replacing high-stakes single-point tests with portfolios. These recommendations are directly applied in post-ChatGPT assessment redesign guidance.

**Chính sách trường học:**

> Gallant argues against purely reactive policy (disciplinary committees, detection tools) and for proactive policy: mandatory academic integrity education for students, faculty development on assessment design, visible institutional commitment from leadership.

**Văn hóa tổ chức:**

> Gallant aligns with McCabe: punitive deterrence without cultural change is ineffective. Faculty must model integrity values and design assessments that make cheating both difficult and pointless.

**AI Literacy:**

> Not addressed (2008). However, Gallant's 'make irrelevant assignments irrelevant to cheat on' argument translates directly: if AI can trivially complete an assignment, the assignment was not testing a meaningful competency. AI literacy for assessment designers means asking: 'What can AI not do here?'

### Khung pháp lý & Đạo đức

**Khung quốc tế:**

> Gallant's work informs ICAI institutional frameworks and UNESCO guidance on assessment integrity. The ASHE series gives this work significant reach in US HE policy circles.

**Pháp luật Việt Nam:**

> Vietnam's Ministry of Education and Training faces precisely the institutional failures Gallant describes: grade-pressure culture, high-stakes standardized testing, faculty reluctance to report, assessment designs vulnerable to AI. Decree 109/2022 creates structural requirements but does not address pedagogical root causes.

**Chính sách châu Á:**

> Asian HE systems (Vietnam, China, South Korea) face intensified versions of Gallant's institutional failures: extreme grade competition (Gaokao, Vietnam THPT), credential economy pressures, high student-to-faculty ratios reducing individualized assessment. Gallant's systemic analysis is highly applicable.

### Góc nhìn EdTech

**AI tutor vs AI cheater:**

> Gallant's framework resolves the tutor/cheater debate: it depends on assessment design. If an assignment requires only information recall or generic essay writing (AI can do this), AI use is cheating by design. If assessment requires personal reflection, authentic problem-solving, or demonstrable process, AI cannot substitute for learning.

**Chính sách EdTech companies:**

> Not addressed (2008). However, Khan Academy's Khanmigo design philosophy (Socratic, refuses to complete work) directly implements Gallant's principle: design the environment to make cheating structurally impossible, not just punitively punished.

**Đề xuất nhà nghiên cứu EdTech:**

> Gallant's framework is the most powerful for EdTech researcher recommendations: (1) Diagnose institutional failures before prescribing AI detection tools; (2) Redesign assessments so AI cannot trivially complete them (authentic, personalized, process-based); (3) Faculty development on AI-resistant assessment design; (4) Reframe academic integrity from compliance to pedagogical quality; (5) Institutional commitment from leadership — not just policy documents.

### Nguồn tài liệu

**Bài báo khoa học:**

> Gallant, T.B. (2008) Academic Integrity in the Twenty-First Century, ASHE Higher Education Report Vol.33 No.5. Gallant, T.B. (2011) Creating the Ethical Academy (Routledge). Gallant, T.B. & Drinan, P. (2008) 'Institutionalizing academic integrity' Journal of Higher Education.

**Sách tham khảo:**

> Gallant (2008) ASHE report. McCabe et al. (2012) Cheating in College. Bretag (2016) Handbook of Academic Integrity. Eaton (2021) Plagiarism in Higher Education.

**Nguồn Việt Nam:**

> Vietnam HE system exhibits many of the institutional failures Gallant identifies: high grade pressure, anonymous mass testing (THPT), faculty reluctance to report violations. The Springer 2024 study (1,386 Vietnamese students) finding that actual AI fraud is ~3x self-reported rate is consistent with Gallant's under-reporting/under-enforcement thesis.

### Other Info

**Publisher:** Jossey-Bass / ASHE Higher Education Report (Vol.33 No.5)

### Uncertain Fields

- con_so_thong_ke_page_count

## Cheating in College {#cheating-in-college}

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Type | book |
| Year | 2012 |
| Author | Donald L. McCabe, Kenneth D. Butterfield, Linda K. Treviño |

> **Key Argument:** Surveyed 70,000 US college students; 2/3 self-report cheating; honor code culture most effective; business school culture most dishonest

### Định nghĩa & Khái niệm

**Định nghĩa:**

> Academic dishonesty (as defined in this work) encompasses cheating on exams, plagiarism, unauthorized collaboration, fabrication, and facilitation — operationalized through large-scale self-report surveys across US higher education institutions.

**Phân loại vi phạm:**

> McCabe, Butterfield & Treviño categorize violations as: (1) exam cheating (copying, smuggling notes, impersonation); (2) written assignment fraud (plagiarism, contract cheating, fabrication); (3) unauthorized collaboration; (4) facilitation (helping others cheat). Severity and norms differ by academic field.

**Lịch sử khái niệm:**

> McCabe began survey research on academic dishonesty in 1990 at Rutgers. This 2012 book synthesizes 20+ years of data (1990–2010). It predates the AI era but establishes the empirical baseline. McCabe also co-founded the International Center for Academic Integrity (ICAI) in 1992 with Gary Pavela.

### Bằng chứng thực tế

**Tên trường hợp:** Survey of 70,000+ US college students across multiple institutions — McCabe's longitudinal dataset (1990–2010)

**Diễn biến:**

> Largest empirical study of academic dishonesty in US higher education. Self-report surveys administered to students at 100+ colleges and universities. Students reported on their own cheating behavior across multiple violation types. Key finding: approximately 2/3 of students self-reported at least one instance of cheating during their academic career.

**Kết quả xử lý:**

> The book does not report disciplinary outcomes; it is descriptive/analytical. Key policy implication: institutions with strong honor code cultures (where students pledge and self-police) showed significantly lower rates of academic dishonesty. Business schools showed the highest rates of cheating.

**Con số thống kê:**

> ~70,000 students surveyed; ~2/3 (approximately 67%) self-reported cheating; honor code institutions: ~25% lower cheating rates than non-honor code institutions; business students: highest cheating rates across disciplines; medical/nursing: lower rates but high-stakes violations.

### Tác hại & Hậu quả

**Hại cho người học:**

> Students who cheat fail to develop critical thinking, writing, and analytical skills that constitute the actual value of higher education. Cheating creates credential inflation where degrees signal less than they claim to.

**Hại cho nghiên cứu:**

> McCabe's work demonstrates that academic dishonesty in research contexts produces unreliable scholarship. In a 2012 context, this primarily concerned plagiarism; in the post-ChatGPT era the same mechanisms apply to AI-assisted fraud.

**Hại cho xã hội:**

> When 2/3 of college students cheat, credential devaluation affects hiring decisions, public trust in institutions, and the integrity of professional gatekeeping across law, medicine, and business.

### Phát hiện & Công cụ

**Công cụ phát hiện:**

> McCabe's era (pre-AI): Turnitin for plagiarism detection, instructor vigilance, honor board processes. The book does not address AI detection as it predates ChatGPT by a decade.

**Hạn chế công cụ:**

> The 2012 book does not address AI detection limitations. However, McCabe's broader finding — that punitive enforcement alone is ineffective — applies directly to AI detection tool debates in the 2020s.

**LLM Watermarking:**

> Not applicable to this 2012 work. However, McCabe's research underpins why watermarking alone (a detection/enforcement tool) would be insufficient without accompanying cultural change.

### Phòng ngừa & Giải pháp

**Thiết kế bài tập:**

> McCabe's research does not specifically address assessment redesign, but implicitly supports process-based and formative assessment as ways to reduce cheating opportunity.

**Chính sách trường học:**

> Core policy finding: honor code culture (rather than legalistic enforcement) is the most effective institutional response. Stanford and Princeton honor codes are referenced as models. The difference between 'modified honor code' (pledge only) and 'full honor code' (unproctored exams, peer reporting) is significant.

**Văn hóa tổ chức:**

> Central thesis: cheating is a product of institutional culture, not individual moral failure. When academic integrity is modeled and valued by faculty, administration, and peers, cheating rates drop substantially. This contradicts purely punitive or detection-focused approaches.

**AI Literacy:**

> Not addressed (2012 predates AI era). However, McCabe's framework translates: AI literacy and ethical AI use should be embedded in institutional culture, not just policy enforcement.

### Khung pháp lý & Đạo đức

**Khung quốc tế:**

> McCabe's work is cited by ICAI (which he co-founded) and informs international academic integrity frameworks. The 6 Core Values of Academic Integrity (ICAI, 1999/2014/2021) emerged from McCabe and Pavela's institutional work.

**Pháp luật Việt Nam:**

> Vietnam's Decree 109/2022/NĐ-CP Điều 20 mandates institutional academic integrity codes — structurally aligned with McCabe's finding that institutional culture (not just law) determines outcomes.

**Chính sách châu Á:**

> McCabe's honor code research influenced policy discussions at NUS, NTU, and CUHK. Asian university contexts differ (collectivism, face culture, grade competition) but the core institutional culture finding transfers.

### Góc nhìn EdTech

**AI tutor vs AI cheater:**

> McCabe's framing positions cheating as institutional failure, not individual pathology — aligning with Neil Selwyn's sociological critique. The question is not whether AI 'causes' cheating but what institutional conditions make cheating attractive or normalized.

**Chính sách EdTech companies:**

> Not directly addressed. Khan Academy's Khanmigo design philosophy (Socratic, no essay completion) parallels McCabe's finding that environment shapes behavior — design can reduce cheating opportunity.

**Đề xuất nhà nghiên cứu EdTech:**

> From an EdTech researcher perspective: McCabe's foundational work remains highly relevant. Key application: AI policy should build honor-code culture (values-based), not just deploy detection tools (enforcement-based). Design learning environments where using AI appropriately is the norm, and misuse is socially as well as technically discouraged.

### Nguồn tài liệu

**Bài báo khoa học:**

> McCabe, D.L. & Treviño, L.K. (1993) 'Academic dishonesty: Honor codes and other contextual influences' Journal of Higher Education 64(5). McCabe, D.L., Treviño, L.K., & Butterfield, K.D. (2001) 'Cheating in academic institutions' Ethics & Behavior 11(3).

**Sách tham khảo:**

> McCabe, Butterfield & Treviño (2012) Cheating in College, Johns Hopkins UP, ISBN 978-1-4214-0716-6. Gallant (2008) Academic Integrity in the Twenty-First Century. Bretag (2016) Handbook of Academic Integrity.

**Nguồn Việt Nam:**

> Vietnam Springer 2024 study (1,386 students) builds on McCabe's self-report methodology but finds actual rates ~3x higher than self-report due to social desirability bias — extends McCabe's findings to Vietnamese HE context.

### Other Info

**Publisher:** Johns Hopkins University Press
