## AI phrase contamination in published papers (2023–2025) {#ai-phrase-contamination-in-published-papers-20232025}

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Type | real_case |
| Year | 2024 |
| Category | research_fraud |

### Định nghĩa & Khái niệm

**Định nghĩa:**

> AI phrase contamination refers to the presence of verbatim or near-verbatim LLM artifact phrases in published academic papers — phrases that betray the use of AI writing assistance without disclosure. Examples include 'As an AI language model, I...', 'As of my last knowledge update...', 'Certainly, here is a possible introduction for your topic', and 'Regenerate response'. These phrases indicate undisclosed AI use in manuscript preparation, violating disclosure norms and, in many cases, journal policies.

**Phân loại vi phạm:**

> Undisclosed AI-assisted writing (academic dishonesty via omission of disclosure). Range from minor non-disclosure violations to research fraud when AI-generated content is factually incorrect or fabricated. Multiple subcategories: đạo văn (if AI text is used without attribution), gian lận AI (undisclosed AI use), and in severe cases bịa đặt (if AI-generated factual claims are false).

**Lịch sử khái niệm:**

> AI phrase contamination emerged as a detectable phenomenon from November 2022 (ChatGPT public release). By early 2023, researchers and science journalists began compiling databases of papers containing telltale LLM phrases. Retraction Watch maintains a running list. The phenomenon peaked in 2024 as LLM use in academic writing became widespread and author disclosure practices remained inconsistent.

### Bằng chứng thực tế

**Tên trường hợp:**

> Multiple incidents: (1) 115+ Google Scholar articles containing 'As an AI language model' or 'As of my last knowledge update' (2023-2024 database); (2) Springer 'Advanced Nanovaccines for Cancer Immunotherapy' book ($119, March 2025) — AI phrase on page 25; (3) Zhang et al. retraction, Surfaces and Interfaces (Elsevier, 2024) — AI artifact phrase in introduction; (4) Bader et al. retraction, Radiology Case Reports (2024) — AI phrase detected.

**Diễn biến:**

> Beginning in 2023, researchers and science journalists began systematically searching Google Scholar and academic databases for papers containing characteristic LLM phrases. A database of 115+ papers containing phrases like 'As an AI language model, I can provide a general perspective' or 'As of my last knowledge update' was compiled. In one Elsevier journal (Surfaces and Interfaces), a paper titled 'The three-dimensional porous mesh structure of Cu-based metal-organic-framework — aramid cellulose separator enhances the electrochemical performance of lithium metal anode batteries' contained the phrase 'certainly, here is a possible introduction for your topic' in its introduction. The paper remained published for 38 days after discovery without correction. The Springer book 'Advanced Nanovaccines for Cancer Immunotherapy' by Nanasaheb Thorat (March 2025) was sold for $119-160 and contained on page 25: 'As an AI language model, I can provide a general perspective, but you should consult with medical professionals for personalized advice.' Springer withdrew the book and investigated. The author claimed to have received 'help' from colleagues and denied knowing how the AI phrase appeared. A broader study (Science/AAAS, 2024) of 1 million+ scientific papers found 22.5% of computer science abstracts showed evidence of LLM modification by September 2024.

**Kết quả xử lý:**

> Springer withdrew the cancer book and initiated investigation. Elsevier retracted the Surfaces and Interfaces paper. Multiple papers on the Retraction Watch 'ChatGPT writing evidence' list were retracted by their respective journals. No criminal prosecutions were reported. Individual journals issued retractions without consistent messaging about AI use policy. The Science/AAAS analysis noted that the frequency of AI-associated words like 'delve' subsequently declined — suggesting authors are now scrubbing detectable AI phrases, making detection harder.

**Con số thống kê:**

> 115+ Google Scholar papers identified containing verbatim LLM artifact phrases (2023-2024). 22.5% of CS paper abstracts showed LLM modification evidence by September 2024 (analysis of 1M+ papers). 1 book ($119 Springer title) withdrawn. Multiple individual journal retractions (exact count varies by database). The word 'delve' increased >3x in scientific abstracts post-ChatGPT launch before declining as it became recognized as an AI marker.

### Tác hại & Hậu quả

**Hại cho người học:**

> Students and researchers reading contaminated papers receive information partly or wholly generated by AI without disclosure, making it impossible to assess the reliability of that information. AI-generated content may contain factual errors or hallucinations presented as expert knowledge. The Springer cancer book is particularly concerning given the medical advice context.

**Hại cho nghiên cứu:**

> AI phrase contamination indicates systematic undisclosed AI use in manuscript preparation. Where AI content contains hallucinated facts or fabricated citations, the scientific record is corrupted. The scale — potentially 20%+ of CS papers by 2024 — represents a fundamental challenge to the integrity of the published record. Peer reviewers are not trained or incentivized to detect AI phrases, as demonstrated by publications that cleared review despite obvious artifacts.

**Hại cho xã hội:**

> Medical information (cancer book), engineering research (materials science), and policy-relevant science contaminated with unverified AI content poses direct public safety risks. The Springer cancer book incident, had it not been flagged, could have provided harmful medical advice to patients who purchased it.

### Phát hiện & Công cụ

**Công cụ phát hiện:**

> Primary detection method: keyword/phrase search in full-text databases for characteristic LLM artifact strings ('As an AI language model', 'Certainly here is', 'Regenerate response', 'As of my knowledge cutoff'). Retraction Watch maintains a curated list. Secondary: statistical analysis of word frequency shifts (Science/AAAS 2024 study). Tools like GPTZero can detect AI-generated text probabilistically but phrase searching is more reliable for obvious cases.

**Hạn chế công cụ:**

> Phrase-based detection only catches unsophisticated cases where authors failed to remove LLM artifact text. Sophisticated AI-assisted writing that has been edited and cleaned is not detectable by phrase search. As noted by the Science/AAAS study, authors are already adapting — reducing 'delve' usage — suggesting an arms race between AI writing and detection. LLM watermarking would provide more reliable attribution.

**LLM Watermarking:**

> If LLMs (OpenAI, Anthropic, Google) implemented mandatory watermarking (Kirchenbauer/UMD scheme, Aaronson/OpenAI scheme), AI phrase contamination would be traceable to specific models and generation events. The Springer book incident demonstrates the value of such watermarking — provenance would be verifiable rather than inferred from artifact phrases.

### Phòng ngừa & Giải pháp

**Thiết kế bài tập:**

> For academic assignments: requiring students to submit AI interaction logs/prompts alongside papers would make undisclosed AI use impossible. For journal submissions: mandatory AI-use declaration forms at submission, with penalties for false declarations. Automated phrase screening as part of submission processing.

**Chính sách trường học:**

> Major journals updated AI disclosure policies after 2023: Nature Portfolio, Elsevier, Springer Nature, Wiley all now require authors to declare AI use in manuscript preparation. However, enforcement relies on author honesty — no automated verification is universal. Some journals (e.g., Science) prohibit AI-generated text in submissions entirely.

**Văn hóa tổ chức:**

> The scale of contamination (22.5% of CS papers by 2024) suggests the problem is systemic, not individual. A culture of disclosure must be built alongside detection. McCabe's honor code research: punitive-only approaches after the fact are insufficient when the behavior is this widespread — structural norms change is required.

**AI Literacy:**

> Authors who accidentally included AI artifact phrases in published papers demonstrate low AI literacy — specifically, not understanding that LLMs insert self-referential phrases or that these phrases are traceable. AI literacy in academic publishing must include: understanding LLM artifact patterns, disclosure obligations, and verification practices.

### Khung pháp lý & Đạo đức

**Khung quốc tế:**

> EU AI Act (2024) Article 50 requires disclosure of AI-generated content. IEEE Code of Ethics and ACM Code of Ethics prohibit misrepresentation in scientific publications. ICMJE (International Committee of Medical Journal Editors) updated authorship guidelines to exclude AI as an author but require disclosure of AI use — directly relevant to medical publishing cases (cancer book, Radiology Case Reports).

**Pháp luật Việt Nam:**

> Nghị định 109/2022/NĐ-CP Điều 20 requires integrity in scientific publication but does not specifically address AI disclosure. The absence of an explicit AI disclosure requirement in Vietnamese research law represents a regulatory gap. Luật KH&CN 2025 (if enacted) may address this.

**Chính sách châu Á:**

> CUHK (Hong Kong) and NUS (Singapore) have developed explicit AI disclosure frameworks. NTU imposed zero marks for work with AI-generated unreliable content. The Springer cancer book and Surfaces and Interfaces cases have been cited in Asian university policy updates.

### Góc nhìn EdTech

**AI tutor vs AI cheater:**

> The spectrum of AI phrase contamination cases illustrates the AI-as-cheater risk: from careless non-disclosure to deliberate fraud. The distinction between AI as writing tool (legitimate) and AI as replacement for scientific thought (fraud) is central. Neil Selwyn's concern is validated — AI enables academic fraud at previously impossible scale.

**Chính sách EdTech companies:**

> Elsevier (ScienceDirect), Springer Nature, and Wiley all added AI-content detection to their editorial systems post-2023. iThenticate/Turnitin updated to flag AI-characteristic phrases. Some EdTech platforms (Coursera, edX) require AI disclosure in submitted assignments.

**Đề xuất nhà nghiên cứu EdTech:**

> From an EdTech/research integrity perspective: (1) Automated AI phrase screening must be standard in all journal submission systems; (2) AI disclosure forms should be mandatory and signed (not just optional checkboxes); (3) LLM providers should implement output logging/watermarking to enable provenance verification; (4) The arms race dynamic (authors scrubbing AI phrases) requires systemic solutions — behavioral norms, not just detection; (5) Attribution standards must evolve: AI as a tool requires citation (e.g., 'Sections X-Y were drafted using [LLM], verified and revised by authors').

### Nguồn tài liệu

**Bài báo khoa học:**

> Science/AAAS (2024): 'One-fifth of computer science papers may include AI content' (science.org). Retraction Watch: 'Papers and peer reviews with evidence of ChatGPT writing' (running database). Pivot-to-AI (2025): '$119 Springer cancer treatments book: As an AI language model' (pivot-to-ai.com). Haider et al. (2024): GPT-fabricated papers HKS Misinformation Review. Scientific American: 'AI Chatbots Have Thoroughly Infiltrated Scientific Publishing.'

**Sách tham khảo:**

> Eaton, S.E. (2024). Second Handbook of Academic Integrity. Springer. McCabe, D.L. et al. (2012). Cheating in College. Johns Hopkins UP. Bretag, T. (2016). Handbook of Academic Integrity. Springer.

**Nguồn Việt Nam:**

> No direct Vietnamese sources on AI phrase contamination. Relevant to Vietnamese researchers submitting to international journals. Nghị định 109/2022 applies. Vietnamese language papers on similar topics may exist in VNU Journal of Science, but none confirmed for this specific phenomenon.

### Uncertain Fields

- con_so_thong_ke (115 figure is a lower bound — actual number of contaminated papers is likely much higher given 22.5% CS paper estimate)
- bader_radiology_case_reports (specific retraction details for Bader et al. not fully confirmed from available sources — [uncertain])

## Aryan Anand — Lehigh University (2024) {#aryan-anand-lehigh-university-2024}

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Type | real_case |
| Institution | Lehigh University |
| Year | 2024 |
| Category | student_disciplinary |
| Institution | Lehigh University |

### Định nghĩa & Khái niệm

**Định nghĩa:**

> Multi-layered academic and admissions fraud combining AI-generated essay writing (ChatGPT), document fabrication (forged transcripts, fake death certificate, falsified income documents), and identity impersonation. Extends beyond standard AI dishonesty into criminal fraud territory.

**Phân loại vi phạm:**

> Contract cheating / AI-assisted dishonesty (ChatGPT used to write scholarship essay); Fabrication (forged Class 12 transcripts, falsified income documents); Fraud / identity impersonation (created fake school domain and email addresses impersonating school principal; fabricated father's death certificate to claim financial hardship scholarship); Visa fraud (misrepresented documents to ICE and State Department).

**Lịch sử khái niệm:**

> The Anand case (2024) represents an evolved form of admissions fraud enabled by generative AI: where earlier fraudsters manually fabricated documents, AI tools reduced the barrier to producing convincing essays and supporting materials. The case predates but parallels the 2024-2025 surge in AI-assisted admissions fraud globally.

### Bằng chứng thực tế

**Tên trường hợp:**

> Aryan Anand — Lehigh University, Bethlehem, Pennsylvania, USA. Undergraduate student on full scholarship (~$85,000 value). Arrested April 30, 2024; pleaded guilty June 12, 2024 to forgery charge; sentenced 1–3 months (time served); expelled by Lehigh University; deported to India.

**Diễn biến:**

> Aryan Anand, an Indian student, obtained a full scholarship to Lehigh University through an elaborate fraud scheme. He: (1) forged his Class 12 transcripts; (2) created a fake school domain and email addresses impersonating his school principal to provide false verification; (3) used ChatGPT to write his admissions essay; (4) fabricated a death certificate for his living father to claim financial hardship; (5) falsified income/tax documents to qualify for need-based scholarship totaling approximately $85,000. The fraud was exposed when Anand anonymously posted on Reddit under the username u/TransportationOk4728. A Reddit moderator traced his identity and alerted Lehigh University. This triggered an internal investigation, leading to his arrest on April 30, 2024.

**Kết quả xử lý:**

> Pleaded guilty to forgery charge (June 12, 2024); sentenced to 1–3 months in Northampton County Prison (equivalent to time served); Lehigh University expelled him and chose not to seek restitution (~$85,000) on condition of return to India; deported to India. The case also raised broader concerns about AI in admissions processes and triggered review of admissions verification practices.

**Con số thống kê:**

> 1 student; ~$85,000 in scholarship funds fraudulently obtained; 1 criminal conviction (forgery); 1 deportation. Self-incrimination via Reddit post was the discovery mechanism.

### Tác hại & Hậu quả

**Hại cho người học:**

> The case demonstrates how AI tools lower the effort threshold for fraud, enabling students who lack authentic qualifications to bypass merit-based selection — directly harming qualified students who are displaced by fraudulent applicants. The fraudulent student himself is deprived of genuine education by substituting fabricated credentials for real learning.

**Hại cho nghiên cứu:**

> Admissions fraud using AI-generated essays undermines the validity of scholarship selection processes. When AI writes the essay, the admissions committee is evaluating AI output rather than student capability, corrupting the human judgment at the core of selection.

**Hại cho xã hội:**

> Erodes trust in international student scholarship programs; increases administrative burden for verification; risks policy backlash that disadvantages legitimate international students; contributes to anti-immigration narratives. Self-incrimination via social media (Reddit) highlights the psychological toll of maintaining fraud.

### Phát hiện & Công cụ

**Công cụ phát hiện:**

> Primary detection: self-exposure via Reddit post (inadvertent self-incrimination). Secondary: Reddit moderator identity tracing; university internal investigation. No automated AI detection tool was the primary discovery mechanism — highlights that AI-generated essays in admissions contexts are rarely caught by technical tools alone.

**Hạn chế công cụ:**

> AI essay detectors (GPTZero, Turnitin) are not routinely applied at admissions stage. Even if applied, a student who edits AI-generated content can evade detection. The Anand case was discovered through human social engineering (Reddit), not technical detection — illustrating the fundamental limitation of automated tools for admissions fraud.

**LLM Watermarking:**

> Prospective LLM watermarking (Kirchenbauer/UMD, Aaronson/OpenAI) could help identify AI-generated admissions essays if implemented by OpenAI. As of 2024, no commercial watermarking was deployed, leaving this gap open.

### Phòng ngừa & Giải pháp

**Thiết kế bài tập:**

> Admissions reform implications: interview-based assessment, in-person writing samples, and multi-stage verification (direct contact with issuing institutions) can reduce AI-assisted admissions fraud. Process-based evidence collection (requiring portfolio of prior writing) can reveal authenticity gaps.

**Chính sách trường học:**

> Lehigh University's existing academic integrity policy covers misrepresentation in admissions materials. Post-case, universities broadly began reviewing AI-use policies in admissions contexts. No public record of Lehigh issuing new specific AI-in-admissions policy following this case.

**Văn hóa tổ chức:**

> The case occurred outside classroom academic culture and within the admissions system — a domain where honor-code culture has less applicability. Enforcement response (arrest, prosecution) was proportional to criminal fraud level. McCabe's honor-code framework does not fully apply to admissions fraud.

**AI Literacy:**

> The case highlights the need to educate prospective students on the legal and ethical boundaries of AI use in applications. AI literacy must include understanding that using AI to write admissions essays without disclosure constitutes fraud, not just policy violation.

### Khung pháp lý & Đạo đức

**Khung quốc tế:**

> ICAI (International Center for Academic Integrity) definition of academic fraud covers fabrication and misrepresentation. EU AI Act Art. 50 requires disclosure of AI-generated content in certain contexts. Anand's conduct also violated US immigration law (visa fraud) — illustrating how AI-assisted academic fraud can have criminal law dimensions.

**Pháp luật Việt Nam:**

> Nghị định 109/2022/NĐ-CP Điều 20 on fabrication in research; Luật Giáo dục 2019 on honest admission processes. Vietnamese law does not yet specifically address AI-generated admissions materials — a regulatory gap. Criminal fraud provisions (Bộ luật Hình sự) would apply to equivalent document forgery.

**Chính sách châu Á:**

> The Anand case (India-origin student) prompted Indian EdTech discussion on AI ethics in US college applications. Singapore's NUS and NTU have disclosure-based AI policies that would classify ChatGPT essay writing without disclosure as misconduct. CUHK's 2023 expulsion-level AI policy focuses on coursework, not admissions.

### Góc nhìn EdTech

**AI tutor vs AI cheater:**

> This case is unambiguously AI-as-cheater: ChatGPT was used to construct false identity and false merit, not to assist learning. No legitimate educational purpose. Neil Selwyn's (Monash) concerns about AI enabling fraud are directly validated by this case.

**Chính sách EdTech companies:**

> OpenAI's terms of service prohibit using ChatGPT for fraudulent purposes. The case illustrates that platform-level policy cannot prevent misuse without institutional and legal enforcement. Common Application and Coalition Application platforms began considering AI detection in essays post-2023.

**Đề xuất nhà nghiên cứu EdTech:**

> EdTech research recommendations: (1) admissions platforms should pilot AI-content detection at essay submission; (2) multi-stage document verification with direct institutional contact; (3) in-person or video interviews as standard for scholarship candidates; (4) AI literacy in secondary school curricula covering legal risks of AI fraud in applications; (5) international cooperation on academic credential verification.

### Nguồn tài liệu

**Bài báo khoa học:**

> Newton, P.M., & Xiromeriti, M. (2023). ChatGPT Performance on Objective Structured Clinical Examinations. Medical Education. Haider, A.S., et al. (2024). AI in Academic Fraud: Current State and Implications. Journal of Academic Ethics.

**Sách tham khảo:** Eaton, S.E. (2024). Academic Integrity in the Age of AI. Springer. Bretag, T. (Ed.) (2016). Handbook of Academic Integrity. Springer.

### Uncertain Fields

- nguon_viet_nam (Springer Vietnamese student survey citation unverified)
- chinh_sach_truong_hoc (no confirmed post-case Lehigh policy update found)

## CUHK — Chính sách đuổi học vì AI (2023) {#cuhk-chính-sách-đuổi-học-vì-ai-2023}

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Type | real_case |
| Institution | Chinese University of Hong Kong (CUHK) |
| Year | 2023 |
| Category | asia_policy |
| Institution | Chinese University of Hong Kong (CUHK) |

### Định nghĩa & Khái niệm

**Định nghĩa:**

> Institutional policy framework at a leading Asian university declaring that unauthorized use of ChatGPT or AI tools constitutes academic misconduct subject to the full range of institutional penalties including expulsion. CUHK defines AI misconduct as use of AI tools 'improperly or without authorisation' — a contextual standard requiring lecturer permission rather than blanket prohibition.

**Phân loại vi phạm:**

> Institutional policy-level response to AI-assisted academic dishonesty. Primary violation type: unauthorized AI-assisted submission (using ChatGPT/AI without tutor permission). Policy covers all AI generative tools. Secondary concern: post-graduation revocation of academic certifications for misconduct discovered after degree award — a novel punitive extension.

**Lịch sử khái niệm:**

> CUHK's policy was issued in early 2023, making it one of the earliest Asian university responses to ChatGPT-era academic integrity concerns. The policy appeared in March 2023 (media confirmation March 2, 2023) via a student guide/FAQ format, responding to the post-ChatGPT (November 2022) wave of AI tool adoption. CUHK was cited in South China Morning Post and Young Post Club as a leading Asian example of decisive AI policy.

### Bằng chứng thực tế

**Tên trường hợp:**

> Chinese University of Hong Kong (CUHK), Sha Tin, Hong Kong SAR. Policy announced March 2023 via student guidelines. One of the first explicit AI expulsion policies in Asian higher education. Policy covers: all unauthorized AI use in coursework; requires tutor permission for any AI tool use; penalties include expulsion and post-graduation certificate revocation.

**Diễn biến:**

> In early 2023, following widespread ChatGPT adoption among students globally, CUHK issued new academic integrity guidelines addressing AI tool use. The guidelines established that: (1) students must obtain tutor/faculty permission before using ChatGPT or any AI tools for assignments; (2) unauthorized AI use constitutes academic misconduct; (3) penalties range from assessment review and permanent demerits, through failing grade, suspension, lower degree classification, to expulsion; (4) graduates found to have used AI inappropriately risk post-graduation revocation of academic certifications. The policy was framed as contextual (permission-based) rather than absolute prohibition — a nuanced stance that recognized potential legitimate educational uses while maintaining strict oversight.

**Kết quả xử lý:**

> Policy implementation (no single case publicly disclosed). Reported outcomes include: permanent demerits on academic record, failing grades for affected courses, suspension, lower degree classification, expulsion, and post-graduation certification revocation. CUHK also announced plans to develop AI detection capabilities and improve plagiarism detection software. By August 2023, University of Hong Kong (HKU, separate institution) dropped its ChatGPT ban — illustrating the policy evolution within Hong Kong higher education.

**Con số thống kê:**

> No specific case count publicly disclosed. Policy scope: all CUHK students (~20,000 enrolled). Timeline: ChatGPT launch November 2022; CUHK policy March 2023 (~3 months lag — one of fastest major Asian university responses). HKU interim ban: February 2023; HKU ban lifted: August 2023. CUHK maintained its permission-based policy without reverting to full ban.

### Tác hại & Hậu quả

**Hại cho người học:**

> A restrictive AI policy without adequate AI literacy support may drive underground use rather than eliminate it. Students unfamiliar with policy boundaries face disproportionate risk of unknowing violations. The post-graduation revocation provision creates permanent vulnerability for graduates — a disproportionate penalty for AI tool use that was permitted in some contexts.

**Hại cho nghiên cứu:**

> AI restrictions without clear guidance on permitted research uses can impede legitimate AI-assisted research methods (literature review, data analysis) that are standard in international research practice. CUHK's contextual permission model attempts to balance this, but ambiguity remains for graduate research students.

**Hại cho xã hội:**

> CUHK's policy established a high-profile benchmark for Asian university AI governance. The expulsion-level threat deterred some legitimate AI use while likely not eliminating strategic cheating by determined students. Post-graduation certificate revocation is unprecedented in scope and creates legal uncertainty about the retroactive application of new academic standards.

### Phát hiện & Công cụ

**Công cụ phát hiện:**

> CUHK announced plans to devise AI detection measures and improve plagiarism detection software — no specific tool named. As of 2023, Turnitin had launched its AI detection module (April 2023); iThenticate and other tools were available. Practical detection at CUHK likely relies on the same Turnitin/GPTZero ecosystem as Western universities.

**Hạn chế công cụ:**

> Detection limitations apply equally at CUHK as globally: Turnitin 4% sentence-level FP rate; Liang et al. (Stanford, 2023) found 61.3% FP for non-native English TOEFL essays. CUHK's large proportion of native Cantonese/Mandarin speakers writing in English creates elevated false positive risk similar to other ESL-dominant contexts. AI 'humanization' tools (paraphrasers, editing tools) can evade detection.

**LLM Watermarking:**

> CUHK's policy predates deployed watermarking solutions. Kirchenbauer et al. (UMD) and Aaronson/OpenAI watermarking research (2022-2023) represents the technical trajectory. If Chinese-market AI tools (Baidu ERNIE, Alibaba Tongyi Qianwen, ByteDance Doubao) implement watermarking, CUHK detection capacity for locally-used AI could improve significantly.

### Phòng ngừa & Giải pháp

**Thiết kế bài tập:**

> CUHK's permission-based model implicitly requires faculty to redesign assessments to specify AI use conditions. Best practice (Stanford, University of Sydney) involves explicit assignment-level AI policies and AI-resistant design (oral components, staged submissions, reflective elements).

**Chính sách trường học:**

> CUHK: permission-based AI policy with expulsion as maximum penalty. Spectrum within Hong Kong: HKU interim ban (Feb 2023) → ban lifted (Aug 2023) → contextual integration. CUHK maintained stricter stance. Contrast with Singapore: NUS disclosure framework (2023/2024); NTU zero-mark for hallucinated citations (2025). CUHK represents the Asian university hardline that preceded the later pivot toward contextual integration.

**Văn hóa tổ chức:**

> CUHK's top-down policy announcement reflects an enforcement-first culture. No publicly documented integrity education campaign accompanied the policy. McCabe's research suggests that enforcement-only approaches (without honor code culture and education) produce compliance through fear rather than internalized integrity values.

**AI Literacy:**

> CUHK's 2023 policy noted that students using permitted AI tools must do so 'responsibly and ethically' and be 'mindful of their limitations' — acknowledging the need for AI literacy, but without specifying curriculum. UNESCO Art. 4 EU AI Act AI literacy mandate provides a framework CUHK could implement to substantiate this requirement.

### Khung pháp lý & Đạo đức

**Khung quốc tế:**

> UNESCO 2023 guidance on generative AI in education recommends contextual, educator-mediated AI policies — consistent with CUHK's permission model. EU AI Act (2024) Annex III high-risk AI systems include educational assessment tools. IEEE EAD and ACM Code of Ethics require honest attribution. CUHK's policy aligns with ICAI's academic integrity framework (honesty, trust, fairness).

**Pháp luật Việt Nam:**

> Vietnam has no direct equivalent of CUHK's explicit AI expulsion policy. Nghị định 109/2022/NĐ-CP addresses research integrity generally. Luật Giáo dục 2019 covers academic honesty requirements. The CUHK model provides a reference point for Vietnamese higher education regulators developing AI-specific academic integrity frameworks.

**Chính sách châu Á:**

> CUHK (2023): permission-based, expulsion maximum, post-graduation revocation. NUS Singapore (2023/2024): disclosure framework, graduated permissions. NTU Singapore (2025): zero marks for AI-hallucinated citations. Japanese universities (2023): mostly guidance-based, no enforcement mechanism. Korean universities (2023): variable, POSTECH issued explicit ban. CUHK was the first major Asian university to formalize an expulsion-level AI policy.

### Góc nhìn EdTech

**AI tutor vs AI cheater:**

> CUHK's policy explicitly acknowledges the legitimate use of AI tools ('permission can be granted in some situations'; students must use tools 'responsibly') — reflecting awareness of the AI-as-tutor potential. The default prohibition with permission-based exceptions reflects institutional risk aversion while leaving space for Rose Luckin's (UCL) AI democratization model.

**Chính sách EdTech companies:**

> CUHK's policy affects which AI tools students may use in coursework. The permission-based model means faculty can explicitly permit Khan Academy-style AI tutoring tools while prohibiting ChatGPT essay generation. Duolingo's AI integration in language learning could be permitted under CUHK's model if instructor-approved. The policy creates a framework compatible with contextual EdTech integration.

**Đề xuất nhà nghiên cứu EdTech:**

> From EdTech research perspective: (1) CUHK's permission-based model is more pedagogically sound than outright bans, but requires substantial faculty development to implement consistently; (2) post-graduation certificate revocation is disproportionate and legally untested — universities should limit retrospective penalties; (3) AI literacy curriculum should be embedded in undergraduate orientation to substantiate the 'responsible and ethical use' requirement; (4) CUHK should publish annual AI misconduct statistics to enable comparative research; (5) the AIAS 5-tier framework (Perkins) provides a more granular permission structure than binary permit/prohibit.

### Nguồn tài liệu

**Bài báo khoa học:**

> Ng, D.T.K., et al. (2023). Examining the use of ChatGPT in public universities in Hong Kong: a case study of restricted access areas. Discover Education. Springer. DOI: 10.1007/s44217-023-00081-8. Perkins, M., et al. (2023). Academic Integrity Considerations of AI Large Language Models in the Post-ChatGPT Era. Journal of University Teaching & Learning Practice, 20(2).

**Sách tham khảo:** Eaton, S.E. (2021). Plagiarism in Higher Education. ABC-CLIO. Bretag, T. (Ed.) (2016). Handbook of Academic Integrity. Springer.

**Nguồn Việt Nam:**

> South China Morning Post (SCMP) coverage of CUHK policy (March 2023) — primary English-language source. Vietnamese media coverage of Hong Kong AI policies in education (VietnamNet, 2023). Relevant Vietnamese framework: Nghị định 109/2022/NĐ-CP; QĐ 1705/QĐ-TTg on education to 2030.

### Uncertain Fields

- con_so_thong_ke (no specific case count for disciplinary actions under the CUHK policy publicly available)
- ket_qua_xu_ly (no specific documented expulsion case under this policy publicly confirmed as of April 2026)

## Benjamin Craver — Emory University (2024) {#benjamin-craver-emory-university-2024}

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Type | real_case |
| Institution | Emory University |
| Year | 2024 |
| Category | student_disciplinary |
| Institution | Emory University |

### Định nghĩa & Khái niệm

**Định nghĩa:**

> An unusual case at the boundary of innovation and academic integrity: a student who won a university-sanctioned $10,000 prize for building an AI study tool was subsequently suspended under the university's honor code for facilitating potential AI-assisted dishonesty by other students — despite no confirmed instances of misuse.

**Phân loại vi phạm:**

> Facilitating academic dishonesty (not direct AI cheating): Emory's honor code prohibits 'intentionally' helping other students cheat, plagiarize, or violate the honor code. The allegation was that Eightball's AI features could be used to complete assignments dishonestly. No evidence of direct AI-assisted exam cheating by Craver himself.

**Lịch sử khái niệm:**

> The Craver case (2024) marks a new category of AI academic integrity dispute: sanction of the tool-builder rather than the tool-user. It highlights the policy ambiguity when universities both incentivize AI EdTech innovation (startup competitions) and enforce anti-AI honor codes — a contradiction that emerged with the EdTech boom post-ChatGPT (2023-2024).

### Bằng chứng thực tế

**Tên trường hợp:**

> Benjamin Craver and unnamed co-founder — Emory University, Atlanta, Georgia. Undergraduates who created AI study tool 'Eightball.' Won $10,000 in Emory's startup pitch competition. Honor Council recommended: Craver — suspension (summer + fall 2024 semesters); co-founder — initially expulsion, reduced to one-year suspension. Craver filed lawsuit against Emory.

**Diễn biến:**

> Benjamin Craver and a co-founder, both Emory University undergraduates, created an AI study tool called 'Eightball' designed to convert coursework materials (lecture notes, syllabi) into study aids such as flashcards and practice tests. The tool won Emory's own startup pitch competition, earning $10,000 in prize money from the university. Subsequently, Emory's Honor Council reviewed the product and determined it 'may be used by students to complete assignments in violation of the Undergraduate Academic Honor Code.' The council invoked the provision prohibiting students from 'intentionally' helping others cheat. Despite Emory's own concession that there was no evidence anyone had ever used Eightball to cheat, the Honor Council recommended suspension for Craver and expulsion for his co-founder (later reduced to one-year suspension). Craver filed a lawsuit against Emory challenging the disciplinary action.

**Kết quả xử lý:**

> Craver: suspended for summer and fall 2024 semesters. Co-founder: initially expelled, penalty reduced to one-year suspension on appeal. Craver sued Emory seeking to reverse the suspension and seeking damages — the lawsuit alleged Emory acted inconsistently by first rewarding and then punishing the same product. Case raised significant media attention about institutional inconsistency on AI policy.

**Con số thống kê:**

> 2 students disciplined; $10,000 prize money awarded by Emory to the same product it later penalized students for creating; 0 confirmed instances of Eightball being used by any student to cheat (per Emory's own admission); 1 lawsuit filed by Craver.

### Tác hại & Hậu quả

**Hại cho người học:**

> The case creates a chilling effect on student AI innovation: legitimate EdTech entrepreneurs face punishment for building tools that could theoretically enable misuse, even without evidence of actual misuse. This discourages students from developing AI literacy and entrepreneurial skills that are valuable to society. It also illustrates how overly broad honor code enforcement can harm rather than protect educational integrity.

**Hại cho nghiên cứu:**

> The case does not directly harm research integrity, but the institutional contradiction — rewarding and punishing the same AI tool — undermines clear policy frameworks needed for responsible AI development in academic contexts.

**Hại cho xã hội:**

> Broad societal implications: if universities punish students for building AI learning tools, they disincentivize the EdTech innovation sector. This case was widely covered in technology media (404 Media, Yahoo Finance, Entrepreneur) and became a reference point in the debate about how institutions should balance AI restriction and AI adoption.

### Phát hiện & Công cụ

**Công cụ phát hiện:**

> No AI detection tool was used. Detection was administrative: the Honor Council reviewed the Eightball product's features and capabilities and applied a prospective risk assessment — punishing potential facilitation rather than actual detected misuse.

**Hạn chế công cụ:**

> This case illustrates the limits of a purely enforcement-based approach: when there is no actual detected cheating event, the institution's only evidence is capability-based (the tool could be misused). This mirrors the broader challenge of regulating AI tools: Turnitin, GPTZero etc. detect after-the-fact misuse; there is no mechanism to prospectively evaluate whether a study tool will be used for cheating.

**LLM Watermarking:**

> Watermarking is not directly relevant to this case, as the alleged violation is tool-building, not text generation. However, if Eightball's AI outputs were watermarkable, it could potentially help distinguish study aid use from assignment submission fraud.

### Phòng ngừa & Giải pháp

**Thiết kế bài tập:**

> The case argues for assignment design that renders AI study tools neutral or beneficial rather than cheating-enabling. If assignments require authentic personal reflection, oral defense, or process portfolios, AI-generated flashcards and practice tests (like Eightball's core function) cannot be misused for final submissions.

**Chính sách trường học:**

> Emory's honor code prohibits intentionally facilitating others' violations. The case exposed a contradiction between Emory's startup competition (which explicitly endorsed AI EdTech building) and its honor code enforcement. Post-case, Emory's policy remained unchanged publicly — no disclosure framework distinguishing 'study aid AI' from 'submission AI' was announced.

**Văn hóa tổ chức:**

> The case exemplifies institutional inconsistency — the antithesis of a trust-based honor code culture (McCabe framework). When a university rewards and punishes the same behavior, it erodes student trust in institutional fairness and reduces voluntary compliance with integrity norms.

**AI Literacy:**

> The Craver case reveals the need for clear institutional guidance distinguishing: AI tools that support learning (flashcards, practice tests, note organization) vs. AI tools that substitute for assessed work (essay writing, exam answer generation). AI literacy programs should address this distinction for both students and faculty.

### Khung pháp lý & Đạo đức

**Khung quốc tế:**

> IEEE EAD and ACM Code of Ethics address the responsibilities of technology creators to prevent foreseeable misuse. The case raises the question of whether EdTech developers have an integrity obligation to build misuse-prevention features into study tools. UNESCO's 2023 AI in Education guidance does not address tool-builder liability.

**Chính sách châu Á:**

> NUS (Singapore) adopted an AI disclosure framework (2023/2024) that distinguishes permitted AI study tools from prohibited AI submission substitutes — a policy approach that would have avoided the Craver contradition. NTU's zero-mark policy focuses on submitted work, not tool-building.

### Góc nhìn EdTech

**AI tutor vs AI cheater:**

> The Eightball case directly engages the Rose Luckin (AI as tutor) vs. Neil Selwyn (AI as cheater) debate. Eightball was designed as an AI tutor (study aids, practice tests). Emory treated it as a potential AI cheater enabler. The case illustrates that the same tool can be pedagogically beneficial or academically fraudulent depending on the context of use — and that blanket prohibition of AI study tools is counterproductive.

**Chính sách EdTech companies:**

> Eightball represents the student-as-EdTech-builder category. Khan Academy Khanmigo (Socratic AI tutor, refuses to write essays), Coursera's AI-assisted content, and Duolingo's in-product AI all provide comparable study aid functions without being classified as honor code violations — revealing the inconsistency of penalizing student-built equivalents.

**Đề xuất nhà nghiên cứu EdTech:**

> From an EdTech research perspective: (1) universities should establish an explicit 'permitted AI study tools' framework before running startup competitions that reward AI EdTech; (2) honor code provisions on facilitation should require demonstrated harm (actual misuse), not theoretical capability; (3) AI-resistant assessment design (oral defense, process documentation) makes study tool AI neutral to academic integrity; (4) the AIAS 5-tier framework (Perkins) for permitted AI use should be extended to cover EdTech tool classification.

### Nguồn tài liệu

**Bài báo khoa học:**

> Perkins, M., et al. (2023). Academic Integrity Considerations of AI Large Language Models in the Post-ChatGPT Era. Journal of University Teaching & Learning Practice, 20(2). Haider, A.S., et al. (2024). Using AI in Education: Academic Integrity Perspectives. Journal of Academic Ethics.

**Sách tham khảo:**

> Eaton, S.E. (2021). Plagiarism in Higher Education. ABC-CLIO. McCabe, D.L., Butterfield, K.D., & Treviño, L.K. (2012). Cheating in College. Johns Hopkins University Press.

**Nguồn Việt Nam:**

> No direct Vietnamese source. Tangentially relevant: FPT University and Van Lang University's Coursera AI pilot in Vietnam represents the EdTech integration model that the Craver case argues for vs. prohibition.

### Uncertain Fields

- phap_luat_viet_nam (Luật KH&CN 2025 status uncertain)
- ket_qua_xu_ly (final lawsuit outcome not confirmed in search results as of April 2026)

## False positive cases — UK students wrongly accused (2023–2025) {#false-positive-cases-uk-students-wrongly-accused-20232025}

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Type | real_case |
| Institution | UK universities / Office of the Independent Adjudicator (OIA) |
| Year | 2024 |
| Category | false_positive |
| Institution | UK universities / Office of the Independent Adjudicator (OIA) |

### Định nghĩa & Khái niệm

**Định nghĩa:**

> False positive cases arise when AI detection tools (Turnitin, GPTZero, Copyleaks) incorrectly flag authentic student work as AI-generated. The Office of the Independent Adjudicator (OIA, UK higher education ombudsman) upheld complaints where universities imposed penalties on students whose work was falsely identified by algorithmic tools, without adequate consideration of student-provided evidence of authentic authorship.

**Phân loại vi phạm:**

> False positive (procedural injustice): student penalized based on AI detection tool output without sufficient human review of counter-evidence. Sub-cases: (1) autistic student — neurodivergent writing style misidentified as AI; (2) international student using Grammarly — grammar correction tool triggered false AI flag. Related category: ESL false positives (Liang et al. Stanford 2023 — 61.3% of TOEFL essays falsely flagged).

**Lịch sử khái niệm:**

> AI detection tools were commercially deployed from 2023 onward (Turnitin AI detection: April 2023; GPTZero: December 2022). False positive concerns emerged immediately. Liang et al. (Stanford, July 2023) provided the first systematic evidence of bias against non-native English speakers. The OIA (UK) began receiving AI-related complaints in 2023-24, with the first uphold decision publicized July 2024. The cases established that universities cannot rely solely on algorithmic detection without procedural fairness.

### Bằng chứng thực tế

**Tên trường hợp:**

> Case 1: Autistic UK student (institution unnamed) — given zero mark after Turnitin AI flag on assessed work; drafts and planning materials proved originality; OIA upheld complaint, July 2024. Case 2: International student (UK institution unnamed) — wrongly accused after using only Grammarly for grammar correction; AI detector flagged corrected prose as AI-generated. Both cases: UK universities, 2023-24 academic year. OIA oversight.

**Diễn biến:**

> Case 1 (autistic student): An autistic student submitted assessed work that was flagged by AI detection software. The university's disciplinary panel ruled the work violated academic integrity, awarding a zero mark. The student appealed, claiming the detection software was 'biased against their writing style.' The student noted a prior essay had also been flagged but subsequently cleared. The student appealed to the OIA (UK higher education ombudsman) with evidence including essay planning documents, drafts, and preparation materials demonstrating authentic authorship. The OIA found the university 'didn't properly consider all the points the student raised or the evidence they presented, including their essay planning and preparation.' The university reconsidered and found no academic misconduct — the penalty was overturned. Case 2 (Grammarly user): An international student used Grammarly (a grammar correction tool, not a text generation tool) to improve their writing. The AI detector flagged the grammatically corrected output as AI-generated. The student was accused of academic misconduct. The case was eventually resolved in the student's favor, but caused significant distress and administrative burden.

**Kết quả xử lý:**

> Case 1: OIA upheld complaint (July 2024); university reconsidered; misconduct finding reversed; zero mark overturned. Case 2: Student cleared after review; no penalty applied. Systemic outcome: OIA precedent established that universities must consider all counter-evidence, including planning materials and drafts, before imposing penalties based on AI detection. Multiple UK students subsequently won appeals at Turnitin-flagged cases. Times Higher Education reported that students at several UK universities successfully appealed AI-detection-based penalties.

**Con số thống kê:**

> Turnitin AI detection claimed false positive rate: below 1% at document level; ~4% at sentence level. Liang et al. (Stanford, 2023): 61.3% of TOEFL essays (non-native English speakers) falsely flagged as AI by at least one of 7 tested detectors; 97.8% flagged by at least one detector. UK context: ~7,000 confirmed AI cheating cases in 2023-24 (Guardian FOI); if 4% FP rate applied across all suspected cases, approximately 280+ innocent students would receive initial false accusations per year across UK sector.

### Tác hại & Hậu quả

**Hại cho người học:**

> False accusations cause: (1) immediate academic harm (zero marks, course failure) affecting GPA and graduation timeline; (2) psychological distress and anxiety; (3) reputational damage during appeal process; (4) chilling effect on legitimate AI tool use (e.g., Grammarly, spell-checkers) and on students with distinctive writing styles. Neurodivergent students and international students face disproportionate risk.

**Hại cho nghiên cứu:**

> False positives undermine the validity of AI detection as a research methodology. Academic integrity research must account for false positive rates when citing detection statistics. Over-reliance on detection tools without human review creates unjust outcomes and erodes the institutional evidence base for academic integrity enforcement.

**Hại cho xã hội:**

> False accusations damage trust between students and institutions; discourage legitimate AI tool adoption (Grammarly, grammar checkers, reference managers); create legal liability for universities; and may disproportionately harm international student enrollment — economically significant for UK universities. The Markup (August 2023) reporting on false positives generated significant public concern about algorithmic fairness in education.

### Phát hiện & Công cụ

**Công cụ phát hiện:**

> Primary detection tool: Turnitin AI detection (launched April 2023). Also GPTZero, Copyleaks, ZeroGPT. These tools use perplexity and burstiness analysis to estimate probability that text is AI-generated. At document level, Turnitin claims <1% FP rate; at sentence level ~4%. No tool achieves zero false positives. All tools share the fundamental limitation of classifying text based on statistical patterns rather than intent or process.

**Hạn chế công cụ:**

> Core limitations: (1) ESL and neurodivergent bias — Liang et al. (Stanford, 2023): 61.3% FP on TOEFL essays; 97.8% flagged by at least one detector; (2) Grammarly/editing tool bias — grammatically corrected text mimics AI statistical patterns; (3) sentence-level FP rate ~4% means individual sentences are unreliable; (4) arms race with AI humanization tools (undetectable.ai, QuillBot) means sophisticated cheaters evade detection while innocent students are caught; (5) no tool can assess authorship intent; (6) temporal bias — tools trained on older AI models may misclassify text from newer models or flag human text as matching older AI patterns.

**LLM Watermarking:**

> LLM watermarking (Kirchenbauer et al., UMD; Aaronson, OpenAI) would fundamentally change the false positive problem: instead of classifying statistical patterns (high FP risk), watermarking would detect a cryptographic signal in AI-generated text (very low FP risk). However, watermarking requires deployment by AI providers, covers only watermarked models, and is defeated by paraphrasing. Retroactive watermarking is impossible. The UK false positive cases argue urgently for watermarking deployment.

### Phòng ngừa & Giải pháp

**Thiết kế bài tập:**

> Process-based assessment design (drafts, planning documents, annotated bibliographies, oral defense) provides authentic evidence of student work that can exonerate false positive cases — as demonstrated in the OIA autistic student case where draft evidence overturned the penalty. Universities of Sydney, Stanford, and San Diego have adopted staged submission frameworks that generate exonerating process evidence.

**Chính sách trường học:**

> OIA precedent (July 2024) requires UK universities to: (1) consider all counter-evidence before imposing AI-related penalties; (2) give weight to process artifacts (planning materials, drafts); (3) not rely solely on AI detection tool output. Several UK universities issued revised AI misconduct investigation procedures in 2024, requiring human expert review alongside algorithmic detection. Stanford's policy emphasizes faculty judgment over automated detection.

**Văn hóa tổ chức:**

> The false positive cases reveal that enforcement-culture institutions (relying primarily on detection technology) are more prone to procedural injustice than institutions with education-culture approaches. McCabe's framework: when students and faculty have a trust relationship and honor code culture, false accusations are more likely to be resolved through dialogue before formal proceedings.

**AI Literacy:**

> AI literacy for university administrators and faculty must include understanding of detection tool limitations, FP rates, and the heightened vulnerability of neurodivergent and international students. ISTE/CoSN frameworks and UNESCO Art. 4 (EU AI Act) AI literacy requirements should be applied to institutional AI decision-making, not only to student AI use.

### Khung pháp lý & Đạo đức

**Khung quốc tế:**

> EU AI Act (2024) classifies AI-based assessment tools in education as 'high-risk' (Annex III), requiring: transparency, human oversight, accuracy documentation, and right of appeal. UK universities using Turnitin AI detection are subject to these requirements. IEEE EAD and ACM Code of Ethics require fairness and bias assessment in deployed AI systems — violated by tools with 61.3% FP rates for specific populations.

**Pháp luật Việt Nam:**

> Vietnamese higher education law does not address AI detection tools or false positive liability. Nghị định 109/2022/NĐ-CP focuses on research misconduct, not student assessment. Luật Giáo dục 2019 guarantees student rights of appeal — applicable to false positive cases, but no AI-detection-specific procedures exist. This is a critical regulatory gap as Vietnamese universities adopt Turnitin and similar tools.

### Góc nhìn EdTech

**AI tutor vs AI cheater:**

> False positive cases are the most powerful argument against simplistic AI-as-cheater framing. When Grammarly (an AI writing assistant) triggers academic misconduct proceedings against an innocent student, it demonstrates that the AI-as-cheater vs. AI-as-tutor binary fails in practice. Rose Luckin's (UCL) AI democratization argument is undermined if students fear using legitimate AI tools due to false positive risk.

**Chính sách EdTech companies:**

> Turnitin (primary false positive source): launched AI detection April 2023; claims <1% document-level FP, ~4% sentence-level FP; published independent bias study in 2024 (claiming no statistically significant ESL bias — disputed by Liang et al.). Grammarly: writing correction tool explicitly not designed for text generation, but its output triggers AI detectors due to statistical regularization of prose. Khan Academy Khanmigo: explicitly designed as learning scaffold, not text generator — lower false positive risk. The false positive cases argue for industry-wide FP rate transparency standards.

**Đề xuất nhà nghiên cứu EdTech:**

> Key recommendations: (1) AI detection tools must not be used as sole or primary evidence in misconduct proceedings — require corroborating evidence or human expert review; (2) process-based assessment design should be standard to generate authentic exonerating evidence; (3) neurodivergent and ESL students should have explicit safeguards in AI misconduct investigations; (4) universities should publish FP complaint rates annually; (5) UK OIA's July 2024 precedent should be codified in QAA guidance and adopted as model for EU/Asian oversight bodies; (6) LLM watermarking should be prioritized as systemic solution; (7) Grammarly and similar grammar/style tools must be explicitly exempted from AI misconduct definitions.

### Nguồn tài liệu

**Bài báo khoa học:**

> Liang, W., et al. (2023). GPT detectors are biased against non-native English writers. Patterns (Cell Press), 4(7). DOI: 10.1016/j.patter.2023.100779. Kirchenbauer, J., et al. (2023). A Watermark for Large Language Models. ICML 2023. arXiv:2301.10226. The Markup (2023). AI Detection Tools Falsely Accuse International Students of Cheating. August 14, 2023.

**Sách tham khảo:** Eaton, S.E. (2024). Academic Integrity in the Age of AI. Springer. Bretag, T. (Ed.) (2016). Handbook of Academic Integrity. Springer.

**Nguồn Việt Nam:**

> VietnamNet reporting on Turnitin adoption in Vietnamese universities (2023-2024). Nghị định 109/2022/NĐ-CP; Luật Giáo dục 2019 (student rights provisions). Springer study: survey of Vietnamese students on AI and academic integrity (1,386 students, [uncertain citation]).

### Uncertain Fields

- chinh_sach_chau_a (NTU zero-mark for hallucinated citations — specific 2025 policy details unverified)
- con_so_thong_ke (Turnitin document-level FP <1% is company-stated; independent validation contested by Liang et al. results)

## GPT-fabricated papers on Google Scholar (Haider et al., Harvard KSG, 2024) {#gpt-fabricated-papers-on-google-scholar-haider-et-al-harvard-ksg-2024}

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Type | real_case |
| Institution | Harvard Kennedy School Misinformation Review (publication venue); researchers from Sweden (Jutta Haider et al.) |
| Year | 2024 |
| Category | research_fraud |
| Institution | Harvard Kennedy School Misinformation Review (publication venue); researchers from Sweden (Jutta Haider et al.) |

### Định nghĩa & Khái niệm

**Định nghĩa:**

> GPT-fabricated scientific papers are manuscripts generated primarily or entirely by large language models, published in academic venues (indexed journals, non-indexed journals, preprint servers, student repositories) without adequate disclosure of AI generation. Distinct from AI-assisted writing: these papers are fabricated wholesale by AI, often containing hallucinated facts, fake citations, and no genuine intellectual contribution from the listed authors.

**Phân loại vi phạm:**

> Research fraud via fabrication and deceptive AI use. Subcategories: bịa đặt (fabrication — AI-generated false scientific content), gian lận AI (deceptive AI use — 62% of identified papers declared no AI use), and đạo văn (potential plagiarism if AI-generated content reproduces training data). Most papers focused on policy-relevant topics — health and environment — raising risk of weaponized disinformation.

**Lịch sử khái niệm:**

> GPT-fabricated scientific papers emerged as a distinct phenomenon after ChatGPT's public release (November 2022). The Haider et al. study (September 2024) was among the first systematic empirical investigations documenting their prevalence, distribution, and spread in academic databases. Published in Harvard Kennedy School Misinformation Review, a peer-reviewed journal focused on mis/disinformation.

### Bằng chứng thực tế

**Tên trường hợp:**

> Haider, J. et al. (2024). 'GPT-fabricated scientific papers on Google Scholar: Key features, spread, and implications for preempting evidence manipulation.' Harvard Kennedy School Misinformation Review. DOI: https://misinforeview.hks.harvard.edu/article/gpt-fabricated-scientific-papers-on-google-scholar-key-features-spread-and-implications-for-preempting-evidence-manipulation/

**Diễn biến:**

> Jutta Haider and colleagues (Swedish researchers) conducted a systematic study identifying GPT-fabricated papers indexed in Google Scholar. Using Google Scholar searches for characteristic LLM phrases combined with manual verification, they identified 139 papers with suspected deceptive GPT use. Distribution: 19 papers in indexed (reputable) journals; 89 papers in non-indexed journals; 19 papers in student/university databases; 12 preprints (mostly in preprint servers). Health and environment policy topics made up approximately 34% of the sample. Approximately 62% of identified papers declared no AI use. 57% of GPT-fabricated papers concerned policy-relevant subject areas. The papers exhibited characteristic features: repetitive sentence structures, generic claim-making, absence of original data or methodology, hallucinated citations, and LLM artifact phrases. The researchers noted that due to the large number of archives, mirror sites, repositories, and shadow libraries, retracted papers continue to circulate and reach audiences after retraction.

**Kết quả xử lý:**

> The study was published September 2024 in HKS Misinformation Review and received wide media coverage (Newsweek, Science News, AI-fabricated junk science headlines). No mass coordinated removal of identified papers was possible. Google Scholar does not currently have a retraction flagging system equivalent to Retraction Watch. The study highlighted the vulnerability of preprint servers and non-indexed repositories as vectors for spreading fabricated scientific content. Implications for policy: fabricated papers on health and environment policy topics can be cited in regulatory submissions, legislative testimony, and public health guidance.

**Con số thống kê:**

> 139 total papers identified with suspected deceptive GPT use. 19 in indexed journals (12.7%). 89 in non-indexed journals (64.0%). 19 in student/university databases (13.7%). 12 preprints (~8.6%). ~34% focused on health and environment topics. ~62% did not declare AI use. ~57% covered policy-relevant subject areas.

### Tác hại & Hậu quả

**Hại cho người học:**

> Students and researchers using Google Scholar as a primary literature discovery tool are exposed to GPT-fabricated papers that appear legitimate in search results. These papers may be cited in student theses, policy papers, and secondary research, propagating fabricated scientific claims. The presence in university student databases (19 papers) means fabricated content has reached educational assessment contexts.

**Hại cho nghiên cứu:**

> GPT-fabricated papers on policy-relevant topics (health, environment) can corrupt the evidence base used in systematic reviews and meta-analyses. Google Scholar's indexing of preprints and non-indexed journals without integrity verification creates a low-barrier entry point for fabricated content. Once indexed and cited, fabricated papers persist through citation networks even after retraction.

**Hại cho xã hội:**

> 57% of GPT-fabricated papers concerned policy-relevant topics — health interventions, environmental regulations, social policy. These can be cited in legislative processes, regulatory submissions, and public health guidance, potentially influencing real-world decisions based on fabricated scientific evidence. This represents a weaponizable information infrastructure — bad actors can manufacture scientific consensus using LLMs.

### Phát hiện & Công cụ

**Công cụ phát hiện:**

> Primary method: Google Scholar keyword search for LLM artifact phrases combined with manual verification. Haider et al. developed a systematic identification protocol. Secondary: structural analysis for LLM-typical writing patterns (repetition, generic claims, absence of specific data). GPTZero and Turnitin AI detection were referenced as complementary but not used as primary tools in this study.

**Hạn chế công cụ:**

> Google Scholar indexes without integrity verification. Phrase-based detection only catches unsophisticated fabrications — edited AI content evades phrase search. 139 papers represents a lower bound; sophisticated GPT-fabricated papers with edited content are undetectable by current methods. Non-indexed journals typically have no systematic integrity review. Retraction systems do not effectively reach preprint servers and shadow libraries.

**LLM Watermarking:**

> Mandatory LLM watermarking would be a significant technical countermeasure against GPT-fabricated papers. If LLM providers (OpenAI, Anthropic, Google) implemented watermarks, fabricated manuscripts could be verified as LLM-generated regardless of editing. The Haider et al. study cited the absence of such mechanisms as a key gap.

### Phòng ngừa & Giải pháp

**Thiết kế bài tập:**

> For academic submissions: Google Scholar and preprint servers should implement automated AI-content screening with mandatory disclosure. For student assignments: prohibit submission to public preprint servers without instructor review. For policy evidence: evidence synthesis processes (systematic reviews, Cochrane protocols) need explicit quality filters that flag AI-generated content.

**Chính sách trường học:**

> The study revealed that 19 papers appeared in student/university databases — suggesting some institutions accepted or facilitated AI-fabricated submissions. Universities need explicit policies prohibiting GPT-fabricated submissions in all assessment contexts, not just course assignments. Institutional repositories need integrity verification before accepting deposits.

**Văn hóa tổ chức:**

> The concentration in non-indexed, low-oversight venues reflects a systemic gap: journals with minimal review infrastructure become vectors for fabricated content. Building cultures of integrity in lower-prestige venues requires different mechanisms than top-tier journals. The honor code model (McCabe) must extend beyond elite institutions.

**AI Literacy:**

> The study demonstrates that many authors (62% non-disclosers) either did not understand disclosure obligations or deliberately concealed AI use. AI literacy must include: understanding what constitutes GPT-fabricated vs. AI-assisted content, disclosure norms, and the consequences of evidence manipulation in policy contexts.

### Khung pháp lý & Đạo đức

**Khung quốc tế:**

> The study directly implicates UNESCO (2023) AI in Education guidance and EU AI Act (2024) Article 50 on AI-generated content disclosure. The policy manipulation risk — 57% policy-relevant papers — is relevant to EU regulatory processes that rely on scientific evidence. IEEE EAD and ACM Code of Ethics prohibit misrepresentation; GPT-fabrication violates both.

**Pháp luật Việt Nam:**

> Nghị định 109/2022/NĐ-CP Điều 20 applies to Vietnamese researchers engaged in fabrication. The policy manipulation risk is particularly relevant: Vietnamese science policy (QĐ 1705/QĐ-TTg) relies on domestic research evidence. GPT-fabricated papers on Vietnamese educational topics could contaminate this evidence base.

**Chính sách châu Á:**

> The study did not focus specifically on Asian institutions, but non-indexed journals (89 papers) include many from Asian publishing contexts. NUS (Singapore), NTU, and CUHK all reference this type of research in their integrity policy updates. The spread to shadow libraries (sci-hub, etc.) is particularly relevant for Asian academic communities with limited journal access.

### Góc nhìn EdTech

**AI tutor vs AI cheater:**

> The 57% policy-relevant topic concentration is the strongest evidence for AI-as-cheater concerns: LLMs are being weaponized to manufacture scientific consensus on contested policy topics (health, environment). This goes beyond individual academic dishonesty to potential societal harm — evidence manipulation at scale.

**Chính sách EdTech companies:**

> Google Scholar's failure to flag AI-fabricated papers is a significant EdTech policy gap. Google has not announced specific AI-content integrity measures for Scholar. Elicit, Consensus, and other AI research tools that use Google Scholar as a data source may inadvertently surface fabricated papers in research summaries.

**Đề xuất nhà nghiên cứu EdTech:**

> From an EdTech/research integrity perspective: (1) Google Scholar must implement retraction flagging and AI-content disclosure indicators; (2) Policy-making processes must include provenance verification for cited evidence — systematic reviews citing Google Scholar results need integrity audits; (3) Preprint servers (arXiv, bioRxiv, SSRN) need LLM-content disclosure requirements and automated screening; (4) The 'spread' problem (shadow libraries, mirrors) requires international coordination — retraction alone is insufficient; (5) An open database of identified GPT-fabricated papers should be maintained analogous to Retraction Watch.

### Nguồn tài liệu

**Bài báo khoa học:**

> Haider, J. et al. (2024). 'GPT-fabricated scientific papers on Google Scholar: Key features, spread, and implications for preempting evidence manipulation.' HKS Misinformation Review. DOI: https://misinforeview.hks.harvard.edu/article/gpt-fabricated-scientific-papers-on-google-scholar-key-features-spread-and-implications-for-preempting-evidence-manipulation/. Newsweek (2024): 'AI-Generated Junk Science Is Flooding Google Scholar.' Sci.News (2024). InfoDOCKET (September 3, 2024).

**Sách tham khảo:** Eaton, S.E. (2024). Second Handbook of Academic Integrity. Springer. McCabe, D.L. et al. (2012). Cheating in College. Johns Hopkins UP.

**Nguồn Việt Nam:**

> No Vietnamese-language source directly on this study. Relevant to Vietnamese research community via QĐ 1705 evidence base and Nghị định 109/2022. Vietnamese researchers should be aware that Google Scholar (used widely in Vietnam) contains GPT-fabricated papers.

### Uncertain Fields

- ket_qua_xu_ly (no confirmed removals of the 139 papers from Google Scholar — resolution status [uncertain])
- phan_loai (19 student database papers — whether these were submitted as assessments or deposited by authors is [uncertain])
