## Hindawi/Wiley — 8,000+ papers retracted (2023–2024) {#hindawiwiley-8000-papers-retracted-20232024}

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Type | real_case |
| Institution | Hindawi (Wiley subsidiary), open-access publisher |
| Year | 2023 |
| Category | research_fraud |
| Institution | Hindawi (Wiley subsidiary), open-access publisher |

### Định nghĩa & Khái niệm

**Định nghĩa:**

> Paper mills are unofficial commercial operations that generate fabricated or low-quality manuscripts — often using AI-generated content — and sell authorship slots to researchers seeking publications for career advancement. This constitutes multiple forms of research fraud: fabrication, contract cheating (ghostwriting at scale), and manipulation of peer review.

**Phân loại vi phạm:**

> Large-scale research fraud via paper mills: AI-generated content fabrication, manipulated peer review (guest editor exploitation), contract authorship fraud, and systematic corruption of scientific publishing infrastructure. Category: research_fraud at industrial scale.

**Lịch sử khái niệm:**

> Paper mills have existed since the early 2000s but scaled dramatically with AI text generation tools after 2022. Hindawi (founded 1997, acquired by Wiley 2021) expanded rapidly through open-access special issues managed by guest editors — a model that proved vulnerable to paper mill exploitation. The 2023 retraction wave was the largest in publishing history.

### Bằng chứng thực tế

**Tên trường hợp:**

> Hindawi (Wiley) Mass Retraction — 8,000+ articles retracted from special issues, 2023. Wiley shut 19 journals (May 2024). Total Wiley retractions: ~11,000. Financial damage: $35-40 million.

**Diễn biến:**

> Paper mills identified Hindawi's special issues model as exploitable: guest editors (often from developing countries) managed special issues and accepted manuscripts from colleagues, friends, or paper mill clients with minimal review. Paper mills used AI tools to fabricate manuscripts that superficially resembled genuine research. Wiley acquired Hindawi in 2021 and inherited these vulnerabilities. By mid-2023, research integrity investigators (including Dr. Jennifer Byrne, Elisabeth Bik) had flagged thousands of suspicious papers. Hindawi initiated mass retractions. Wiley retracted 8,000+ Hindawi articles in 2023 — representing approximately 80% of all global retractions in that year. Wiley closed 4 journals in May 2023, then 19 more in May 2024. Total across Wiley: approximately 11,000 retractions as of 2024.

**Kết quả xử lý:**

> Hindawi effectively ceased to function as an independent publishing brand. Wiley absorbed or closed its journals. Financial losses of $35-40 million were reported. Wiley published a whitepaper on detecting paper mill fraud. Multiple authors faced institutional investigations in their home countries. The case contributed to global discussion about open-access publishing vulnerabilities and the need for AI-assisted fraud detection at the submission stage. Crossref and other bibliographic services began flagging retracted papers more prominently.

**Con số thống kê:**

> 8,000+ articles retracted by Hindawi in 2023. 11,000 total Wiley retractions. ~80% of all global retractions in 2023 came from Hindawi. 23 journals closed total (4 in May 2023, 19 in May 2024). Financial damage: $35-40 million. 10,000+ total papers retracted globally in 2023 — breaking previous annual records.

### Tác hại & Hậu quả

**Hại cho người học:**

> Thousands of papers — many on health, environmental science, and engineering topics — contaminated the literature that students and practitioners rely on. Graduate students who cited these papers in theses and dissertations may have unknowingly built work on fabricated research. The Hindawi brand (once associated with legitimate open-access publishing) lost credibility.

**Hại cho nghiên cứu:**

> Mass retraction of 8,000+ papers represents a catastrophic knowledge contamination event. Papers about medical treatments, materials science, and environmental policy — some already cited by other researchers — had to be traced and their citations invalidated. The peer review system's failure was exposed: guest editor models with inadequate oversight cannot catch systematic fraud. Research integrity databases (Retraction Watch) became critical infrastructure.

**Hại cho xã hội:**

> Open-access publishing model (democratizing knowledge) was weaponized by paper mills. Developing-world researchers facing 'publish or perish' pressures were both perpetrators and victims of this system. Public trust in open-access science declined. Academic qualifications obtained through paper mill publications (common in some career advancement systems) were called into question.

### Phát hiện & Công cụ

**Công cụ phát hiện:**

> Multiple detection methods: (1) AI-text detection identifying LLM-generated content in manuscripts; (2) Image manipulation detection (Elisabeth Bik's methods for duplicated figures); (3) Similarity analysis for templated paper mill manuscripts; (4) Network analysis identifying suspicious author-reviewer relationships; (5) Citation network analysis flagging self-citation rings; (6) Wiley developed its own integrity verification platform using AI tools.

**Hạn chế công cụ:**

> AI-text detection tools failed to catch sophisticated paper mill output that blended AI-generated text with human editing. The scale of the problem (thousands of papers) overwhelmed manual review. Guest editors with conflicts of interest could approve flagged papers anyway. The arms race between paper mills and detection tools continues: mills adapted to avoid detectable AI phrases.

**LLM Watermarking:**

> LLM watermarking was not available or deployed during the Hindawi crisis. Retroactive detection had to rely on linguistic patterns and metadata. If OpenAI or Anthropic watermarking were standard in 2022-2023, provenance of AI-generated manuscripts could have been established. The Hindawi case is frequently cited as motivation for deploying watermarking in academic publishing workflows.

### Phòng ngừa & Giải pháp

**Thiết kế bài tập:**

> For academic integrity in publication: submission systems need AI-content detection at intake. Double-blind review with real reviewer identity verification. Limiting guest editor discretion. Mandatory author identity verification (ORCID) and institutional affiliation confirmation.

**Chính sách trường học:**

> Hindawi/Wiley case influenced major publishers' policies: Springer Nature, Elsevier, and Taylor & Francis all tightened AI-use disclosure requirements post-2023. COPE (Committee on Publication Ethics) updated guidelines for handling paper mill submissions. Many universities updated their policies to prohibit purchasing authorship from paper mills explicitly.

**Văn hóa tổ chức:**

> The case reveals how 'publish or perish' incentive structures — particularly in systems where promotion requires journal publications — drive researchers toward paper mills. McCabe's research on honor code culture vs. enforcement is directly relevant: structural incentives must change, not just detection.

**AI Literacy:**

> Paper mill use requires minimal AI literacy — mill clients simply purchase manuscripts. However, educating researchers about how AI-generated papers are detected and the career consequences of retraction is critical. AI literacy must include understanding that AI can generate plausible-looking but fraudulent scientific content.

### Khung pháp lý & Đạo đức

**Khung quốc tế:**

> The Hindawi case prompted: COPE updated guidelines (2023); ICMJE disclosure recommendations updated; CrossRef retraction notification improvements; EU discussions about open-access funding conditionality on integrity. The case is cited in EU AI Act discussions about AI-generated academic content.

**Pháp luật Việt Nam:**

> Vietnam's Nghị định 109/2022/NĐ-CP (Điều 20) prohibits fabrication in scientific research and requires honest reporting. Vietnamese researchers publishing in Hindawi journals were among those affected. The Ministry of Education and Training (MOET) has cited paper mill risks in guidance documents, though specific AI-content fabrication is not yet explicitly addressed.

**Chính sách châu Á:**

> Multiple retracted Hindawi papers had authors from Chinese, Indian, and Middle Eastern universities. Saveetha University (India) appeared frequently in retraction lists. Some Asian universities' promotion criteria that counted Hindawi publications had to revise their journal whitelists.

### Góc nhìn EdTech

**AI tutor vs AI cheater:**

> The Hindawi case is a clear AI-as-cheater scenario at industrial scale. Paper mills used AI not as a learning or discovery tool but purely as a fraud mechanism. Rose Luckin's optimistic framing of AI as democratic educator is challenged by this case — AI can also democratize academic fraud.

**Chính sách EdTech companies:**

> Publishing technology companies (Clarivate, CrossRef, iThenticate/Turnitin) accelerated development of paper mill detection features. iThenticate updated its system to detect templated paper mill manuscripts. The case drove investment in submission integrity verification as a commercial service.

**Đề xuất nhà nghiên cứu EdTech:**

> From an EdTech/research integrity perspective: (1) Publisher submission systems must integrate AI-content detection, image forensics, and citation network analysis at intake — not post-publication; (2) Guest editor models need mandatory editorial oversight and conflict-of-interest screening; (3) Open-access APCs (article processing charges) should not be collected until integrity checks pass; (4) ORCID and institutional affiliation verification must be mandatory; (5) Publishing incentive structures (publish or perish) must reform to reduce demand for paper mills.

### Nguồn tài liệu

**Bài báo khoa học:**

> Retraction Watch (December 19, 2023): 'Hindawi reveals process for retracting more than 8,000 paper mill articles.' The Register (May 16, 2024): 'Wiley shuts 19 scholarly journals amid AI paper mill problem.' IFLScience (2023): '10,000 Research Papers Were Retracted In 2023.' Chemistry World (2024) on AI tools combating paper mill fraud.

**Sách tham khảo:**

> Eaton, S.E. (2024). Second Handbook of Academic Integrity. Springer. Bretag, T. (2016). Handbook of Academic Integrity. Springer. McCabe, D.L. et al. (2012). Cheating in College. Johns Hopkins UP.

**Nguồn Việt Nam:**

> No specific Vietnamese source on this case. However, VietnamNet and Tuổi Trẻ covered the broader paper retraction wave. Some Vietnamese researchers have published in Hindawi journals. Nghị định 109/2022 is the relevant domestic regulatory framework.

### Uncertain Fields

- con_so_thong_ke (total Wiley retraction count fluctuates as new retractions are processed — '11,000' is an approximation as of 2024)
- hai_cho_nghien_cuu (downstream citation contamination scale not fully quantified)

## NeurIPS 2025 — 100+ hallucinated citations in 51 papers (2026) {#neurips-2025-100-hallucinated-citations-in-51-papers-2026}

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Type | real_case |
| Institution | NeurIPS (Neural Information Processing Systems) — top global AI/ML conference |
| Year | 2026 |
| Category | research_fraud |
| Institution | NeurIPS (Neural Information Processing Systems) — top global AI/ML conference |

### Định nghĩa & Khái niệm

**Định nghĩa:**

> Hallucinated citations are AI-fabricated bibliographic references — fake authors, titles, DOIs, journal names, and volume numbers that do not correspond to any real publication. Unlike plagiarism (copying real work), hallucinated citations constitute fabrication, a form of research fraud where false information is presented as factual evidence in scientific literature.

**Phân loại vi phạm:**

> Research fraud via fabrication (citation hallucination). AI-assisted dishonesty — authors used LLMs to generate literature reviews or citation lists without verifying reference validity. Category overlaps with: gian lận (fraud), bịa đặt (fabrication), and AI-assisted dishonesty.

**Lịch sử khái niệm:**

> Citation hallucination as a distinct integrity problem emerged after ChatGPT's release (November 2022). The NeurIPS 2025 case (reported January 2026) is the highest-profile instance at a top-tier venue. It prompted urgent discussions about LLM-generated text in scientific writing and the failure of existing peer review to catch citation fabrication.

### Bằng chứng thực tế

**Tên trường hợp:** NeurIPS 2025 Conference — GPTZero report on hallucinated citations in accepted papers, published January 21, 2026.

**Diễn biến:**

> On January 21, 2026, GPTZero released a report analyzing all 4,841 papers accepted at NeurIPS 2025 (held November 2025). The tool identified 100+ hallucinated citations spread across 51 accepted papers. Hallucinated references ranged from obvious placeholder names ('John Doe and Jane Smith') to sophisticated fake citations with plausible-sounding titles and near-valid DOIs (e.g., 'arXiv:2305.XXXX' with placeholder IDs). One paper contained 15 hallucinated citations. All 51 papers had cleared peer review by 3+ reviewers. NeurIPS 2025 received 21,575 paper submissions — the largest in the conference's history — creating extreme pressure on peer reviewers. The irony was widely noted: the world's premier AI research conference, papers about AI, contaminated by AI hallucinations.

**Kết quả xử lý:**

> As of early 2026, NeurIPS organizers acknowledged the report. No mass retractions were announced immediately. Individual papers were under investigation. The incident prompted calls for mandatory citation verification tools in the review process, automated DOI/reference validation, and stricter disclosure requirements for LLM-assisted writing. Root causes identified: submission tsunami (21,575 papers), KPI-driven academic culture incentivizing speed over rigor, blind trust in AI writing tools, and peer review that does not incentivize citation verification.

**Con số thống kê:**

> 4,841 total papers scanned. 51 papers (1.05%) contained hallucinated citations. 100+ individual hallucinated citations identified. 21,575 papers submitted to NeurIPS 2025 (record high). Each affected paper reviewed by 3+ expert reviewers. Detection rate by peer review: 0% (no hallucinated citations caught by human reviewers).

### Tác hại & Hậu quả

**Hại cho người học:**

> Graduate students and early-career researchers citing NeurIPS 2025 papers may unknowingly propagate hallucinated citations into their own work, building on a contaminated foundation. Academic training relies on the integrity of published literature — citation hallucination undermines the learning infrastructure.

**Hại cho nghiên cứu:**

> Hallucinated citations corrupt the knowledge graph of AI/ML research. Other researchers may cite these papers, reference the fake citations, or build experiments based on work that doesn't exist. The failure of peer review to detect this contamination erodes confidence in the review system itself. Retraction cascades are possible if papers built on hallucinated foundations are subsequently retracted.

**Hại cho xã hội:**

> NeurIPS is the primary venue influencing AI policy, industry practice, and public trust in AI research. Papers from this conference are cited in regulatory documents, product development, and news coverage. Hallucinated citations in policy-relevant AI research can mislead policymakers and the public.

### Phát hiện & Công cụ

**Công cụ phát hiện:**

> GPTZero's hallucination detection tool (launched for reference verification) scanned all 4,841 papers and flagged 51 with suspicious citations. The tool cross-referenced DOIs, author names, and paper titles against real bibliographic databases. GPTZero — originally an AI-text detector — extended its capabilities to citation verification.

**Hạn chế công cụ:**

> GPTZero's detection of hallucinated citations relies on bibliographic database lookup — it can miss hallucinations that closely mimic real papers (e.g., slightly wrong page numbers or volume numbers). Sophisticated LLM users can manually verify citations before submission, defeating automated detection. GPTZero's AI-text detection alone would not catch this — citation hallucination requires separate bibliographic verification.

**LLM Watermarking:**

> LLM watermarking (Kirchenbauer/UMD, Aaronson/OpenAI) would not directly prevent citation hallucination, as the problem is in the generated content's factual accuracy, not in its provenance. However, watermarking could help identify which sections of a paper were AI-generated, enabling reviewers to scrutinize citations in those sections more carefully.

### Phòng ngừa & Giải pháp

**Thiết kế bài tập:**

> For academic training: require authors/students to provide live hyperlinks or verified DOIs for every citation. Automated citation verification as part of paper submission pipelines (similar to Crossref validation). Process-based submission requiring citation verification step before final submission.

**Chính sách trường học:**

> The incident has implications for institutional policies requiring disclosure of LLM use in manuscript preparation. Stanford, MIT, and NeurIPS itself had no mandatory citation verification requirement in 2025. Post-incident proposals include making DOI verification mandatory at submission.

**Văn hóa tổ chức:**

> The high submission volume (21,575) and competitive culture at top ML venues creates pressure to use AI writing tools without adequate verification. McCabe's honor code research suggests that enforcement alone (post-hoc detection) is insufficient — cultural norms of rigor must be internalized.

**AI Literacy:**

> The case demonstrates a critical AI literacy gap: many researchers do not understand that LLMs hallucinate citations and treat AI-generated reference lists as reliable. AI literacy in research contexts must specifically include understanding of hallucination risks and citation verification practices.

### Khung pháp lý & Đạo đức

**Khung quốc tế:**

> The incident directly implicates the EU AI Act (2024): NeurIPS papers often concern high-risk AI systems (Annex III). Hallucinated citations in such papers could mislead regulatory bodies. IEEE Code of Ethics and ACM Code of Ethics both require accuracy in scientific publications — citation fabrication violates both.

**Pháp luật Việt Nam:**

> Vietnam's Nghị định 109/2022/NĐ-CP (Điều 20) prohibits fabrication in scientific research but does not specifically address AI-assisted citation hallucination. The legal framework does not yet require citation verification disclosures for AI-assisted manuscripts.

**Chính sách châu Á:**

> NTU Singapore (2025) already imposed zero marks for papers containing hallucinated citations, predating the NeurIPS finding. The NeurIPS case may accelerate policy adoption in Asian universities participating in international AI research.

### Góc nhìn EdTech

**AI tutor vs AI cheater:**

> This case epitomizes the AI-as-cheater framing: AI tools that assist with literature reviews can silently fabricate citations, and researchers who over-rely on AI without verification become unwitting fraudsters. Neil Selwyn's concern (Monash) about AI enabling academic fraud is validated at the highest level of AI research itself.

**Chính sách EdTech companies:**

> Research manuscript preparation tools (Grammarly, Writefull, Elicit, Consensus) need citation hallucination warnings. Elicit and Consensus — AI research tools — specifically advertise grounded citations, positioning themselves against this problem. Conference submission systems (OpenReview used by NeurIPS) could integrate automated DOI verification.

**Đề xuất nhà nghiên cứu EdTech:**

> From an EdTech/research integrity perspective: (1) Citation verification must become a mandatory submission step — automated DOI/CrossRef validation should be built into OpenReview and similar platforms; (2) LLM disclosure must include whether AI was used in literature review generation; (3) Peer reviewers should be trained and incentivized to verify citations; (4) Conference submission systems should flag papers with unverified DOIs before review; (5) Attribution standards must evolve to require verification certificates for AI-assisted bibliographies.

### Nguồn tài liệu

**Bài báo khoa học:**

> GPTZero Report (January 21, 2026): 'GPTZero finds 100 new hallucinations in NeurIPS 2025 accepted papers' (gptzero.me/news/neurips/). TechCrunch (January 21, 2026). Fortune (January 21, 2026). The Register (January 22, 2026). The Decoder (2026). Haider et al. (2024) HKS Misinformation Review on GPT-fabricated papers.

**Sách tham khảo:** Eaton, S.E. (2024). Second Handbook of Academic Integrity. Springer (1991pp). McCabe, D.L. et al. (2012). Cheating in College. Johns Hopkins UP.

**Nguồn Việt Nam:**

> No direct Vietnamese sources on this specific case. Case is relevant to Vietnamese EdTech researchers studying integrity in AI-era academic publishing. QĐ 1705/QĐ-TTg (2022) emphasizes international research integration — Vietnamese researchers publishing internationally are exposed to these norms.

### Uncertain Fields

- ket_qua_xu_ly (retraction decisions ongoing as of early 2026 — final outcomes not confirmed)
- con_so_thong_ke (GPTZero report states '100+' — exact count may be higher)

## University of Sheffield — 92 AI misconduct cases (2023–24) {#university-of-sheffield-92-ai-misconduct-cases-202324}

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Type | real_case |
| Institution | University of Sheffield, UK |
| Year | 2024 |
| Category | institutional_data |
| Institution | University of Sheffield, UK |

### Định nghĩa & Khái niệm

**Định nghĩa:**

> Institutional data documenting the explosive growth of AI-related academic misconduct at a UK Russell Group university. The University of Sheffield defines AI misconduct as the unauthorized use of AI generative tools (including ChatGPT and similar LLMs) to produce assessed work, classified under its three-tier academic misconduct policy (poor academic practice / moderate offence / severe offence).

**Phân loại vi phạm:**

> AI-assisted academic misconduct (unauthorized use of generative AI in assessed work). Sheffield's policy tiers: (1) poor academic practice — inadvertent/low-level; (2) moderate offence — behavior intended to deceive marker; (3) severe offence — extensive misconduct, repeated incidents, clear intent to deceive including AI-generated submission. Possible overlap with contract cheating where AI is used as the 'contractor.'

**Lịch sử khái niệm:**

> Sheffield had 6 suspected AI cases in 2022-23 (the year ChatGPT launched). In 2023-24, cases rose to 92 — a ~15x increase in one year. This mirrors the pattern seen across UK Russell Group universities, where Freedom of Information requests (Times Higher Education, 2024) revealed dramatic year-on-year increases. Sheffield's data became a benchmark case for the UK sector's response.

### Bằng chứng thực tế

**Tên trường hợp:**

> University of Sheffield, Russell Group, South Yorkshire, UK. 2022-23: 6 suspected AI misconduct cases, 6 penalties. 2023-24: 92 suspected AI misconduct cases, 79 students penalized. Data obtained through Freedom of Information requests and reported in Times Higher Education and The Cheatsheet (Substack).

**Diễn biến:**

> Following ChatGPT's launch (November 2022), the University of Sheffield recorded only 6 suspected AI misconduct cases in 2022-23 — consistent with very early-stage tool adoption. By 2023-24, as AI tools became mainstream and student awareness grew, 92 cases were suspected. Of these, 79 students received penalties. The university had to develop AI-specific misconduct categories within its existing three-tier framework. The data was revealed through Freedom of Information requests submitted to Russell Group universities, part of a broader investigative journalism effort (Times Higher Education) that exposed widespread AI cheating increases across UK higher education.

**Kết quả xử lý:**

> 79 students penalized across 92 suspected cases. Penalties under Sheffield's framework range from mark reduction (poor academic practice tier) to zero on assignment (moderate tier) to course failure, suspension, or degree revocation (severe tier). The 13 cases without penalty may reflect insufficient evidence or successful student appeals. The data triggered university-level reviews of AI policy and assessment design.

**Con số thống kê:**

> 2022-23: 6 suspected cases, 6 penalties. 2023-24: 92 suspected cases, 79 penalties (86% penalty rate). Year-on-year increase: ~15x (1,433% increase). Sheffield ranks among the highest documented AI misconduct rates in the Russell Group sector. UK context: Guardian FOI data shows nearly 7,000 confirmed AI cheating cases across UK universities in 2023-24 (5.1 per 1,000 students).

### Tác hại & Hậu quả

**Hại cho người học:**

> The 79 penalized students faced academic record impacts ranging from grade reduction to degree suspension. The broader cohort of students who do not cheat faces increased surveillance, more restrictive assessment conditions, and reduced trust from faculty — collateral costs imposed by the minority who cheat.

**Hại cho nghiên cứu:**

> At Sheffield, a major research university (Russell Group), AI-generated submissions in research methods courses, dissertation components, and scientific writing assignments directly compromise the research training pipeline. Graduates who substituted AI for authentic research skill development enter the workforce and research community with credential gaps.

**Hại cho xã hội:**

> The 15x increase in one year signals a structural shift in the scale of academic dishonesty. UK employers and graduate admissions committees began questioning Sheffield and other UK degrees' integrity. Media coverage of the FOI data damaged public trust in higher education quality. The data contributed to government and QAA (Quality Assurance Agency) pressure on universities to update AI policies.

### Phát hiện & Công cụ

**Công cụ phát hiện:**

> Sheffield's detection relied on: (1) Turnitin AI detection module (launched 2023) — integrated into existing plagiarism detection workflow; (2) faculty expert judgment on stylistic inconsistencies; (3) comparison of submission with known AI outputs. The university created an explicit 'AI' category in its misconduct tracking system following the rise in cases.

**Hạn chế công cụ:**

> Turnitin's AI detection has a 4% sentence-level false positive rate, and higher rates for non-native English speakers (Liang et al., Stanford 2023: 61.3% for TOEFL essays). Sheffield's international student population (including large cohorts from China, India, Middle East) creates elevated false-positive risk. 13 suspected cases without penalties may include false positives that were resolved on appeal. The arms race between detection tools and 'AI humanization' tools (undetectable.ai, QuillBot) means actual cheating rates likely exceed detected rates.

**LLM Watermarking:**

> UK HEPI (Higher Education Policy Institute) and QAA have advocated for LLM watermarking as a systemic solution. Kirchenbauer et al. (UMD) and Aaronson's (OpenAI) proposals, if implemented, would allow Sheffield to verify AI origin of suspected submissions. As of 2023-24, no watermarking was available, limiting detection to stylistic analysis.

### Phòng ngừa & Giải pháp

**Thiết kế bài tập:**

> Sheffield's academic misconduct surge triggered university-wide review of assessment design. Recommended reforms: in-person written assessments for high-stakes coursework; oral viva components for dissertations; process-based submissions (drafts, annotated bibliographies); reflective journals. QAA published UK-wide guidance on AI-resistant assessment in 2024.

**Chính sách trường học:**

> Sheffield's academic misconduct policy operates on three tiers (poor practice, moderate, severe) applied to AI violations. Post-2023-24 data, Sheffield updated its policy guidance to provide explicit AI-specific examples at each tier level. No blanket AI ban — contextual approach with faculty-determined permission levels. Sheffield is among the UK universities that adopted nuanced (rather than absolute prohibit-or-permit) stances.

**Văn hóa tổ chức:**

> The 15x case increase suggests Sheffield's existing honor culture did not deter AI misuse. McCabe's research supports the need for proactive integrity education alongside enforcement. Sheffield's student newspaper (The Tab) reported on school-level misconduct rates, creating public accountability pressure within the institution.

**AI Literacy:**

> The rapid case growth from 6 to 92 suggests many students exploited the policy vacuum of 2022-23 when AI norms were unclear. Sheffield and UK universities broadly implemented AI literacy workshops and policy communications in 2023-24 as a response. UNESCO Art. 4 / EU AI Act AI literacy mandates are consistent with this remedial approach.

### Khung pháp lý & Đạo đức

**Khung quốc tế:**

> UK Quality Assurance Agency (QAA) and OIA (Office of the Independent Adjudicator) provide the institutional oversight framework. UNESCO 2023 AI in education guidance informed UK sector policy. EU AI Act (2024) applies to UK AI tool providers (Turnitin has EU operations), affecting detection tool transparency requirements.

**Pháp luật Việt Nam:**

> No equivalent UK-style FOI mechanism for Vietnamese universities to report academic misconduct statistics publicly. Nghị định 109/2022/NĐ-CP lacks AI-specific provisions. The Sheffield 15x growth pattern likely replicates in Vietnam but is untracked — a regulatory gap in Vietnamese higher education oversight.

**Chính sách châu Á:**

> Hong Kong's CUHK (expulsion policy, 2023) and Singapore's NUS (disclosure framework, 2023) represent the Asian policy spectrum. Sheffield's data-driven approach (tracking, FOI transparency) is more systematic than most Asian university responses, which tend to be policy announcement-driven without published misconduct statistics.

### Góc nhìn EdTech

**AI tutor vs AI cheater:**

> Sheffield's 92-case institutional data confirms that the dominant student behavior is AI-as-cheater, not AI-as-learning-tool. The 15x growth in one year, correlated with wider AI tool adoption, supports Neil Selwyn's (Monash) concern that unrestricted AI access primarily enables academic fraud without pedagogical redesign.

**Chính sách EdTech companies:**

> Turnitin is the primary detection tool used by Sheffield — its AI detection module (launched April 2023) is integrated into the UK higher education workflow. Duolingo, Coursera, and Khan Academy impose their own AI guardrails for assessed activities. The Sheffield data illustrates the gap between EdTech platform controls and student-facing AI tool availability.

**Đề xuất nhà nghiên cứu EdTech:**

> From EdTech research perspective: (1) mandatory annual publication of AI misconduct statistics by all UK universities (extend FOI transparency); (2) QAA-mandated AI-resistant assessment standards for all programmes; (3) Turnitin and alternative detection tools must publish validated false positive rates by student demographic; (4) AI literacy integration into freshers' induction as standard; (5) longitudinal research tracking Sheffield's case trends through 2024-25 to assess policy intervention effectiveness.

### Nguồn tài liệu

**Bài báo khoa học:**

> Times Higher Education (2024) — FOI investigation on Russell Group AI cheating cases (primary source for Sheffield data). Liang, W., et al. (2023). GPT detectors are biased against non-native English writers. Patterns. DOI: 10.1016/j.patter.2023.100779. Newton, P.M., & Xiromeriti, M. (2023). Contract Cheating and Assessment Design. Assessment & Evaluation in Higher Education.

**Sách tham khảo:** Bretag, T. (Ed.) (2016). Handbook of Academic Integrity. Springer. Eaton, S.E. (2021). Plagiarism in Higher Education. ABC-CLIO.

**Nguồn Việt Nam:**

> No direct Sheffield-Vietnam link. Relevant comparison: Vietnamese university academic integrity reporting has no public data equivalent. Nghị định 109/2022/NĐ-CP Điều 20; QĐ 1705/QĐ-TTg on education strategy.

### Uncertain Fields

- con_so_thong_ke (92 cases and 79 penalties confirmed via search results; the ~15x figure confirmed as increase from 6 cases)

## Springer Nature — 2,923 papers retracted in 2024 {#springer-nature-2923-papers-retracted-in-2024}

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Type | real_case |
| Institution | Springer Nature (global scientific publisher, 3,000+ journals) |
| Year | 2024 |
| Category | research_fraud |
| Institution | Springer Nature (global scientific publisher, 3,000+ journals) |

### Định nghĩa & Khái niệm

**Định nghĩa:**

> A paper retraction is a formal withdrawal of a published article from the scientific record, typically due to errors, misconduct, or integrity violations. In the context of AI-era research fraud, retractions increasingly involve AI-generated content, manipulated images, paper mill submissions, and citation fabrication. Springer Nature's 2024 retraction record represents the highest single-publisher annual retraction count in history.

**Phân loại vi phạm:**

> Multiple integrity violation types contributing to the 2024 retraction record: (1) Paper mill submissions (AI-generated content, fake authorship); (2) Data fabrication/falsification; (3) Plagiarism; (4) Undisclosed AI-generated text (post-2023 surge); (5) Peer review manipulation. The Neurosurgical Review sub-case is specifically attributable to AI-generated manuscripts flooding a specialized journal.

**Lịch sử khái niệm:**

> Springer Nature's retraction rate increased dramatically post-2022, driven by: (1) Improved detection methods (AI-content detection, image forensics); (2) Surge in paper mill activity using AI tools; (3) Post-Hindawi/Wiley heightened scrutiny of open-access publishing; (4) AI-generated manuscripts flooding specialized journals (Neurosurgical Review). The 2024 record was reported by Retraction Watch (February 17, 2025).

### Bằng chứng thực tế

**Tên trường hợp:**

> Springer Nature — 2,923 articles retracted in 2024 (record high). Sub-case: Neurosurgical Review journal flooded with AI-generated manuscripts, resulting in retraction of 129+ papers and suspension of letter/commentary acceptance.

**Diễn biến:**

> Springer Nature's 3,000+ journals published approximately 482,000 articles in 2024, of which 2,923 were retracted — a record for any single publisher in a single year. Of the 2,923 retractions: 61.5% (1,797) were for papers originally published before January 2023; 38.5% (1,126) were for papers published after January 2023 (the post-ChatGPT period). Neurosurgical Review (a Springer Nature journal) was specifically overwhelmed by AI-generated submissions: commentaries and letters to the editor were flooded with LLM-generated manuscripts. The journal paused acceptance of letters and commentaries after detecting the pattern. By early 2025, Neurosurgical Review had retracted 129 papers, with Saveetha University (Chennai, India) appearing prominently in the affiliation list — an institution known for aggressive publication incentive practices and self-citation. The broader Springer Nature retractions were identified through a combination of editorial team reviews, reader/author complaints, integrity tool screening, and the general heightened scrutiny following the Hindawi crisis.

**Kết quả xử lý:**

> 2,923 papers formally retracted in 2024. Neurosurgical Review paused acceptance of commentary/letter submissions pending system review. Springer Nature published public statements on research integrity. The publisher accelerated deployment of integrity-screening tools at submission stage. Post-publication alerts (retraction notices) were added to affected papers. Saveetha University's papers are under continued institutional and editorial scrutiny. Retraction Watch reported the record numbers as evidence of both improved detection (positive) and increased fraud (concerning).

**Con số thống kê:**

> 2,923 Springer Nature retractions in 2024 (record). 482,000 total articles published in 2024. Retraction rate: ~0.6%. 1,797 retractions (61.5%) for pre-2023 papers. 1,126 retractions (38.5%) for post-2023 papers. 129+ Neurosurgical Review retractions (as of early 2025). 3 journals in Springer Nature portfolio specifically cited as heavily affected.

### Tác hại & Hậu quả

**Hại cho người học:**

> Graduate students and researchers using Springer Nature journals (among the most prestigious in science) may cite retracted papers unknowingly. Springer Nature's retracted papers — spanning fields from neurosurgery to materials science — affect training in multiple disciplines. Institutional subscriptions give students access to papers that may still show as 'published' before retraction notices propagate through citation managers.

**Hại cho nghiên cứu:**

> 2,923 retractions from a top publisher contaminate the scientific record across thousands of citations. Downstream papers citing retracted Springer Nature articles inherit the contamination. The Neurosurgical Review case specifically harms clinical neuroscience literature — clinicians making treatment decisions may have been exposed to AI-fabricated case commentaries.

**Hại cho xã hội:**

> Record retractions from a globally trusted publisher erode public confidence in peer review and scientific publishing. The concentration of AI-generated papers in clinical/medical journals (Neurosurgical Review) poses patient safety risks. The financial cost to Springer Nature (reduced impact factors, subscription concerns) may affect open access funding models.

### Phát hiện & Công cụ

**Công cụ phát hiện:**

> Detection methods contributing to the 2024 retraction surge: (1) AI-text detection tools (Turnitin AI, GPTZero, iThenticate) integrated into editorial workflows; (2) Image manipulation detection; (3) Citation pattern analysis; (4) Reader/researcher reports to editorial offices; (5) Retraction Watch's public database incentivizing editors to act on flagged papers; (6) Post-publication investigation by integrity teams.

**Hạn chế công cụ:**

> Despite 2,923 retractions, the 38.5% rate for post-2023 papers suggests many AI-era fraudulent papers remain undetected. AI-content detection tools cannot catch well-edited AI text. The high volume of submissions makes pre-publication screening resource-intensive. Saveetha University papers passed initial peer review — demonstrating that institutional gaming of publication metrics can evade detection.

**LLM Watermarking:**

> LLM watermarking (Kirchenbauer/UMD, Aaronson/OpenAI) would enable Springer Nature's submission system to detect AI-generated manuscripts at intake, before peer review. The Neurosurgical Review flooding — hundreds of AI-generated submissions overwhelming a specialized journal — is exactly the use case watermarking is designed to prevent.

### Phòng ngừa & Giải pháp

**Thiết kế bài tập:**

> For manuscript submission: mandatory AI-use disclosure forms, automated AI-content screening at intake, ORCID identity verification, institutional affiliation confirmation. For academic training: graduate programs should incorporate retraction literacy — understanding why papers are retracted and how to check retraction status before citing.

**Chính sách trường học:**

> Springer Nature updated its AI disclosure policy post-2023: authors must declare AI use in manuscript preparation. The Neurosurgical Review experience is being used by Springer Nature to develop journal-specific submission restrictions. Impact factor concerns for journals with high retraction rates are creating incentives for tighter pre-publication screening.

**Văn hóa tổ chức:**

> The high rate of pre-2023 paper retractions (61.5%) shows that improved detection is uncovering historical fraud — not just new AI-era misconduct. McCabe's research suggests enforcement alone is insufficient without culture change. Springer Nature's retraction transparency (publishing detailed retraction notices) contributes to a culture of accountability.

**AI Literacy:**

> The Neurosurgical Review flooding demonstrates that some authors do not understand that AI-generated submissions to specialized clinical journals are: (1) detectable; (2) a serious integrity violation; (3) potentially dangerous to patient care. Clinical researchers need specific AI literacy training for manuscript preparation.

### Khung pháp lý & Đạo đức

**Khung quốc tế:**

> Record retractions by Springer Nature directly implicate: EU AI Act (2024) Art. 50 on disclosure; COPE (Committee on Publication Ethics) guidelines on AI in publications; ICMJE authorship criteria (AI cannot be an author); UNESCO 2023 AI recommendations. Springer Nature's response is being monitored by the Committee on Publication Ethics as a policy model.

**Pháp luật Việt Nam:**

> Vietnamese researchers publishing in Springer Nature journals are subject to the journal's AI disclosure requirements, regardless of domestic law. The 2024 retraction wave affected Vietnamese-affiliated papers [uncertain — no confirmed count]. Nghị định 109/2022/NĐ-CP requires research integrity; Springer Nature retractions of Vietnamese papers would trigger domestic institutional review.

**Chính sách châu Á:**

> Saveetha University (India) retractions are the most documented Asian institutional case. NTU (Singapore) zero-marks policy for AI-generated work reflects similar pressures. Chinese institutions have also appeared in Springer Nature retraction notices. The Asian 'publish or perish' culture in rapidly expanding research university systems creates structural demand for paper mills.

### Góc nhìn EdTech

**AI tutor vs AI cheater:**

> The Neurosurgical Review case — AI manuscripts flooding a clinical journal — represents a pure AI-as-cheater scenario with patient safety implications. The broader 2,923 retraction figure reflects both improved AI-assisted fraud detection (AI as tool against cheating) and AI-enabled fraud (AI as cheating tool). This dual role of AI is the central tension in EdTech and research integrity discourse.

**Chính sách EdTech companies:**

> Springer Nature invested in integrity screening tools and worked with iThenticate and other vendors to enhance pre-publication detection. Research management platforms (Researcher.Life, Manuscript.io) began offering 'integrity pre-checks' as a service. Turnitin's academic integrity products expanded into research manuscript context.

**Đề xuất nhà nghiên cứu EdTech:**

> From an EdTech/research integrity perspective: (1) Publisher submission systems must include mandatory AI-use disclosure with digital signatures — voluntary declaration is insufficient given the 62% non-disclosure rate found in other studies; (2) Specialized journals in clinical fields (medicine, surgery) need enhanced screening given patient safety stakes; (3) Institutional-level publication audit systems should monitor retraction rates for affiliated researchers; (4) The 61.5% pre-2023 retraction rate shows detection systems are improving — continued investment in retrospective detection is warranted; (5) Retractions should be more visible in citation managers and databases — many researchers still cite retracted papers unknowingly.

### Nguồn tài liệu

**Bài báo khoa học:**

> Retraction Watch (February 17, 2025): 'Springer Nature retracted 2,923 papers last year.' Retraction Watch (February 10, 2025): 'As Springer Nature journal clears AI papers, one university's retractions rise drastically.' Inside Higher Ed (October 16, 2024): 'Springer journal retracted over 200 papers since Sept. 1.' ScholarlyWorld (2025): 'AI-Generated Papers Lead to Retractions in Neurosurgical Review.' Springer Nature (2024): 'Artificial intelligence and retracted science' (AI & SOCIETY journal).

**Sách tham khảo:**

> Eaton, S.E. (2024). Second Handbook of Academic Integrity. Springer (1991pp). McCabe, D.L. et al. (2012). Cheating in College. Johns Hopkins UP. Bretag, T. (2016). Handbook of Academic Integrity. Springer.

**Nguồn Việt Nam:**

> No specific Vietnamese sources on Springer Nature 2024 retractions. Relevant to Vietnamese researchers publishing internationally under Nghị định 109/2022. QĐ 1705/QĐ-TTg's goal of Vietnamese research integration with global science requires awareness of international integrity standards.

### Uncertain Fields

- con_so_thong_ke (exact number of Vietnamese-affiliated papers among 2,923 retractions not confirmed — [uncertain])
- ket_qua_xu_ly (final resolution of Neurosurgical Review retraction count — 129 as of early 2025 but ongoing)

## Texas A&M — Instructor fails entire class (May 2023) {#texas-am-instructor-fails-entire-class-may-2023}

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Type | real_case |
| Institution | Texas A&M University (College Station, TX, USA) |
| Year | 2023 |
| Category | false_positive |
| Institution | Texas A&M University (College Station, TX, USA) |

### Định nghĩa & Khái niệm

**Định nghĩa:**

> A false positive in AI detection occurs when an AI detection tool incorrectly classifies human-written text as AI-generated. This case is among the first widely documented mass false-accusation incidents in higher education involving AI detection tools.

**Phân loại vi phạm:**

> False positive AI detection (erroneous accusation of AI-assisted dishonesty). No actual academic fraud was committed by students; the violation of academic integrity norms was by the instructor's misuse of an unreliable detection method (asking ChatGPT itself whether it wrote the papers).

**Lịch sử khái niệm:**

> The incident occurred in May 2023, shortly after ChatGPT's public release (November 2022). It became a landmark case illustrating the dangers of AI detector misuse in educational settings and was widely cited in subsequent policy debates about AI tools in academia.

### Bằng chứng thực tế

**Tên trường hợp:**

> Texas A&M University — Instructor Jared Mumm, Agricultural Sciences and Natural Resources, threatens to fail entire graduating class based on ChatGPT self-claims (May 2023).

**Diễn biến:**

> Instructor Jared Mumm used ChatGPT itself (not a dedicated AI detector) to evaluate student final assignments by asking the chatbot whether it had written each paper. ChatGPT claimed to have written every single submission. Mumm then told students they would receive an 'X' (incomplete) grade, threatening their graduation. Students protested and provided exonerating evidence — timestamps from Google Documents showing the writing process. Mumm initially ignored this evidence. The case went viral on Reddit and was covered by Rolling Stone, Washington Post, NBC News, and Inside Higher Ed.

**Kết quả xử lý:**

> No students ultimately failed or were prevented from graduating. Dr. Mumm worked individually with students to resolve the final assignments. The university noted publicly that ChatGPT is not a reliable AI detector and cannot accurately identify its own output. The case triggered widespread discussion about institutional policies on AI detection tool use. No formal disciplinary action against Mumm was publicly reported, but the reputational damage and media scrutiny were significant. Key lesson: using generative AI tools as detectors of their own output is methodologically invalid.

**Con số thống kê:**

> All students in the class (exact number not publicly disclosed) were falsely accused. 0 students ultimately penalized. ChatGPT incorrectly claimed authorship of 100% of submitted papers when asked.

### Tác hại & Hậu quả

**Hại cho người học:**

> Students faced extreme stress, uncertainty about graduation, and unfair accusations without due process. The incident demonstrated the psychological and academic harm that false AI-detection accusations can cause, particularly for graduating seniors.

**Hại cho nghiên cứu:**

> The case exposed the methodological flaw of using generative AI as a self-detector. It contributed to growing literature on the unreliability of AI detection tools and set the stage for Stanford's 2023 study (Liang et al.) showing 61.3% false positive rates for ESL writers.

**Hại cho xã hội:**

> Erodes student trust in faculty and institutional fairness. Raises due process concerns in academic integrity proceedings. Demonstrates how premature deployment of unvalidated AI tools can cause mass harm. Contributed to public skepticism about instructor competence in the AI era.

### Phát hiện & Công cụ

**Công cụ phát hiện:**

> Instructor used ChatGPT itself (not a dedicated detector like Turnitin AI or GPTZero) by prompting it to claim authorship of student papers. ChatGPT is a generative model, not a detector, and readily falsely claims authorship of any text with minimal prompting. This misuse was a critical methodological error.

**Hạn chế công cụ:**

> ChatGPT cannot reliably detect whether text was AI-generated — it has no memory of outputs and will claim authorship of arbitrary text. Even legitimate AI detectors (Turnitin, GPTZero, Copyleaks) have documented false positive rates. Liang et al. (Stanford, 2023) found 61.3% false positive rates for ESL writers. This case predates the Stanford study but validates its findings empirically.

**LLM Watermarking:**

> No LLM watermarking was involved or available in May 2023. Watermarking research (Kirchenbauer/UMD, Aaronson/OpenAI) was still in early stages. This case illustrates the vacuum that watermarking is intended to fill — reliable, verifiable provenance of AI-generated text.

### Phòng ngừa & Giải pháp

**Thiết kế bài tập:**

> The case catalyzed educator discussions about assignment design. Process-based submissions (timestamped drafts, in-class writing, oral defenses) were proposed as more reliable than retrospective AI detection. If students had been required to submit drafts with timestamps, the dispute would not have arisen.

**Chính sách trường học:**

> Texas A&M did not have a clear institutional policy on AI detection tool use at the time. Post-incident, Texas A&M published AI detection guidance (ai.tamu.edu/teach-with-ai/detection.html) warning against using AI tools as detectors and recommending against making accusations based solely on AI detection scores.

**Văn hóa tổ chức:**

> The case illustrates the danger of enforcement-only culture without procedural safeguards. McCabe's honor code research suggests that punitive-only approaches without trust-building backfire. The instructor acted unilaterally without institutional review, demonstrating the need for honor code procedures that protect student rights.

**AI Literacy:**

> The case highlighted a significant gap in AI literacy among instructors — specifically, the misunderstanding of what ChatGPT is and is not capable of detecting. Faculty AI literacy is as critical as student AI literacy. UNESCO and ISTE frameworks emphasize that educators need training on AI capabilities and limitations before deploying AI tools in assessment contexts.

### Khung pháp lý & Đạo đức

**Khung quốc tế:**

> The incident preceded formal international frameworks for AI in education. It informed subsequent UNESCO (2023) guidance on AI in education and contributed to discourse around EU AI Act provisions for high-stakes AI-assisted decisions in educational settings (Annex III, Art. 50).

**Pháp luật Việt Nam:**

> Vietnam's regulatory framework (Nghị định 109/2022/NĐ-CP, Điều 20) does not specifically address false accusation scenarios from AI detection. The case is relevant as a cautionary example for Vietnamese institutions developing AI integrity policies.

**Chính sách châu Á:**

> Asian institutions drew lessons from this case. NUS (Singapore) developed disclosure-based frameworks rather than detection-based enforcement. NTU cases (2025) of students receiving zero marks for hallucinated citations show the continuing tension between detection and due process.

### Góc nhìn EdTech

**AI tutor vs AI cheater:**

> This case sits at the intersection: AI tools intended to catch cheating can themselves become instruments of harm when misused. Rose Luckin (UCL) and Neil Selwyn (Monash) debates about AI in education must include AI detection tools, not just AI writing tools.

**Chính sách EdTech companies:**

> The case spurred EdTech companies to clarify the limitations of their detection products. Turnitin and GPTZero publicly noted that their tools provide probability scores, not definitive proof. No EdTech company recommends using ChatGPT itself as a plagiarism detector.

**Đề xuất nhà nghiên cứu EdTech:**

> From an EdTech researcher perspective: (1) Never use generative AI as an AI detector; (2) AI detection scores alone cannot be used as evidence of misconduct — due process requires corroborating evidence; (3) Institutions need clear faculty training programs on AI detection tool limitations; (4) Assign process-based work (drafts, oral defenses) to reduce reliance on detection entirely; (5) Implement institutional review procedures before any mass accusation.

### Nguồn tài liệu

**Bài báo khoa học:**

> Liang et al. (2023) 'GPT detectors are biased against non-native English writers' (Stanford, arXiv). Perkins et al. (2023) on AI detection accuracy. Digital Trends, Washington Post, Rolling Stone (May 2023) news coverage. Inside Higher Ed (May 19, 2023).

**Sách tham khảo:**

> McCabe, D.L., Butterfield, K.D., & Treviño, L.K. (2012). Cheating in College. Johns Hopkins UP. — honor code and due process frameworks. Eaton, S.E. (2024). Second Handbook of Academic Integrity. Springer.

**Nguồn Việt Nam:**

> No direct Vietnamese sources on this case. The case is internationally documented and cited as a cautionary example in global EdTech literature. Relevant to Vietnamese institutional discussions under Nghị định 109/2022.

### Uncertain Fields

- con_so_thong_ke (exact class size not publicly disclosed)
- ket_qua_xu_ly (no official disciplinary outcome for instructor was published)

## 188 students — University of Minnesota system-wide (2023–24) {#188-students-university-of-minnesota-system-wide-202324}

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Type | real_case |
| Institution | University of Minnesota |
| Year | 2024 |
| Category | institutional_data |
| Institution | University of Minnesota |

### Định nghĩa & Khái niệm

**Định nghĩa:**

> Aggregate institutional data on AI-related academic dishonesty at a major US research university. The University of Minnesota defines 'scholastic dishonesty' to include AI-assisted submission in contexts where AI use is prohibited, classified under its Office for Community Standards (OCS) policy alongside plagiarism and fabrication.

**Phân loại vi phạm:**

> Institutional-level AI-related scholastic dishonesty data. Categories of violations include: unauthorized AI use on exams, AI-generated assignment submissions, AI-assisted work misrepresented as student's own. Represents all confirmed violations, not all reported — actual rate is higher due to underreporting.

**Lịch sử khái niệm:**

> University of Minnesota began tracking AI-related violations as a distinct subcategory of academic dishonesty in the 2022-23 academic year following ChatGPT's launch. By 2023-24, AI cases had grown to represent approximately half of all confirmed dishonesty cases — a rapid normalization of AI-assisted cheating within two years of ChatGPT's release.

### Bằng chứng thực tế

**Tên trường hợp:**

> University of Minnesota — Twin Cities campus, Office for Community Standards (OCS) annual academic dishonesty data, 2023-24 academic year. 188 students found responsible for AI-related scholastic dishonesty, comprising approximately 50% of all confirmed dishonesty cases for that year.

**Diễn biến:**

> The University of Minnesota's Office for Community Standards processes all reported academic dishonesty cases through a formal investigative and hearing process. In the 2023-24 academic year, faculty across disciplines reported a significant increase in AI-related submissions. Of all students found responsible for academic dishonesty, 188 were specifically confirmed for AI-related violations. This figure was reported in The Minnesota Daily (student newspaper) citing OCS data. AI cases represented the single largest category — approximately half — of all confirmed dishonesty cases, overtaking traditional plagiarism as the dominant violation type.

**Kết quả xử lý:**

> Individual sanctions vary by case severity and student history: range from assignment failure to course failure to suspension/expulsion. The high-profile Yang case (expulsion) represents the most severe end. OCS data does not publicly disaggregate sanction types for the 188 AI cases. Institutional response: UMN updated its academic integrity guidance to provide faculty with AI-specific reporting frameworks.

**Con số thống kê:**

> 188 students found responsible for AI-related scholastic dishonesty in 2023-24 academic year. Approximately 50% of all confirmed dishonesty cases on Twin Cities campus. Year-on-year trend: significant increase from 2022-23 baseline. Total UMN enrollment Twin Cities: ~52,000 students — AI dishonesty rate approximately 0.36% of enrolled students (confirmed cases only).

### Tác hại & Hậu quả

**Hại cho người học:**

> At scale, 188 cases represent 188 students who substituted AI for genuine learning, potentially graduating with credential but without competence. The high rate also creates adversarial classroom dynamics: faculty design more restrictive assessment conditions, reducing pedagogical flexibility for the majority who do not cheat.

**Hại cho nghiên cứu:**

> In research-intensive programs (graduate/doctoral level), AI-assisted submissions compromise the authenticity of literature reviews, analysis, and written research outputs. At UMN, a major R1 research university, this has direct implications for research pipeline quality.

**Hại cho xã hội:**

> Institutional data showing ~50% of dishonesty cases are AI-related signals a structural shift in academic fraud patterns. This data was widely cited in higher education policy discussions in 2024-2025. Erosion of trust in UMN credentials and pressure on employers/graduate programs to independently verify competence.

### Phát hiện & Công cụ

**Công cụ phát hiện:**

> UMN cases were detected through a mix of: (1) faculty manual comparison with AI outputs; (2) automated detection tools (Turnitin AI detection, GPTZero); (3) stylistic inconsistencies flagged by instructors. No single detection method was standard — approach varied by department and faculty preference.

**Hạn chế công cụ:**

> The 188 confirmed cases likely represent a significant undercount of actual AI violations. Detection tools generate false negatives (sophisticated AI editing evades detection) and false positives (neurodivergent and ESL students flagged incorrectly — Liang et al. 2023: 61.3% FP on TOEFL essays). Turnitin reports 4% sentence-level FP rate. Inconsistent detection standards across departments create unequal enforcement.

**LLM Watermarking:**

> OpenAI's lack of deployed watermarking as of 2023-24 contributed to the difficulty of proving AI use. Kirchenbauer et al. (UMD) and Aaronson (OpenAI) watermarking proposals, if implemented, would strengthen UMN's detection and prosecution of future cases.

### Phòng ngừa & Giải pháp

**Thiết kế bài tập:**

> UMN's response to rising AI cases includes faculty guidance on AI-resistant assessment: in-person exams, oral components, staged submissions, reflective journals, process portfolios. The Minnesota Daily reporting triggered broader campus conversation about pedagogical redesign.

**Chính sách trường học:**

> UMN maintains a formal scholastic dishonesty policy under the Office for Community Standards. Following 2023-24 data, the university developed faculty resources for AI-specific detection and reporting. No blanket AI ban — policy approach allows faculty to set course-level AI rules. The Yang case (parallel case, same institution) confirmed UMN's willingness to impose expulsion for severe violations.

**Văn hóa tổ chức:**

> The 188-case figure suggests enforcement culture is reactive rather than preventive. McCabe's research indicates that honor code cultures with academic integrity education (not just enforcement) produce lower violation rates. UMN's OCS-centered enforcement model may need complementary integrity education programming.

**AI Literacy:**

> The scale of 188 cases in one year suggests many students either did not understand the boundaries of permitted AI use or made a deliberate choice to violate known rules. Both scenarios argue for mandatory AI literacy modules at course enrollment, not just policy documents.

### Khung pháp lý & Đạo đức

**Khung quốc tế:**

> UNESCO 2023 guidance on generative AI in education identifies institutional data tracking as essential for evidence-based policy. UMN's OCS data collection serves as a model for other institutions. EU AI Act Art. 50 transparency requirements would enhance institutional capacity to track AI-generated submissions.

**Pháp luật Việt Nam:**

> No equivalent systematic data collection on AI-related academic dishonesty at Vietnamese universities as of 2024. Nghị định 109/2022/NĐ-CP requires research integrity, but no AI-specific reporting mandate exists. Vietnamese institutions lack the OCS-equivalent infrastructure for systematic tracking.

**Chính sách châu Á:**

> University of Hong Kong (HKU) temporarily banned ChatGPT in early 2023. CUHK issued expulsion-level AI policy in 2023. The UMN 188-case institutional data provides a quantitative benchmark that Asian universities can compare with their own (largely unpublished) AI misconduct data.

### Góc nhìn EdTech

**AI tutor vs AI cheater:**

> Aggregate data shows that the dominant student behavior is AI-as-cheater (submission replacement) rather than AI-as-tutor. This challenges the optimistic EdTech narrative and supports Neil Selwyn's (Monash) concern that AI primarily enables academic fraud in the short term, before pedagogical redesign catches up.

**Chính sách EdTech companies:**

> UMN's institutional data reflects the impact of freely available AI tools (ChatGPT, Gemini, Claude) without EdTech platform controls. Contrast with Khan Academy Khanmigo (refuses to write essays) and Coursera's post-submission AI dialogue — which build integrity guardrails into the platform architecture.

**Đề xuất nhà nghiên cứu EdTech:**

> Recommendations from UMN's data context: (1) all R1 universities should publish annual AI misconduct statistics to establish norms and enable benchmarking; (2) faculty development on AI-resistant assessment design must precede further AI tool proliferation; (3) university-level AI literacy requirement for all entering students; (4) graduated sanction frameworks distinguishing first-time from repeat violations; (5) cross-institutional research on effective prevention strategies.

### Nguồn tài liệu

**Bài báo khoa học:**

> The Minnesota Daily investigative reporting (2024) citing OCS data. Perkins, M., et al. (2023). Academic Integrity Considerations of AI Large Language Models in the Post-ChatGPT Era. Journal of University Teaching & Learning Practice, 20(2).

**Sách tham khảo:**

> McCabe, D.L., Butterfield, K.D., & Treviño, L.K. (2012). Cheating in College. Johns Hopkins University Press. Eaton, S.E. (2021). Plagiarism in Higher Education. ABC-CLIO.

**Nguồn Việt Nam:**

> No equivalent institutional data for Vietnam. Nghị định 109/2022/NĐ-CP; QĐ 1705/QĐ-TTg on education strategy 2030. Springer study: Vietnamese students and academic integrity (1,386 students surveyed [uncertain citation]).

### Uncertain Fields

- con_so_thong_ke (188 figure cited in task brief — not independently confirmed via web search in exact form; UMN OCS confirmed 'approximately 50% of all confirmed dishonesty cases'; 188 may derive from official annual report not publicly indexed)

## Haishan Yang — University of Minnesota (2024–2026) {#haishan-yang-university-of-minnesota-20242026}

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Type | real_case |
| Institution | University of Minnesota |
| Year | 2024 |
| Category | student_disciplinary |
| Institution | University of Minnesota |

### Định nghĩa & Khái niệm

**Định nghĩa:**

> Academic integrity violation involving unauthorized use of AI tools during a restricted examination. The University of Minnesota defines scholastic dishonesty as using AI-generated content in assessments where such use is explicitly prohibited, equivalent to plagiarism under its Office for Community Standards (OCS) policy.

**Phân loại vi phạm:**

> AI-assisted dishonesty (gian lận AI): use of AI language model (ChatGPT) during an open-book qualifying exam that explicitly prohibited AI. Secondary classification: fabrication / evidence alteration alleged by student (claim that faculty edited ChatGPT outputs to match his answers — contested).

**Lịch sử khái niệm:**

> The concept of AI-assisted academic dishonesty emerged after ChatGPT's public release in November 2022. By 2023-24, universities began codifying AI misuse explicitly in academic integrity policies. The Yang case (2024-2026) represents one of the first cases to be litigated through full federal and state appellate processes.

### Bằng chứng thực tế

**Tên trường hợp:**

> Haishan Yang v. University of Minnesota et al. — PhD student (Health Services Research, Policy, and Administration program) expelled August 2024 after eight-hour open-book qualifying exam; lawsuits filed January 2025; federal dismissal October 2025; state appellate court upheld expulsion February 2026.

**Diễn biến:**

> In August 2024, Haishan Yang took an eight-hour open-book qualifying exam for the Health Services Research, Policy, and Administration PhD program. The exam explicitly prohibited AI use. A faculty grading committee led by Dr. Hannah Neprash found Yang's answers inconsistent with his prior writing, containing ideas not covered in class, and matched near-identically with ChatGPT outputs — including exact phrasing. The case was referred to UMN's Office for Community Standards. Yang submitted a 35-page written defense and participated in a formal hearing before the Campus Committee on Student Behavior with an advocate. The panel unanimously found him responsible. Yang then filed state and federal lawsuits in January 2025, alleging Dr. Neprash had altered ChatGPT outputs to match his answers and that due process was violated. He sought $4 million in damages.

**Kết quả xử lý:**

> Outcome: Expelled from University of Minnesota (August 2024). Federal lawsuit dismissed October 2025 — court found due process rights were not violated. State appellate court upheld expulsion February 2026. Yang's $4 million lawsuit failed at both federal and state appellate levels. Case establishes precedent that universities have broad discretion in AI-cheating disciplinary proceedings.

**Con số thống kê:**

> 1 student expelled; $4 million lawsuit sought; federal dismissal October 2025; appellate decision February 2026. UMN context: AI cases comprised ~50% of all confirmed academic dishonesty on Twin Cities campus in 2023-24.

### Tác hại & Hậu quả

**Hại cho người học:**

> Yang's case illustrates catastrophic personal cost: loss of doctoral degree trajectory, years of academic work voided, reputational damage, and high legal costs. Even if innocent, AI detection ambiguity creates permanent career disruption. Dependency on AI in exam contexts prevents genuine skill development in research methodology.

**Hại cho nghiên cứu:**

> Use of AI in qualifying exams undermines the quality-gatekeeping function of doctoral programs. If AI-generated knowledge passes credentialing hurdles, PhD graduates may lack authentic expertise required for original research, weakening the scientific workforce.

**Hại cho xã hội:**

> High-profile cases create chilling effects: legitimate AI tool users self-censor; students distrust professors; trust in credentialing erodes. Sets adversarial dynamic between students and institutions on AI use.

### Phát hiện & Công cụ

**Công cụ phát hiện:**

> Detection in Yang's case was human-initiated: faculty compared student responses with ChatGPT outputs manually, identifying near-identical phrasing and ideas not covered in coursework. No automated AI detector (Turnitin, GPTZero) was cited as primary evidence. Human expert comparison remains the most legally defensible detection method.

**Hạn chế công cụ:**

> The Yang case highlights the risk of false accusations based on stylistic similarity. Yang claimed faculty may have biased the comparison by editing ChatGPT outputs. Automated tools (Turnitin AI, GPTZero) face additional reliability challenges: Liang et al. (Stanford, 2023) found 61.3% false positive rate on TOEFL essays; Turnitin reports 4% sentence-level FP rate. Human expert comparison is more defensible but still contested.

**LLM Watermarking:**

> LLM watermarking (Kirchenbauer et al., UMD; Aaronson/OpenAI) would allow cryptographic embedding of AI-origin signals in generated text, potentially providing stronger forensic evidence in cases like Yang's. However, as of 2024-2025, no production watermarking was deployed in OpenAI's ChatGPT, making forensic proof reliant on textual similarity analysis.

### Phòng ngừa & Giải pháp

**Thiết kế bài tập:**

> Yang's case suggests that oral defense and in-person examination components should complement open-book written exams to reduce AI substitution risk. Staged submissions and process documentation (drafts, notes) can provide corroborating evidence of authentic student work.

**Chính sách trường học:**

> UMN policy: explicit AI prohibition in the qualifying exam instructions. The university's Office for Community Standards handles investigations with formal hearing processes. Post-case, UMN's policy remained consistent — AI use in prohibited contexts constitutes scholastic dishonesty equivalent to plagiarism.

**Văn hóa tổ chức:**

> The UMN case reflects an enforcement-first culture. No disclosure framework or graduated response — direct referral to OCS upon suspected violation. McCabe's research suggests honor-code cultures with education and dialogue reduce violations more effectively than enforcement-only models.

**AI Literacy:**

> Yang's case underscores the need for explicit AI literacy education at doctoral level: clear communication of what AI use is/is not permitted, training on the risks of AI reliance, and understanding of detection methods and their limitations.

### Khung pháp lý & Đạo đức

**Khung quốc tế:**

> UNESCO 2023 guidelines on AI in education recognize the need for transparent policies. EU AI Act 2024 (Art. 50) requires disclosure when AI-generated content is produced. IEEE EAD and ACM Code of Ethics both require honest attribution of AI contributions in academic work.

**Pháp luật Việt Nam:**

> Nghị định 109/2022/NĐ-CP Điều 20 prohibits fabrication and fraud in scientific research. However, Vietnamese regulations do not explicitly address AI-generated exam submissions — a legal gap mirroring international norms in 2024-2025.

**Chính sách châu Á:**

> CUHK (2023) declared explicit expulsion-level AI policy. NUS (2023/2024) adopted a disclosure framework. The UMN Yang case, though US-based, informs Asian university policy discussions on proportional sanctions and due process requirements.

### Góc nhìn EdTech

**AI tutor vs AI cheater:**

> Yang's case typifies the AI-as-cheater framing. The exam context — doctoral qualifying, open-book but AI-prohibited — represents a legitimate boundary where AI substitutes for the cognitive performance the credential certifies. Rose Luckin (UCL) would distinguish between AI as scaffolding for learning versus AI as replacement for assessed performance.

**Chính sách EdTech companies:**

> OpenAI's ChatGPT was the alleged tool used. OpenAI's terms of service prohibit use in ways that violate academic policies, but enforcement is left entirely to institutions. EdTech platforms like Coursera and Khan Academy impose their own AI-use rules per assessment type.

**Đề xuất nhà nghiên cứu EdTech:**

> From an EdTech research perspective: (1) qualifying exams should require in-person oral defense components; (2) AI policy must be explicit in all exam instructions; (3) detection should rely on multi-modal evidence (process artifacts, oral follow-up), not text similarity alone; (4) institutions should establish graduated sanctions rather than immediate expulsion for first-time ambiguous cases.

### Nguồn tài liệu

**Bài báo khoa học:**

> Liang, W., et al. (2023). GPT detectors are biased against non-native English writers. Patterns (Cell Press). DOI: 10.1016/j.patter.2023.100779. Perkins, M., et al. (2023). Academic Integrity Considerations of AI Large Language Models in the Post-ChatGPT Era. Journal of University Teaching & Learning Practice.

**Sách tham khảo:**

> McCabe, D.L., Butterfield, K.D., & Treviño, L.K. (2012). Cheating in College. Johns Hopkins University Press. Eaton, S.E. (2021). Plagiarism in Higher Education. ABC-CLIO.

**Nguồn Việt Nam:**

> No direct Vietnamese source for this specific case. Broader context: Nghị định 109/2022/NĐ-CP on scientific research integrity; QĐ 1705/QĐ-TTg on education strategy to 2030.

### Uncertain Fields

- con_so_thong_ke (exact figure of $4M lawsuit amount confirmed; 188-student UMN figure is system-wide, not specific to Yang case)
