## ICAI — 6 Core Values of Academic Integrity {#icai-6-core-values-of-academic-integrity}

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Type | framework |
| Organization | International Center for Academic Integrity |
| Url | https://www.academicintegrity.org/ |

### Định nghĩa & Khái niệm

**Định nghĩa:**

> The ICAI defines academic integrity as 'a commitment to six fundamental values: honesty, trust, fairness, respect, responsibility, and courage.' Each value has operational meaning: Honesty (represent work and findings accurately), Trust (create environments where trust can flourish), Fairness (fair standards and practices), Respect (respect self and others), Responsibility (uphold values personally and institutionally), Courage (act on values even under pressure). The framework is the most widely cited operational definition of academic integrity globally.

**Phân loại vi phạm:**

> The 6 values constitute a normative framework — not a violation taxonomy. Violations are classified by which values they breach: plagiarism breaches Honesty; accepting others' help without disclosure breaches Trust; grade fraud breaches Fairness; disrespecting peers' work breaches Respect; failing to report known violations breaches Responsibility; staying silent when witnessing cheating out of peer pressure breaches Courage.

**Lịch sử khái niệm:**

> ICAI founded 1992 by Donald McCabe (Rutgers) and Gary Pavela at a meeting of 7 universities. First statement of fundamental values published 1999 with 5 values (Honesty, Trust, Fairness, Respect, Responsibility). Courage added in 2014 edition — explicitly to address peer pressure and bystander effects. Most recent edition: 2021. The 2021 edition explicitly addresses AI, GenAI, and postplagiarism conditions.

### Bằng chứng thực tế

**Tên trường hợp:**

> ICAI framework is referenced in virtually every academic integrity policy globally. Specific institutional applications: University of Maryland honor code, University of Sydney AI integrity policy (2023), NUS Academic Honesty Policy, CUHK Plagiarism Policy.

**Diễn biến:**

> The 6-value framework operates as: (1) a normative foundation for institutional honor codes; (2) a pedagogical tool for teaching academic integrity to students; (3) a benchmarking standard for institutional policy audit. ICAI produces the 'Fundamental Values of Academic Integrity' document (freely available, translations in 12+ languages). ICAI also administers an annual 'Donald L. McCabe Award' and publishes the journal 'Academic Integrity.'

**Kết quả xử lý:**

> The framework has been adopted explicitly by 500+ member institutions globally (as of 2024). It informs UNESCO guidance (2023), EU AI Act commentary, and national higher education policies in Australia, Canada, UK, and USA. McCabe's 2012 empirical work validates that institutions that operationalize these values — especially through honor codes — demonstrate significantly lower cheating rates.

### Tác hại & Hậu quả

**Hại cho người học:**

> When institutions fail to operationalize the 6 values, students receive mixed signals about what integrity means in practice. The 'Courage' value specifically addresses peer pressure — students need institutional support to maintain integrity when peers normalize cheating.

**Hại cho nghiên cứu:**

> The values apply to research integrity: Honesty (accurate reporting), Trust (transparent methods), Fairness (peer review rigor), Responsibility (retraction when errors found), Courage (reporting misconduct in one's own lab or field).

**Hại cho xã hội:**

> The 6-value framework provides a universal ethical foundation that transcends specific technology or violation types. As AI creates new forms of academic dishonesty, the question becomes how these 6 values apply — not whether they apply.

### Phát hiện & Công cụ

**Công cụ phát hiện:**

> The ICAI framework does not prescribe specific detection tools. However, the 'Fairness' value implies that detection tools must not produce disparate impact — directly relevant to Liang et al.'s findings on AI detector ESL bias.

**Hạn chế công cụ:**

> ICAI's position (aligned with McCabe's research): detection tools are a supplement to, not a substitute for, a values-based culture. The Courage value specifically addresses why detection-only approaches fail — students need to internalize values, not just fear punishment.

**LLM Watermarking:**

> Not directly addressed by ICAI, but the Honesty value (accurately representing one's work) and Responsibility value (taking ownership of work) are precisely the values violated by submitting AI-generated work without disclosure — which watermarking could help enforce.

### Phòng ngừa & Giải pháp

**Thiết kế bài tập:**

> ICAI's pedagogical materials include classroom assessment integrity design guides. The Fairness value requires that assessments give all students an equitable opportunity to demonstrate genuine learning — which AI-vulnerable assessments fail to provide.

**Chính sách trường học:**

> The ICAI framework is the primary reference for honor code design. Stanford, Princeton, and MIT honor codes directly cite or implement the 6-value framework. NUS and NTU honor codes align structurally with ICAI values.

**Văn hóa tổ chức:**

> The core insight of the ICAI framework — confirmed by McCabe's empirical research — is that values internalization outperforms enforcement. Institutions that build a culture of Honesty, Trust, and Responsibility see lower violation rates than those that rely on Turnitin and punitive codes.

**AI Literacy:**

> The 2021 ICAI edition explicitly addresses AI literacy as a component of academic integrity education. Students need to understand what AI tools do, when using them is honest and responsible, and when it constitutes misrepresentation — all framed through the 6 values.

### Khung pháp lý & Đạo đức

**Khung quốc tế:**

> ICAI values are the de facto international standard. UNESCO 2023 GenAI guidance aligns its recommendations with the ICAI framework. EU AI Act Article 50 (disclosure obligation) operationalizes the ICAI Honesty value. ACM Code of Ethics Section 1.3 ('Be Honest and Trustworthy') mirrors ICAI principles.

**Pháp luật Việt Nam:**

> Vietnam's Decree 109/2022 Điều 20 mandates institutional integrity codes but does not specify a value framework. ICAI's 6 values could serve as the normative foundation Vietnam lacks — especially Courage (acting on values under institutional pressure) and Responsibility (shared institutional obligation).

**Chính sách châu Á:**

> CUHK, NUS, and NTU honor systems align with ICAI values. The 2023 Asian HE AI integrity cases (CUHK expulsions, NUS disclosure framework) can be analyzed through all 6 ICAI values — Honesty (disclosure), Fairness (equitable enforcement), Courage (faculty reporting).

### Góc nhìn EdTech

**AI tutor vs AI cheater:**

> The 6-value framework provides a principled resolution: AI use is honest and responsible when disclosed and when the student genuinely engages with the learning; it is dishonest when it misrepresents authorship or bypasses genuine intellectual work. This is a values-based answer to the tutor/cheater dichotomy.

**Chính sách EdTech companies:**

> Khan Academy Khanmigo explicitly embodies the Trust and Honesty values by refusing to complete work for students. Coursera's post-submission dialogue embodies Responsibility. Duolingo's exam proctoring enforces Fairness. All can be analyzed through the ICAI framework.

**Đề xuất nhà nghiên cứu EdTech:**

> The ICAI 6-value framework is the recommended normative foundation for any EdTech academic integrity policy: (1) Anchor AI use policies in the 6 values (not just rules); (2) Use the Courage value to address normalization of AI fraud; (3) Apply Fairness to require bias audits of detection tools; (4) Use Responsibility to establish shared institutional/student/faculty ownership; (5) The 2021 ICAI update directly addresses GenAI — use it as the foundational document for policy development.

### Nguồn tài liệu

**Bài báo khoa học:**

> McCabe, D.L. & Pavela, G. (2004) 'Ten principles of academic integrity' Change: The Magazine of Higher Learning. ICAI (2021) 'The Fundamental Values of Academic Integrity, 3rd ed.' Clemson, SC. McCabe, D.L. (1993) 'Academic dishonesty among male college students' Journal of College Student Development.

**Sách tham khảo:**

> ICAI (2021) Fundamental Values of Academic Integrity, 3rd ed. McCabe et al. (2012) Cheating in College (Johns Hopkins UP). Bretag (2016) Handbook of Academic Integrity. Eaton (2024) Second Handbook of Academic Integrity.

## AIAS — AI Assessment Scale (Perkins, BUV) {#aias-ai-assessment-scale-perkins-buv}

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Type | framework |
| Institution | British University Vietnam |
| Author | Mike Perkins |
| Institution | British University Vietnam |

### Định nghĩa & Khái niệm

**Định nghĩa:**

> The AI Assessment Scale (AIAS) is a five-tier framework developed by Mike Perkins at British University Vietnam (BUV) to help educators design assignments and communicate clear AI use expectations to students. Rather than treating AI use as a binary permitted/prohibited decision, AIAS provides a gradient that allows educators to calibrate the role of AI in each specific assessment context. The scale was designed to be applicable across disciplines and education levels, from secondary school to higher education.

**Phân loại vi phạm:**

> AIAS defines five tiers of permitted AI engagement: Tier 1 — No AI use (traditional closed assessments, creative work where human authorship is the learning objective); Tier 2 — AI for research/information gathering only (AI cannot be used to generate or edit written output); Tier 3 — AI-assisted writing with mandatory disclosure (AI tools used in drafting must be declared in an AI use statement); Tier 4 — AI use encouraged as part of task completion (AI integration is expected, but human critical engagement with AI output is assessed); Tier 5 — AI co-creation is the assignment (the assessment itself involves designing, prompting, and critically evaluating AI output). Violations occur when students use AI at a higher tier than the assessment specifies.

**Lịch sử khái niệm:**

> The AIAS framework was developed by Mike Perkins and collaborators at British University Vietnam and published in 2023, directly responding to the post-ChatGPT policy crisis in higher education. It was formally published in the Journal of University Teaching and Learning Practice (JUTLP) in 2023. BUV, operating in Vietnam's rapidly evolving EdTech context, was an early adopter because Vietnamese universities faced acute AI integrity challenges without adequate regulatory guidance.

### Bằng chứng thực tế

**Tên trường hợp:**

> The AIAS framework has been adopted or cited by institutions across Southeast Asia, the UK, and Australia. Key adoption cases: British University Vietnam (institutional policy, 2023); Bond University Australia (referenced in AI policy development); multiple UK higher education institutions following QAA guidance on AI. Perkins and colleagues also conducted empirical research on student AI use at BUV that informed the scale.

**Diễn biến:**

> Perkins developed the AIAS in response to a clear institutional need: educators at BUV observed diverse AI use among students but lacked a shared vocabulary to communicate expectations. The 5-tier scale was piloted at BUV in 2023. Faculty reported that having a numbered scale allowed clearer assignment briefings ('This is a Tier 2 assessment') and reduced ambiguity. The framework was subsequently published and promoted through international academic integrity conferences and networks including ENAI (European Network for Academic Integrity).

**Kết quả xử lý:**

> The AIAS framework has been adopted as a policy tool at multiple institutions. Perkins et al. (2023) published empirical findings on its use in JUTLP. The scale was recognized by UNESCO 2023 guidance on GenAI in education as a practical implementation tool. It is referenced in Eaton's Second Handbook of Academic Integrity (2024). BUV's approach has been held up as a model for Vietnamese universities developing AI policies.

**Con số thống kê:**

> 5 tiers; developed 2023; published in JUTLP Vol.20 No.2 (2023); institutional pilot at BUV (British University Vietnam); cited in UNESCO 2023 GenAI guidance [uncertain: exact citation count]; applicable across K-12 and higher education. Perkins is based at BUV — one of the few Vietnam-based contributions to the international academic integrity literature.

### Tác hại & Hậu quả

**Hại cho người học:**

> AIAS directly addresses harm prevention for learners: by clearly specifying the tier of each assessment, students understand what cognitive work is genuinely required of them. Tier 1 and 2 assessments protect essential skill development (critical thinking, original writing) from AI displacement. Tier 4-5 assessments ensure students develop AI collaboration skills needed in professional contexts. Without a framework like AIAS, students receive contradictory signals across courses, creating confusion and incentivizing over-reliance on AI.

**Hại cho nghiên cứu:**

> For research contexts, AIAS Tier 2 (AI for research/information gathering) specifically addresses AI hallucination risks: students who use AI only for research identification (not writing) can cross-check sources, reducing fabricated citation problems. Tier 3-5 require disclosure of AI tool use, creating a record that supports research reproducibility and attribution standards.

**Hại cho xã hội:**

> AIAS provides a scalable policy framework that can maintain the value of academic credentials: Tier 1-2 assessments ensure graduates possess demonstrable skills; Tier 4-5 assessments ensure graduates possess AI collaboration competencies needed in the modern workforce. This dual assurance supports credential legitimacy in an AI-saturated economy.

### Phát hiện & Công cụ

**Công cụ phát hiện:**

> AIAS shifts emphasis from detection to upfront policy design. For Tier 1 assessments (AI prohibited), traditional detection tools (Turnitin, GPTZero) remain relevant. For Tier 3, disclosure statements reduce detection burden — the student self-declares AI use. For Tier 4-5, detection is irrelevant because AI use is expected. The framework thus makes detection tools selectively relevant rather than universally applied.

**Hạn chế công cụ:**

> AIAS implicitly acknowledges detection tool limitations by providing an alternative governance structure. False positives from AI detectors (Liang et al., 2023: 61.3% for ESL writers) are most damaging in Tier 1-2 contexts. By explicitly marking which assessments are Tier 1-2, institutions can focus detection resources where they matter and avoid false accusations in Tier 3-5 contexts.

**LLM Watermarking:**

> LLM watermarking would be most useful for AIAS Tier 1-2 enforcement — where AI use is prohibited or limited. Watermarks could help identify Tier 1 violations. For Tier 3-5, watermarking is less relevant since AI use is permitted or expected. The AIAS framework provides the policy context within which watermarking technology could be selectively deployed.

### Phòng ngừa & Giải pháp

**Thiết kế bài tập:**

> AIAS is fundamentally an assessment design tool. Practical implications: Tier 1 — closed exams, timed in-class writing, oral presentations; Tier 2 — annotated bibliographies where AI identified sources but student wrote; Tier 3 — essays with mandatory AI disclosure statement and process log; Tier 4 — comparative analysis of AI and human perspectives on a topic; Tier 5 — design a prompt, critique AI output, reflect on AI limitations. Faculty using AIAS report clearer learning objectives and more defensible grading.

**Chính sách trường học:**

> AIAS has been adopted as an institutional policy framework at BUV and referenced by multiple universities developing AI policies. Stanford's AI policy (contextual use by course) and NUS's course-level disclosure framework both reflect AIAS-like thinking even without explicitly citing it. Eaton's postplagiarism framework provides the theoretical rationale; AIAS provides the operational mechanism. The Australian Government's 2023 AI in education guidance referenced tiered approaches consistent with AIAS.

**Văn hóa tổ chức:**

> AIAS supports a culture of clarity over a culture of prohibition. By normalizing AI use in Tier 3-5 contexts, it removes the incentive for covert use and reduces the adversarial dynamic between students and institutions. This aligns with McCabe's honor culture research: transparent expectations with clear rationales produce better integrity outcomes than blanket bans with unpredictable enforcement.

**AI Literacy:**

> AIAS inherently promotes AI literacy by requiring students to understand what each tier means and why different assessments have different tiers. Faculty who implement AIAS must articulate the learning objectives that AI use would undermine (Tiers 1-2) or enhance (Tiers 4-5) — forcing explicit pedagogical reasoning about AI's role in education. This is directly aligned with EU AI Act Article 4 (mandatory AI literacy for providers/users) and UNESCO 2023 AI literacy recommendations.

### Khung pháp lý & Đạo đức

**Khung quốc tế:**

> AIAS is consistent with UNESCO 2023 guidance (selective regulation, not blanket prohibition), EU AI Act Article 50 (disclosure of AI-generated content maps to Tier 3 disclosure requirement), and ICAI's 6 values (Honesty via disclosure, Fairness via consistent tiered standards, Responsibility via student ownership of AI use). The framework bridges policy principles and classroom practice.

**Pháp luật Việt Nam:**

> Vietnam's Decree 109/2022 lacks operational assessment-level guidance for AI use. AIAS is directly applicable to Vietnamese higher education and has been developed at a Vietnam-based institution (BUV). Vietnamese universities developing AI policies could adopt AIAS as a ready-made operational framework. The MOET (Ministry of Education and Training) has not yet issued specific AI assessment guidance as of early 2026 [uncertain: status of any MOET AI guidance].

**Chính sách châu Á:**

> BUV (Vietnam) developed AIAS; NUS Singapore developed a disclosure framework that mirrors Tier 3; CUHK's 2023 AI prohibition corresponds to Tier 1; NTU's zero-marks policy for hallucinated citations enforces what would be a Tier 2 violation. AIAS provides a common vocabulary for Asian institutions that are converging on similar tiered approaches from different starting points.

### Góc nhìn EdTech

**AI tutor vs AI cheater:**

> AIAS resolves the tutor/cheater dichotomy contextually rather than categorically: AI is a legitimate tutor in Tier 4-5 assessments and a cheating tool in Tier 1-2 assessments. The determination is made by the educator at the assignment design stage based on learning objectives, not by a universal rule. This contextual approach is more defensible than either blanket prohibition or blanket permission.

**Chính sách EdTech companies:**

> Khan Academy Khanmigo (Socratic, refuses essays) aligns with Tier 1-2 contexts; Coursera's post-submission dialogue aligns with Tier 3 (declare AI use after submission); tools like ChatGPT Education or Copilot used for drafting align with Tier 3-4 use cases. Duolingo's AI integration in learning but banning in exams implements a Tier 1/Tier 4 split within a single platform. EdTech companies could use AIAS tiering to label their tools by appropriate assessment context.

**Đề xuất nhà nghiên cứu EdTech:**

> From an EdTech researcher perspective: (1) AIAS is the most operationally practical framework for Vietnamese universities to adopt — it was developed at a Vietnam-based institution and requires no legal change; (2) Recommend piloting AIAS at 5-10 Vietnamese universities with structured evaluation; (3) Integrate AIAS into faculty development programs for MOET; (4) Map AIAS tiers to specific assessment types in Vietnamese higher education (giữa kỳ, cuối kỳ, báo cáo, đồ án); (5) Study whether BUV's AIAS implementation has measurably affected AI violation rates — this empirical evidence would support wider adoption.

### Nguồn tài liệu

**Bài báo khoa học:**

> Perkins, M., Roe, J., Postma, D., McGaughran, J., & Hickerson, D. (2023). Academic Integrity Considerations of AI Large Language Models in the Post-Pandemic Era: ChatGPT and Beyond. Journal of University Teaching & Learning Practice, 20(2). DOI: 10.53761/1.20.02.07. Perkins, M. (2023). Academic Integrity Considerations of AI Large Language Models. IJEI. Nguyen, A. et al. (2023). Ethical Principles for Artificial Intelligence in Education. Education and Information Technologies.

**Sách tham khảo:**

> Eaton, S.E. (2024). Second Handbook of Academic Integrity. Springer. Bretag, T. (2016). Handbook of Academic Integrity. Springer. ICAI (2021). Fundamental Values of Academic Integrity, 3rd ed. UNESCO (2023). Guidance for Generative AI in Education and Research.

## ACM Code of Ethics and Professional Conduct (2018) {#acm-code-of-ethics-and-professional-conduct-2018}

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Type | policy |
| Year | 2018 |
| Organization | Association for Computing Machinery |
| Url | https://www.acm.org/code-of-ethics |

### Định nghĩa & Khái niệm

**Định nghĩa:**

> The ACM Code of Ethics and Professional Conduct (2018 revision) is the primary professional ethics standard for computing professionals worldwide. Published by the Association for Computing Machinery — the oldest and largest computing professional society (founded 1947, 100,000+ members) — the Code establishes ethical obligations for all ACM members and, by widely accepted professional norm, for computing practitioners globally. The 2018 revision updated the 1992 Code to address contemporary computing issues including algorithmic systems, privacy, and AI. The Code is structured in three tiers: General Ethical Principles (Section 1), Professional Responsibilities (Section 2), and Leadership Principles (Section 3).

**Phân loại vi phạm:**

> The ACM Code does not classify violations taxonomically but establishes obligations violated by different forms of academic AI fraud: (1) AI fraud violates 1.3 (Honesty and Trustworthiness) — submitting AI-generated work as one's own is a misrepresentation; (2) Creating AI tools designed for academic fraud violates 1.2 (Avoid Harm) — such tools harm educational institutions and fellow students; (3) Building AI detection systems with known false positive biases violates 2.5 (Thorough Evaluations) — inadequate testing that harms users; (4) Deploying insufficiently secured AI in educational assessments violates 2.9 (Robustness and Security); (5) Hallucinated citations in AI-generated research violate 1.3 (scientific honesty).

**Lịch sử khái niệm:**

> The ACM Code was originally adopted in 1972, comprehensively revised in 1992, and last updated in 2018. The 2018 revision was led by a task force chaired by Don Gotterbarn and involved three years of development and public consultation. Key changes from 1992 to 2018: (1) Strengthened algorithmic system responsibilities (explicitly addressing automated decision-making); (2) Added explicit prohibition on using computing skills to circumvent security; (3) Strengthened diversity and inclusion language; (4) Added 2.9 (Design Robustly and Securely) as a new professional responsibility. The 2018 revision predates generative AI but its principles directly address all AI-era integrity challenges.

### Bằng chứng thực tế

**Tên trường hợp:**

> The ACM Code is cited in academic integrity cases involving computing technologies: (1) Software plagiarism detection systems — developers of MOSS (Stanford) and Turnitin cite ACM code obligations for system accuracy; (2) AI exam proctoring systems — companies like Proctorio and ExamSoft are subject to ACM professional obligations of their computing staff; (3) AI writing tool developers — ACM members working at OpenAI, Anthropic, Google face Code obligations regarding AI systems that facilitate academic fraud; (4) EdTech companies — computing professionals in EdTech are bound by ACM Code obligations regarding the accuracy and fairness of their systems.

**Diễn biến:**

> The Code's practical application to AI academic integrity: Section 1.3 (Honesty) is most directly applicable — it states 'Computing professionals should be honest about their qualifications, and about any limitations and potential problems with their work.' For AI tool developers, this means being honest about hallucination rates, false positive rates, and known limitations. For computing researchers, it means being honest about AI assistance in their publications. Section 2.5 (Thorough Evaluations) requires developers of AI systems used in high-stakes decisions to conduct comprehensive evaluation including bias testing.

**Kết quả xử lý:**

> The ACM Code provides the professional ethics foundation for computing practitioners involved in EdTech. While the Code's enforcement is primarily through professional reputation rather than legal sanctions, violation of the Code can result in ACM membership revocation and professional censure. The Code is widely cited in: academic integrity policy documents at computing departments; EdTech company codes of conduct; AI research publication guidelines (including ACM Digital Library submission requirements requiring disclosure of AI assistance in paper preparation since 2023).

### Tác hại & Hậu quả

**Hại cho người học:**

> Section 1.2 (Avoid Harm) applies to EdTech developers: computing professionals who build tools that enable academic fraud cause measurable harm to learners who are displaced by fraudulently credentialed peers in competitive programs and employment. Section 2.5 requires that educational AI systems be comprehensively evaluated — including evaluation of whether they genuinely support learning or merely enable credential acquisition without learning. The Code frames student harm not as abstract but as a professional responsibility of computing practitioners.

**Hại cho nghiên cứu:**

> Section 1.3's honesty requirement directly applies to AI-assisted research: ACM members who publish research must honestly disclose AI assistance, honestly represent AI-generated content, and honestly characterize AI-generated data or analyses. The 2023 ACM Digital Library requirement for AI disclosure in paper preparation directly implements Section 1.3. For ACM members building AI research tools, Section 2.5 requires thorough evaluation of accuracy — directly addressing hallucinated citations from AI literature review tools.

**Hại cho xã hội:**

> Section 1.2 frames harm avoidance in terms of societal impact: computing professionals who develop AI academic fraud tools cause harm not only to immediate academic integrity but to the broader social contract that academic credentials represent. The Code's societal harm framing is broader than institutional harm — it includes harm to the social value of education as a meritocratic mechanism and harm to public trust in institutions.

### Phát hiện & Công cụ

**Công cụ phát hiện:**

> Sections 2.5 and 2.9 apply directly to AI detection tool developers: (1) 2.5 requires comprehensive and thorough evaluations of AI detectors — including bias testing across demographic groups (directly addressing the Liang et al., 2023 ESL false positive finding); (2) 2.9 requires robust and secure design — an AI detector that can be easily fooled by text humanizers is not robustly designed; (3) 1.3 requires honesty about detector limitations — vendors who market tools without disclosing accuracy limitations violate the honesty principle. The ACM Code provides the professional ethics basis for demanding higher standards from AI detection tool vendors.

**Hạn chế công cụ:**

> The ACM Code's professional responsibility framework holds computing practitioners personally responsible for the systems they build and deploy. For AI detection tools with known ESL bias (Liang et al., 2023): (1) The original developers who failed to test for ESL bias may have violated 2.5 (thorough evaluations); (2) The companies that marketed tools with undisclosed false positive rates may have violated 1.3 (honesty); (3) Institutional IT staff who deployed biased tools without adequate evaluation may have violated 2.5 and 1.2 (avoid harm). The Code creates a professional accountability chain for detection tool bias.

**LLM Watermarking:**

> Section 2.9 (Design Robustly and Securely) applies to LLM watermarking: computing professionals designing watermarking systems must ensure they are robust against stripping attacks, secure against manipulation, and do not create new privacy risks. Section 1.3 (Honesty) applies to watermarking's purpose: if watermarking is represented as reliable proof of AI generation but is easily defeated, that representation is dishonest. The Code requires watermarking researchers to accurately characterize the limitations of current watermarking approaches.

### Phòng ngừa & Giải pháp

**Thiết kế bài tập:**

> The ACM Code does not directly address assessment design, but Section 1.5 (Respect Privacy) applies to AI-based assessment monitoring systems used to detect cheating: comprehensive surveillance of student behavior during exams may violate students' reasonable privacy expectations. Section 2.5 applies to computing professionals who build or evaluate assessment AI systems — they must comprehensively assess whether the system accurately measures what it claims to measure and whether it introduces bias.

**Chính sách trường học:**

> Computing departments at universities have the strongest professional culture connection to ACM Code obligations. CS/IT faculty who are ACM members have professional obligations regarding: (1) Honesty in their own research and teaching (1.3); (2) Avoiding harm in computing instruction (1.2); (3) Thorough evaluation of computing tools deployed in courses (2.5). The ACM Code provides a professional ethics dimension that complements institutional academic integrity policies — computing faculty and students have dual obligations under both.

**Văn hóa tổ chức:**

> The ACM Code is explicitly a professional values document, not just a compliance checklist — it is designed to inculcate professional culture in computing practitioners from their earliest training. Computer science education programs use the Code to socialize students into professional ethics norms. This culture-building function parallels McCabe's honor culture approach to academic integrity: the goal is professional identity formation, not just rule compliance.

**AI Literacy:**

> The ACM Code's professional development obligations (Section 2.6: Perform Work Only in Areas of Competence) imply that computing professionals must maintain competency in AI ethics as AI becomes central to their work. For EdTech practitioners specifically, competency in AI ethics includes understanding AI limitations, bias risks, and integrity implications — the components of AI literacy. The Code requires that computing professionals continually update their understanding of the systems they build, which in 2024-2026 means understanding generative AI characteristics including hallucination and bias.

### Khung pháp lý & Đạo đức

**Khung quốc tế:**

> The ACM Code is the primary professional ethics standard for computing — distinct from but complementary to UNESCO 2021 (government), EU AI Act 2024 (law), and IEEE EAD 2019 (engineering standards). ACM Code Section 1.3 (Honesty) implements the same value as UNESCO Honesty/Transparency principles and ICAI Honesty value in a professional context. The Code's Section 1.2 (Avoid Harm) parallels UNESCO's 'Do No Harm' principle and IEEE's 'Human Well-being' principle. ACM and IEEE Codes together cover the professional obligations of computing practitioners; UNESCO and EU Act cover governmental obligations.

**Pháp luật Việt Nam:**

> ACM membership and Code obligations apply to Vietnamese computing professionals who are ACM members. Vietnam has a growing ACM membership through universities like HCMUS, VNU, HUST, and FPT University. Vietnamese computing faculty and researchers who are ACM members have professional obligations under the Code — including honesty in AI-assisted research. Vietnam's domestic computing professional organizations (VAIP — Vietnam Association for Information Processing) have their own codes of conduct but have not formally aligned with ACM Code [uncertain: exact VAIP code content].

**Chính sách châu Á:**

> ACM has 30+ chapters across Asia including active chapters in Vietnam (HCMUS ACM Student Chapter, VNU ACM Chapter), Singapore (NUS ACM Chapter), South Korea, Japan, and India. ACM Code obligations apply to all chapter members. ACM's Asia computing education conferences (SIGCSE Asia-Pacific) have addressed AI academic integrity. The NUS School of Computing requires CS students to understand ACM Code obligations as part of the professional ethics curriculum — integrating Code compliance with academic integrity education.

### Góc nhìn EdTech

**AI tutor vs AI cheater:**

> The ACM Code's honesty principle provides a professional ethics resolution: AI functions as a legitimate tutor when its use is disclosed (Section 1.3) and the student retains genuine cognitive responsibility; AI functions as a cheating tool when students use it to misrepresent their capabilities. For computing professionals building AI tools, Section 1.2 creates a design obligation: do not build tools whose primary use case is enabling academic fraud. The tutor/cheater distinction maps to the ACM professional obligation to consider 'all reasonably foreseeable uses' of a system.

**Chính sách EdTech companies:**

> Computing professionals employed by EdTech companies are bound by ACM Code obligations in their professional work: (1) Khan Academy developers — Section 1.2 obligations to avoid harm in educational AI design; Khanmigo's Socratic design reflects Code-consistent harm avoidance; (2) Turnitin developers — Section 2.5 obligations to evaluate AI detector accuracy including bias testing; (3) OpenAI/Anthropic engineers — Section 1.2 obligations regarding AI systems that enable academic fraud; (4) Duolingo engineers — Section 2.9 obligations for secure, robust AI design in educational assessments. The ACM Code applies to individual practitioners, not just companies.

**Đề xuất nhà nghiên cứu EdTech:**

> Applying ACM Code to EdTech AI integrity research: (1) Use Sections 1.3 and 2.5 to create an ethical audit framework for AI detection tools deployed in Vietnamese universities — requiring vendors to document accuracy, bias characteristics, and limitations; (2) Propose that Vietnamese computing professional organizations (VAIP) adopt ACM Code-aligned standards for EdTech AI practitioners; (3) Design AI literacy curricula for computing students that explicitly incorporate ACM Code ethics alongside technical AI education; (4) Use Section 1.2 (Avoid Harm) to argue for computing practitioners' professional responsibility in the AI academic fraud ecosystem — developers, deployers, and users all share ethical obligations; (5) Recommend that HCMUS and VNU computing programs include ACM Code analysis of AI integrity cases as a mandatory component of professional ethics courses.

### Nguồn tài liệu

**Bài báo khoa học:**

> ACM (2018). Code of Ethics and Professional Conduct. https://www.acm.org/code-of-ethics. Gotterbarn, D. et al. (2018). ACM Code of Ethics: A Guide for Positive Action. Communications of the ACM, 61(1), 121-128. Fiesler, C. et al. (2020). What Do We Teach When We Teach Tech Ethics? SIGCSE 2020 Proceedings.

**Sách tham khảo:**

> ACM (2018). Code of Ethics and Professional Conduct (full text and case studies). Tavani, H.T. (2015). Ethics and Technology: Controversies, Questions, and Strategies for Ethical Computing, 5th ed. Wiley. Quinn, M.J. (2020). Ethics for the Information Age, 8th ed. Pearson.

**Nguồn Việt Nam:**

> ACM Student Chapters exist at HCMUS and VNU — chapter members are subject to ACM Code obligations. Vietnamese computing education programs at HCMUS, VNU-HCMUT, and HUST include professional ethics modules that may reference ACM Code [uncertain: which programs explicitly teach ACM Code]. The ACM Code is available in Vietnamese translation [uncertain: whether official Vietnamese translation exists]. VAIP (Vietnam Association for Information Processing) is the domestic professional body but its code of conduct relationship to ACM Code has not been formally documented.

## EU AI Act (Regulation 2024/1689) {#eu-ai-act-regulation-20241689}

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Type | policy |
| Year | 2024 |
| Organization | European Union |
| Url | https://eur-lex.europa.eu/legal-content/EN/TXT/?uri=OJ:L_202401689 |

### Định nghĩa & Khái niệm

**Định nghĩa:**

> The EU AI Act (Regulation (EU) 2024/1689, Official Journal L 2024/1689) is the world's first comprehensive binding legal framework specifically regulating artificial intelligence. It was adopted by the European Parliament on 13 March 2024 and entered into force on 1 August 2024, with phased implementation timelines extending to 2026-2027. The Act classifies AI systems by risk level (unacceptable, high, limited, minimal) and imposes corresponding obligations on AI providers and deployers. For education, the Act establishes that AI systems used for admissions, student assessment, and exam monitoring are 'high-risk' and subject to the most stringent requirements, including mandatory conformity assessments, transparency, and human oversight.

**Phân loại vi phạm:**

> The EU AI Act creates four risk categories with different compliance obligations: (1) Unacceptable risk — prohibited AI practices (social scoring, subliminal manipulation) — none specifically in education; (2) High-risk — AI in education (Annex III, §3): admission decisions, assessment of learning outcomes, monitoring student behavior during exams, educational guidance systems; (3) Limited risk — AI systems with transparency obligations (Article 50): AI-generated content must be disclosed as AI-generated; (4) Minimal risk — AI tools without specific requirements. Most GenAI writing assistants used by students fall under limited risk (Article 50 disclosure); AI-based plagiarism detection systems potentially fall under high-risk (assessment of learning outcomes).

**Lịch sử khái niệm:**

> The EU AI Act was proposed by the European Commission on 21 April 2021 (the same month as UNESCO's AI Ethics Recommendation was in development). The legislative process spanned 2021-2024: Commission proposal (2021), Council general approach (2022), European Parliament amendments (2023 — significantly expanding the scope to include foundation models/GenAI), and final trilogue agreement (December 2023). The Act's education provisions were strengthened during the 2023 Parliament negotiations, reflecting the post-ChatGPT awareness of GenAI risks in education. The Act is the first binding global AI law and sets a regulatory template that other jurisdictions are expected to follow.

### Bằng chứng thực tế

**Diễn biến:**

> Implementation timeline: (1) 1 August 2024 — Act entered into force; (2) 2 February 2025 — prohibited AI practices apply; (3) 2 August 2025 — GPAI (General Purpose AI) obligations apply; (4) 2 August 2026 — high-risk AI systems (including education Annex III §3), Article 50 transparency obligations, and Article 4 AI literacy requirements apply; (5) 2 August 2027 — high-risk AI embedded in existing products. The EU AI Office was established to oversee implementation, particularly for GPAI models. National competent authorities (one per member state) are being designated to enforce the Act.

**Kết quả xử lý:**

> By early 2026: (1) EU AI Office established and operational; (2) GPAI Code of Practice in development (voluntary commitments from AI developers including OpenAI, Google, Anthropic); (3) National AI authorities designated in most EU member states; (4) Article 50 enforcement preparation underway — EdTech platforms preparing disclosure mechanisms; (5) Universities conducting fundamental rights impact assessments for high-risk AI in admissions and assessment; (6) Non-EU jurisdictions (UK, Singapore, India, Vietnam) monitoring EU implementation as model for their own regulation.

**Con số thống kê:**

> Regulation (EU) 2024/1689; entered force 1 August 2024; phased to August 2026-2027; Annex III lists 8 high-risk categories (§3 = education); Article 50 effective 2 August 2026; Article 4 effective 2 August 2026; applies to 27 EU member states + EEA; Brussels Effect extends impact globally; penalties up to 35 million EUR or 7% global turnover for prohibited practice violations; 35 articles with education-specific provisions.

### Tác hại & Hậu quả

**Hại cho người học:**

> The EU AI Act's education provisions protect learners through: (1) High-risk classification ensuring AI admission and assessment systems are rigorously tested before deployment — preventing algorithmic discrimination in university admissions; (2) Human oversight requirement meaning AI-generated assessment scores must be reviewable by a human educator; (3) Transparency for students — they must be informed when AI systems are involved in decisions about them; (4) Fundamental rights impact assessment requirement before deploying high-risk AI on vulnerable populations (students).

**Hại cho nghiên cứu:**

> Article 50's requirement that AI-generated text be disclosed as AI-generated directly addresses research integrity: researchers using GenAI to draft manuscripts, generate literature reviews, or produce data analysis must disclose this. While Article 50 is primarily directed at AI providers (requiring disclosure mechanisms), it establishes the legal norm that AI-generated academic content must be labeled — supporting journal policies requiring AI disclosure in research publications. The Act's transparency requirements for high-risk AI also apply to AI-based peer review tools.

**Hại cho xã hội:**

> The EU AI Act is the most significant legislative intervention in AI governance globally. For education specifically: (1) It protects credential validity by requiring that AI used in assessment be rigorously governed; (2) It prevents discriminatory AI from gatekeeping educational access; (3) Article 4's AI literacy requirement builds societal capacity to use AI responsibly — directly addressing the long-term risk of AI-enabled academic fraud becoming normalized.

### Phát hiện & Công cụ

**Công cụ phát hiện:**

> Annex III §3's inclusion of 'assessment of learning outcomes' as high-risk may encompass AI detection tools if they are used in ways that affect students' grades or academic standing. If Turnitin's AI detector or GPTZero's output is used as evidence in disciplinary proceedings, the system could be classified as high-risk, requiring: conformity assessment, bias testing, transparency to affected students, and human oversight. This interpretation — if confirmed by the EU AI Office — would substantially raise compliance burdens for AI detection tool providers.

**Hạn chế công cụ:**

> The Act's non-discrimination and fundamental rights requirements directly address the ESL bias problem in AI detectors: (1) Annex III high-risk systems must demonstrate that they do not produce discriminatory outcomes by protected characteristics — language background (linked to ESL status) may be a relevant proxy; (2) The Liang et al. (Stanford, 2023) finding of 61.3% false positives for ESL writers, if replicated in EU academic contexts, would constitute evidence of prohibited discrimination in a high-risk system; (3) Article 50 disclosure obligations could require AI detectors to disclose their AI nature and methodology to users.

**LLM Watermarking:**

> Article 50 of the EU AI Act requires AI systems that generate synthetic content to mark it in a machine-readable format — effectively mandating watermarking for commercial GenAI outputs (applicable from 2 August 2026). This is the most significant government mandate for LLM watermarking globally. For educational contexts: AI writing assistants must implement disclosure mechanisms; students using watermarked AI output and submitting it undisclosed would be in breach of Article 50's transparency norm. This provides the legal architecture for enforcement of attribution requirements in academic integrity policies.

### Phòng ngừa & Giải pháp

**Thiết kế bài tập:**

> The EU AI Act's high-risk classification for exam monitoring AI directly motivates assessment design changes: (1) Remote proctoring AI that monitors student behavior during online exams is high-risk and faces significant compliance burdens — making simpler assessment designs (open-book, take-home with AI disclosure, oral defense) more attractive than high-risk proctored exams; (2) Article 4's AI literacy requirement creates a pedagogical opportunity: assessments that require students to analyze AI outputs, audit AI systems, or design AI use policies directly address the mandatory AI literacy competency.

**Chính sách trường học:**

> European universities must comply with the EU AI Act in: (1) Admissions — AI systems used in admissions decisions are high-risk and require conformity assessment; (2) Assessment — AI grading systems are high-risk; (3) Exam monitoring — proctoring AI is high-risk; (4) Student information systems — AI-powered student advising must comply with limited risk transparency requirements. Non-EU universities using EU-based EdTech or serving EU students may also face compliance obligations under the Act's extraterritorial provisions.

**Văn hóa tổ chức:**

> The EU AI Act shifts the institutional culture around AI from voluntary ethics to mandatory compliance. For academic integrity specifically: Article 50 makes AI disclosure a legal obligation (not just an ethical recommendation) in EU contexts; Article 4 makes AI literacy a legal requirement for organizations deploying AI (not just a pedagogical aspiration). This legal grounding reinforces the cultural shift toward responsible AI use that Bretag and McCabe advocate through educational means.

**AI Literacy:**

> Article 4 of the EU AI Act mandates that providers and deployers of AI systems 'take measures to ensure a sufficient level of AI literacy of their staff and other persons dealing with the operation and use of AI systems.' For educational institutions that deploy AI (admissions AI, AI grading, AI tutoring), this creates a binding obligation to provide AI literacy training to faculty and staff — the first legally mandated AI literacy requirement globally. This directly reinforces UNESCO 2023's AI literacy recommendations with the force of EU law.

### Khung pháp lý & Đạo đức

**Khung quốc tế:**

> The EU AI Act is the most advanced binding AI governance instrument globally and is expected to serve as a model for national AI regulation worldwide. Its relationship to other frameworks: UNESCO 2021 Recommendation — AI Act implements UNESCO principles in binding EU law; UNESCO 2023 GenAI Guidance — Article 50 operationalizes UNESCO transparency principles; ICAI 6 Values — Article 50 (Honesty), Annex III (Fairness), Article 4 (Responsibility); IEEE EAD — Act's transparency and accountability principles align with IEEE's Transparency and Accountability pillars; ACM Code of Ethics — Article 50 implements ACM 1.3 (Be Honest and Trustworthy) in law.

**Pháp luật Việt Nam:**

> The EU AI Act does not directly apply to Vietnam, but its extraterritorial reach affects: (1) Vietnamese EdTech companies operating in EU markets must comply; (2) Vietnamese researchers publishing in EU-based journals face Article 50 disclosure norms; (3) Vietnamese universities partnered with EU institutions face compliance expectations. Vietnam's AI governance is evolving: Decision 127/QD-TTg (2021) on AI strategy does not yet have a comprehensive AI regulation law. The EU AI Act serves as a model for Vietnam's developing AI regulation, and Vietnamese legal scholars are actively studying it as a template.

**Chính sách châu Á:**

> The EU AI Act's global influence through the Brussels Effect: (1) Singapore's MAS/IMDA AI Governance Framework explicitly references EU AI Act principles; (2) South Korea's AI Basic Act (under development) draws on EU framework; (3) Japan's Cabinet Office AI Strategy references EU risk-based approach; (4) China's AI regulations (Provisional Measures on GenAI, 2023; AI Safety Measures, 2024) parallel the EU Act's risk classification concept; (5) India's Digital India Act references EU principles. Asian universities serving EU students or using EU-based EdTech platforms face indirect compliance obligations.

### Góc nhìn EdTech

**AI tutor vs AI cheater:**

> The EU AI Act provides a legal framework for the tutor/cheater distinction: AI used as a tutor (educational tool disclosed to students, subject to transparency requirements) is regulated but permitted; AI used by students to generate undisclosed academic work violates Article 50's disclosure requirement (once effective August 2026) and institutional policies implementing the Act. The Act creates legal liability for AI providers who facilitate academic dishonesty through inadequate disclosure mechanisms — shifting some responsibility from students to EdTech companies.

**Chính sách EdTech companies:**

> The EU AI Act creates binding obligations for EdTech companies: (1) Article 50 — AI writing tools, AI tutoring chatbots, AI content generators must implement disclosure mechanisms by August 2026; (2) Annex III high-risk — AI admission systems, AI assessment tools, AI exam monitors must undergo conformity assessment; (3) Article 4 — EdTech companies deploying AI must ensure users have adequate AI literacy; (4) GPAI provisions — foundation model providers (OpenAI, Google, Anthropic) must provide transparency documentation enabling downstream EdTech compliance. Khan Academy, Coursera, Duolingo, and all major EdTech platforms operating in EU markets are preparing compliance programs.

**Đề xuất nhà nghiên cứu EdTech:**

> Applying EU AI Act to EdTech AI integrity research: (1) Research opportunity: analyze how Vietnamese universities' AI use (admissions AI, plagiarism detection) would be classified under EU AI Act Annex III — identifying governance gaps; (2) Policy recommendation: advocate for a Vietnamese AI governance law that adopts the EU risk-based classification approach, applying Annex III §3 equivalents to Vietnamese education contexts; (3) Compliance analysis: Vietnamese EdTech companies expanding to EU markets need immediate AI Act compliance audits; (4) Article 50 as model: recommend that Vietnamese MOET adopt a disclosure requirement for AI-generated academic work modeled on EU Article 50, implementing it before the EU deadline (August 2026) to align Vietnam's standards with international best practice; (5) AI literacy: use Article 4 to argue for mandatory AI literacy provisions in Vietnam's Teacher Law (Luat Nha giao) currently under development.

### Nguồn tài liệu

**Bài báo khoa học:**

> European Parliament (2024). Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 on Artificial Intelligence. Official Journal of the EU. Veale, M. & Borgesius, F.Z. (2021). Demystifying the Draft EU Artificial Intelligence Act. Computer Law Review International, 22(4), 97-112. Edwards, L. (2022). The EU AI Act: A Summary Analysis. Alan Turing Institute. Novelli, C. et al. (2024). Generative AI in EU Law. arXiv:2401.17051.

**Sách tham khảo:**

> European Commission (2021). Proposal for a Regulation on Artificial Intelligence. COM(2021) 206 final. Floridi, L. (ed.) (2023). Ethics, Governance, and Policies in Artificial Intelligence. Springer. Doshi-Velez, F. et al. (2017). Accountability of AI Under the Law. Berkman Klein Center Report. UNESCO (2021). Recommendation on the Ethics of AI.

**Nguồn Việt Nam:**

> Vietnam is not an EU member state and the EU AI Act does not directly apply. However, Vietnamese legal scholars at HCMUS, VNU Law Faculty, and the Ministry of Justice are actively studying the EU AI Act as a model for Vietnam's AI regulatory framework [uncertain: exact policy development status]. Vietnamese EdTech companies with EU operations (e.g., VNG, FPT Software) must comply with the Act. Vietnam's Law on Science and Technology (2025) and the developing AI governance framework may incorporate EU Act principles. The Bretag/UNESCO/EU framework convergence provides a coherent international standard for Vietnam to adopt.

## IEEE Ethically Aligned Design (EAD1e, 2019) {#ieee-ethically-aligned-design-ead1e-2019}

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Type | policy |
| Year | 2019 |
| Organization | IEEE Global Initiative on Ethics of Autonomous and Intelligent Systems |
| Url | https://engagestandards.ieee.org/rs/211-FYL-955/images/EAD1e.pdf |

### Định nghĩa & Khái niệm

**Định nghĩa:**

> IEEE Ethically Aligned Design (EAD), First Edition (EAD1e, 2019), is the IEEE Global Initiative on Ethics of Autonomous and Intelligent Systems' comprehensive framework for aligning AI and autonomous systems with human values. Published by the IEEE Standards Association after four years of development (2016-2019) involving 500+ contributors from over 100 countries, EAD1e is one of the most technically detailed AI ethics documents produced before the EU AI Act. It provides both high-level ethical principles and concrete technical recommendations for AI system designers, engineers, and organizations. For educational AI and academic integrity, EAD1e's principles of Transparency, Accountability, and Awareness of Misuse are most directly relevant.

**Phân loại vi phạm:**

> EAD1e organizes AI ethics obligations under eight General Principles. For academic integrity contexts, the most relevant are: (1) Transparency — AI systems used in educational assessment must be transparent in their operation and decision-making; (2) Accountability — clear human accountability for AI system outcomes; (3) Awareness of Misuse — AI designers must consider how their systems could be misused, including for academic fraud; (4) Data Agency — students have rights over data collected about them by educational AI; (5) Human Well-being — AI systems should enhance, not undermine, human flourishing and genuine learning. Violations include: deploying opaque AI assessment systems; failing to audit AI systems for misuse potential; neglecting data rights of students.

**Lịch sử khái niệm:**

> The IEEE Global Initiative was launched in 2016 as a response to growing concerns about AI safety and ethics. EAD was developed through three draft versions (v1: 2016, v2: 2017) before the First Edition (EAD1e) was released in 2019. The development process was unusual for IEEE in being open, consultative, and multi-disciplinary rather than purely technical. EAD1e was accompanied by draft IEEE standards including P7000 (Model Process for Addressing Ethical Concerns), P7001 (Transparency of Autonomous Systems), and P7002 (Data Privacy Process). The framework influenced subsequent AI ethics documents including the EU AI Act and UNESCO 2021 Recommendation.

### Bằng chứng thực tế

**Tên trường hợp:**

> EAD1e has been applied in: (1) IEEE member companies developing educational AI (e.g., IBM Watson Education, Microsoft Azure AI in Education); (2) Government AI ethics frameworks in Australia, Canada, and Singapore that reference IEEE standards; (3) University AI ethics programs that use EAD1e as a teaching document; (4) EdTech industry self-regulation efforts citing IEEE principles. The Transparency principle (and its derived standard IEEE P7001) has been applied to AI-based student assessment systems in several European universities.

**Diễn biến:**

> EAD1e's development: IEEE formed the Global Initiative in 2016; 13 committees (General Principles, Embedding Values into Autonomous AI, Methodologies, Data, Legal, Economics, Well-being, Education, Mixed Reality, Policy, Military, News/Entertainment, Medically-enabled AI) produced reports that were integrated into EAD1e. The Education committee specifically addressed AI in education — making EAD1e one of the few major AI ethics documents to include education-specific guidance from a technical professional society perspective. The document was released free of charge to maximize impact.

**Kết quả xử lý:**

> EAD1e has influenced: (1) IEEE P7000-series standards development (several standards now published); (2) ACM/IEEE joint software engineering ethics work; (3) EU AI Act's risk classification approach (EAD1e's risk-based thinking influenced EU policy); (4) National AI strategies in 50+ countries; (5) Corporate AI ethics frameworks at Google, Microsoft, IBM, and Amazon all reference or align with EAD principles. In education specifically, IEEE published a companion 'AI in Education' report (2019) that applies EAD principles to educational contexts.

**Con số thống kê:**

> Development: 2016-2019; 4 years; 500+ contributors; 100+ countries represented; 294 pages (EAD1e); 8 general principles; 10+ draft standards generated (P7000 series); free download; IEEE membership 420,000 in 160+ countries; EAD1e available in English (translations limited) [uncertain: translation status]; cited in EU AI Act development process.

### Tác hại & Hậu quả

**Hại cho người học:**

> EAD1e's Human Well-being principle explicitly prioritizes genuine human flourishing over narrow metrics: an AI educational system that optimizes test scores while undermining genuine learning (by enabling AI-assisted fraud) violates the Human Well-being principle. The Data Agency principle protects students' rights over data collected about them by educational AI — including exam proctoring data, AI tutoring interaction logs, and behavioral analytics. EAD1e requires that AI systems be designed to enhance human capability, not create dependency — directly addressing the risk of over-reliance on AI writing tools.

**Hại cho nghiên cứu:**

> EAD1e's Transparency principle directly addresses research integrity: AI systems involved in research (literature review, data analysis, manuscript generation) must be transparent about their operation. The Accountability principle requires identifiable human accountability for AI-assisted research outputs — if an AI system generates a hallucinated citation, the researcher (not the AI) is accountable for verifying it. EAD1e's Data Agency principle applies to AI systems trained on scientific papers — the rights of original authors over their data in AI training sets.

**Hại cho xã hội:**

> The Human Well-being principle frames AI's societal impact in terms of genuine human flourishing, not just immediate user satisfaction. AI academic fraud that allows students to obtain degrees without genuine competency harms not only the educational institution but the professionals, patients, clients, and communities who will be served by incompetent credentialed graduates. EAD1e explicitly addresses societal-level harm from AI systems that optimize local metrics (student grades) at the expense of broader values (genuine education).

### Phát hiện & Công cụ

**Công cụ phát hiện:**

> The Transparency principle (and its associated IEEE P7001 standard on Transparency of Autonomous Systems) directly applies to AI detection tools: (1) AI detectors used in academic integrity proceedings must be transparent about their operation; (2) Affected students have a right to understand how the AI detector made its determination; (3) The Accountability principle requires identifiable human accountability for false positive accusations generated by AI detectors; (4) The Awareness of Misuse principle requires that AI detector designers consider how detectors can be gamed (humanization tools, paraphrasing) and design mitigations.

**Hạn chế công cụ:**

> EAD1e's Data Agency principle is particularly relevant to AI detection bias: students provide their writing to AI detectors, which constitutes data collection. Under Data Agency, students should have rights over how their writing data is used, stored, and processed. The Liang et al. (Stanford, 2023) ESL bias finding creates an accountability problem under EAD1e: the organizations that deployed biased detectors must be held accountable for false positive harms under the Accountability principle.

**LLM Watermarking:**

> IEEE P7001 (Transparency of Autonomous Systems, published 2021) provides technical standards for AI system transparency that apply to watermarking: watermarking schemes must be transparent to affected parties about what information is embedded, who can read it, and how it will be used. EAD1e's Data Agency principle raises privacy concerns about watermarking: if watermarks can de-anonymize student writing, they may infringe on student data rights. The Awareness of Misuse principle requires watermarking researchers to address watermark stripping attacks in their design.

### Phòng ngừa & Giải pháp

**Thiết kế bài tập:**

> EAD1e's Human Well-being and Accountability principles motivate assessment designs that verify genuine human capability: assessments where AI can invisibly complete all the work violate the Educational purpose that assessments serve (Human Well-being). The Accountability principle applied to assessment design: when AI completes an assessment, who is accountable for the learning outcome? Accountability requires that assessments be designed so that human learners bear genuine accountability for their performance — motivating process-based, oral, and portfolio assessments.

**Chính sách trường học:**

> EAD1e's Accountability principle requires institutions to establish clear governance for educational AI deployment: who is responsible when an AI admission system makes a biased decision? When an AI grading system makes an error? When an AI proctor wrongly flags a student? The principle requires institutional policies to specify accountability chains for all educational AI applications — a governance design requirement that most university AI policies currently do not meet.

**Văn hóa tổ chức:**

> EAD1e explicitly addresses organizational culture: Chapter 3 (Accountability) discusses the role of organizational culture in ensuring that engineering ethics is not merely procedural but embedded in professional identity. This aligns with McCabe's honor culture research and Bretag's Approach element: the most effective integrity cultures are those where values are internalized, not just enforced. EAD1e requires AI development organizations to build ethical AI culture through hiring, training, and incentive alignment.

**AI Literacy:**

> EAD1e's awareness of misuse principle requires AI practitioners to understand how AI can be misused — this is a professional AI literacy competency that parallels the student/faculty AI literacy required by UNESCO 2023 and EU AI Act Article 4. The Education chapter of EAD1e specifically addresses AI literacy in education systems — recommending that educational institutions teach AI ethics alongside AI technical skills. EAD1e provides the engineering professional perspective on AI literacy that complements UNESCO's educational perspective.

### Khung pháp lý & Đạo đức

**Khung quốc tế:**

> EAD1e is the primary technical professional society contribution to AI ethics, complementing: UNESCO 2021 (intergovernmental), EU AI Act (legislative), ACM Code (computing professionals). IEEE EAD and ACM Code together cover professional obligations of engineers and computing practitioners. EAD1e's Transparency principle is operationalized by EU AI Act Article 50; its Accountability principle by EU AI Act's human oversight requirements; its Data Agency principle by EU AI Act's data governance requirements. The convergence of these frameworks reflects an emerging international consensus on AI ethics.

**Pháp luật Việt Nam:**

> IEEE has a Vietnam Section (IEEE Vietnam Section) with thousands of members in engineering and technology fields. Vietnamese IEEE members — particularly those working in EdTech, AI, and computing — have professional obligations under EAD1e's principles. Vietnam's Law on Science and Technology (2025) references international standards; IEEE standards (including P7001 Transparency) are potential technical references for Vietnam's AI governance. The Awareness of Misuse principle is directly applicable to Vietnamese EdTech developers building AI tools used in educational assessment.

**Chính sách châu Á:**

> IEEE has the largest Asian membership of any technology professional society. EAD1e principles are increasingly influential in Asian AI governance: Singapore's AI Governance Framework references IEEE standards; Japan's Society 5.0 AI ethics framework aligns with EAD principles; South Korea's AI ethics guidelines reference IEEE EAD; India's Responsible AI Framework references IEEE standards. In higher education, IEEE-affiliated engineering schools in Asia (IITs, NUS Engineering, HCMUT) are natural venues for EAD1e adoption.

### Góc nhìn EdTech

**AI tutor vs AI cheater:**

> EAD1e's Human Well-being principle provides the most precise technical ethics answer to the tutor/cheater debate: AI functions as a tutor when it demonstrably enhances human learning (measurable improvement in genuine competency) and as a cheater when it displaces human learning (no competency development despite credential acquisition). The Accountability principle ensures that the student, not the AI, remains accountable for learning outcomes — regardless of AI assistance level. The Awareness of Misuse principle obligates AI tool designers to consider and mitigate the cheating use case.

**Chính sách EdTech companies:**

> EAD1e's five principles create design obligations for EdTech companies: (1) Transparency — EdTech AI must explain its operations to students and teachers; (2) Accountability — clear responsibility chains for AI-generated educational decisions; (3) Human Well-being — EdTech AI must demonstrably enhance, not harm, learning outcomes; (4) Data Agency — students must have meaningful control over their educational data; (5) Awareness of Misuse — EdTech companies must assess and mitigate AI misuse for academic fraud. Current EdTech company policies vary widely in meeting these obligations; Khan Academy Khanmigo's design most comprehensively satisfies EAD1e principles among major platforms.

**Đề xuất nhà nghiên cứu EdTech:**

> Applying EAD1e to EdTech AI integrity research: (1) Use the Transparency principle to develop an audit instrument for AI systems used in Vietnamese university assessment — requiring technical documentation meeting IEEE P7001 standards; (2) Apply the Accountability principle to map responsibility chains in Vietnamese EdTech deployments — who is accountable when an AI admission or assessment system makes a harmful error?; (3) Use the Awareness of Misuse principle to require that Vietnamese EdTech developers conduct misuse risk assessments before deploying educational AI; (4) Apply Data Agency principles to analyze Vietnamese university EdTech data governance — do students have meaningful rights over data collected about them?; (5) Propose that Vietnam's VAIP (computing professional association) and IEEE Vietnam Section jointly develop an EAD1e-aligned code of conduct for Vietnamese EdTech practitioners.

### Nguồn tài liệu

**Bài báo khoa học:**

> IEEE Global Initiative on Ethics of Autonomous and Intelligent Systems (2019). Ethically Aligned Design: A Vision for Prioritizing Human Well-being with Autonomous and Intelligent Systems, First Edition. IEEE. Chatila, R. et al. (2021). Trustworthy AI. In: Brains and Machines. Springer. IEEE (2021). IEEE P7001 Transparency of Autonomous Systems. IEEE Standards.

**Sách tham khảo:**

> IEEE EAD1e (2019) full document (free download). Floridi, L. et al. (2018). AI4People. Minds and Machines. Mittelstadt, B. et al. (2016). The Ethics of Algorithms. Big Data & Society. Russell, S. (2019). Human Compatible: Artificial Intelligence and the Problem of Control. Viking.
