## UNESCO Recommendation on the Ethics of AI (2021) {#unesco-recommendation-on-the-ethics-of-ai-2021}

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Type | policy |
| Year | 2021 |
| Organization | UNESCO |
| Url | https://unesdoc.unesco.org/ark:/48223/pf0000380455 |

### Định nghĩa & Khái niệm

**Định nghĩa:**

> The UNESCO Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence (2021) is the first global normative instrument on AI ethics, adopted unanimously by all 193 UNESCO member states at the 41st General Conference in November 2021. It defines AI ethics as the set of values, principles, and processes that guide the development and use of AI in a way that respects human rights, promotes human dignity, and safeguards societal well-being. The Recommendation is non-binding but carries significant normative authority as the only globally agreed AI ethics framework. It explicitly addresses AI in education and research as a distinct policy domain requiring special protections.

**Phân loại vi phạm:**

> The Recommendation addresses ethical AI violations across multiple domains. For education and research, it identifies: (1) Algorithmic bias — AI systems that produce inequitable outcomes in student assessment or admission; (2) Transparency failures — AI systems whose decision-making processes are opaque to affected parties; (3) Privacy violations — educational AI that collects student data beyond what is necessary; (4) Autonomy undermining — AI that displaces human judgment in educational decisions; (5) Research integrity risks — AI tools that generate fabricated or unverifiable research content. The Recommendation frames these not as criminal violations but as ethical harms requiring preventive governance.

**Lịch sử khái niệm:**

> The Recommendation was developed through a two-year consultative process (2019-2021) involving member states, civil society, and technical experts. It builds on earlier UNESCO work including the 2019 'Beijing Consensus on AI and Education' and the OECD AI Principles (2019). As the first binding-level (though technically non-binding) global AI ethics instrument, it filled the normative gap before national AI regulations (like the EU AI Act, 2024) were enacted. The Recommendation is reviewed every four years; the 2025 review is expected to address generative AI specifically.

### Bằng chứng thực tế

**Tên trường hợp:**

> The UNESCO 2021 Recommendation has been referenced in national AI strategies across member states. Direct applications: EU AI Act (2024) explicitly builds on UNESCO 2021 principles; Vietnam's national AI strategy (Decision 127/QD-TTg) references UNESCO standards; Singapore's AI Governance Framework cites UNESCO principles; India's National AI Strategy references UNESCO Recommendation. In higher education, the Recommendation has been cited by universities developing AI ethics policies.

**Diễn biến:**

> The Recommendation's development: UNESCO commissioned the Ad Hoc Expert Group on the Ethics of AI (2020-2021), conducted global consultations in all regions, and presented the final text to the 41st General Conference. The unanimous adoption by 193 member states — including geopolitical rivals — is historically significant. Implementation is monitored through a Readiness Assessment Methodology (RAM) that helps member states evaluate their AI ethics governance capacity. UNESCO has committed to supporting implementation through technical assistance and capacity building.

**Kết quả xử lý:**

> Since adoption in 2021: (1) UNESCO has supported 50+ countries in developing national AI ethics frameworks using the Recommendation as a template; (2) The Readiness Assessment Methodology has been applied in multiple national reviews; (3) UNESCO's 2023 GenAI in Education guidance directly implements Recommendation principles; (4) The Recommendation is cited in EU AI Act recitals; (5) Multiple national higher education AI policies reference UNESCO 2021 as normative foundation.

**Con số thống kê:**

> Adopted November 2021; 193 member states unanimous; 4 policy action areas; 11 core values and principles; 10+ policy areas covered; first global normative AI ethics instrument; reviewed every 4 years; UNESCO RAM applied in 50+ countries [uncertain: exact RAM application count]; document: 36 pages in original English.

### Tác hại & Hậu quả

**Hại cho người học:**

> The Recommendation's education policy area specifically protects learners: AI systems used for student assessment must be transparent and subject to human oversight; automated grading must not replace teacher judgment in high-stakes decisions; student data collected by educational AI is protected by data sovereignty principles. The Recommendation explicitly states that AI in education should enhance, not replace, the teacher-student relationship.

**Hại cho nghiên cứu:**

> The Recommendation's research policy area requires: (1) AI-generated research content must be transparent about its AI origins; (2) Research institutions must conduct Ethical Impact Assessments before deploying AI in research workflows; (3) Algorithmic bias in research AI (e.g., AI that preferentially surfaces certain research in literature reviews) must be identified and mitigated; (4) Human researchers must maintain oversight of AI in the research process — directly relevant to hallucinated citations and fabricated data problems.

**Hại cho xã hội:**

> The Recommendation's core principle of human oversight means that AI cannot displace human accountability in education systems: teachers remain responsible for educational decisions even when AI assists them; institutions remain accountable for outcomes even when AI systems influence those outcomes. This accountability principle directly addresses the risk of credential inflation through AI-assisted academic fraud — the institution cannot disclaim responsibility by citing AI use.

### Phát hiện & Công cụ

**Công cụ phát hiện:**

> The Recommendation does not prescribe specific detection tools but requires algorithmic transparency for any AI system used in educational assessment — including AI detection tools. Under UNESCO principles, an AI detector used to adjudicate academic misconduct charges must have: (1) transparent methodology; (2) documented accuracy and bias characteristics; (3) human oversight of final decisions; (4) appeal mechanisms. These requirements, if applied, would substantially constrain how tools like Turnitin's AI detector and GPTZero can be used in disciplinary proceedings.

**Hạn chế công cụ:**

> The Recommendation's principles directly support the case against sole reliance on AI detection tools: (1) Transparency principle — AI detectors must disclose their methodology; (2) Non-discrimination principle — tools must not produce disparate outcomes for ESL/EAL students (directly addressing Liang et al.'s 61.3% false positive finding); (3) Human oversight — detector outputs must be reviewed by humans before being used in disciplinary action; (4) Accountability — institutions deploying detectors bear responsibility for false positive harms.

**LLM Watermarking:**

> LLM watermarking aligns with the Recommendation's transparency principle — watermarks make AI provenance detectable, supporting the goal of transparent AI in academic contexts. However, the Recommendation's data sovereignty principles raise questions about watermarking schemes that could de-anonymize or track individual students' AI use. Any watermarking scheme deployed in educational contexts would need to satisfy UNESCO's privacy and data protection requirements.

### Phòng ngừa & Giải pháp

**Thiết kế bài tập:**

> The Recommendation's principle that AI should enhance rather than replace human capabilities in education directly motivates assessment designs that verify genuine human learning. Process-based assessments, portfolio submissions, and oral defenses — which require students to demonstrate AI-irreducible human competencies — align with UNESCO's vision of AI as an educational tool rather than a replacement for learning. The Recommendation's Ethical Impact Assessment requirement could apply to assessment designs: do AI-vulnerable assessments ethically serve their educational purpose?

**Chính sách trường học:**

> The Recommendation provides the normative foundation that university AI policies should reference. Stanford, NUS, MIT, and major universities developing AI policies cite or align with UNESCO 2021 principles without always citing them explicitly. The Recommendation's human oversight requirement supports tiered policy approaches (like AIAS) that maintain human judgment in assessment design while permitting AI use in appropriate contexts.

**Văn hóa tổ chức:**

> The Recommendation's 'proportionality and do no harm' principle requires institutional AI policies to be proportionate to the integrity risk — blanket AI prohibitions may cause disproportionate harm to students with legitimate AI needs (accessibility, language support) while failing to deter determined bad actors. This supports the McCabe/Bretag insight that culture-based approaches are more proportionate and effective than enforcement-only approaches.

**AI Literacy:**

> The Recommendation's education policy area explicitly calls for AI literacy education at all levels — students, teachers, and administrators must develop understanding of AI capabilities, limitations, and ethical implications. This is the precursor to the EU AI Act Article 4 (mandatory AI literacy for providers and users). UNESCO has developed AI competency frameworks for teachers (2021) and students as implementation tools for this principle.

### Khung pháp lý & Đạo đức

**Khung quốc tế:**

> UNESCO 2021 is the apex normative instrument in the global AI ethics hierarchy. It informed: EU AI Act (2024), which implements UNESCO principles in binding EU law; national AI strategies in 50+ countries; UNESCO's own 2023 GenAI in Education guidance; OECD AI Principles alignment; G20 AI Principles (2019, 2023). In the academic integrity domain, it provides the human rights and dignity foundation that makes academic integrity ethically significant beyond mere rule compliance.

**Pháp luật Việt Nam:**

> Vietnam is a UNESCO member state and therefore formally committed to implementing UNESCO 2021 Recommendation. Vietnam's national AI strategy (Decision 127/QD-TTg, 2021) references international AI ethics standards consistent with UNESCO. However, Vietnam's Decree 109/2022 on research integrity does not explicitly implement UNESCO 2021 principles for educational AI. The gap between Vietnam's UNESCO commitments and domestic implementation represents a research and policy advocacy opportunity.

**Chính sách châu Á:**

> Asian UNESCO members have engaged with the 2021 Recommendation to varying degrees: Singapore's Model AI Governance Framework (2020, updated 2023) pre-dates but aligns with UNESCO principles; South Korea's AI policy references UNESCO standards; China participated in developing the Recommendation but has implemented AI governance through different domestic frameworks; Vietnam, Thailand, and Indonesia are in early stages of UNESCO RAM implementation [uncertain: exact status for each country].

### Góc nhìn EdTech

**AI tutor vs AI cheater:**

> UNESCO 2021's principle that AI should augment human capabilities and preserve human agency provides the clearest normative resolution to the tutor/cheater debate: AI used as a tutor (enhancing human learning) is ethically supported; AI used as a cheater (replacing human learning, undermining assessment validity) violates the human agency and dignity principles. The distinction is not about the technology but about the human's relationship with the technology.

**Chính sách EdTech companies:**

> UNESCO 2021 implies duties for EdTech companies: (1) Transparency requirement — EdTech AI products must explain their decision-making to users (students and teachers); (2) Non-discrimination — AI tutoring tools must not produce worse outcomes for disadvantaged students; (3) Data protection — student data collected by EdTech platforms is protected by UNESCO principles; (4) Human oversight — EdTech AI assessment tools must allow human review of AI-generated evaluations. These principles are stricter than most current EdTech company policies.

**Đề xuất nhà nghiên cứu EdTech:**

> Applying UNESCO 2021 to EdTech AI integrity research: (1) Use the Recommendation's Ethical Impact Assessment requirement as a mandatory pre-deployment standard for educational AI tools in Vietnam; (2) Apply UNESCO's transparency principle to require disclosure of AI tool capabilities and limitations in Vietnamese university AI policies; (3) Use the non-discrimination principle to mandate bias testing of AI detectors used in Vietnamese academic integrity proceedings; (4) Argue that Vietnam's MOET obligations as a UNESCO member state require updating Decree 109 to implement UNESCO 2021 education policy area provisions; (5) UNESCO's RAM tool provides a ready-made assessment instrument for evaluating Vietnam's AI ethics governance readiness.

### Nguồn tài liệu

**Bài báo khoa học:**

> UNESCO (2021). Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence. Paris: UNESCO. Document: SHS/BIO/PI/2021/1. Voeneky, S. & Mommsen, C. (eds.) (2022). The Cambridge Handbook of Responsible Artificial Intelligence. Cambridge UP. UNESCO (2022). K-12 AI Curricula: A Mapping of Government-Endorsed AI Curricula. Paris: UNESCO.

**Sách tham khảo:**

> UNESCO (2021). Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence (full text). UNESCO (2019). Beijing Consensus on Artificial Intelligence and Education. Calo, R. (2017). Artificial Intelligence Policy: A Primer and Roadmap. UC Davis Law Review. Floridi, L. et al. (2018). AI4People — An Ethical Framework for a Good AI Society. Minds and Machines.

## UNESCO Guidance for Generative AI in Education and Research (2023) {#unesco-guidance-for-generative-ai-in-education-and-research-2023}

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Type | policy |
| Year | 2023 |
| Organization | UNESCO |
| Url | https://unesdoc.unesco.org/ark:/48223/pf0000386693 |

> **Key Recommendation:** Rather than seek to prohibit use, students and staff need to be supported in using GenAI tools effectively, ethically and transparently. Recommends age limits, data privacy, selective banning where AI displaces essential learning, AI literacy curricula.

### Định nghĩa & Khái niệm

**Định nghĩa:**

> The UNESCO Guidance for Generative AI in Education and Research (2023) is the first major international policy document specifically addressing generative AI (GenAI) technologies — such as ChatGPT, Gemini, and image generators — in educational and research contexts. Published in September 2023, it operationalizes the 2021 UNESCO AI Ethics Recommendation for the specific challenges posed by Large Language Models. The document is structured as practical guidance for governments, educational institutions, and EdTech companies, covering governance, curriculum integration, data privacy, AI literacy, and research integrity.

**Phân loại vi phạm:**

> The guidance identifies five categories of AI-related risks in education: (1) Academic dishonesty — students submitting AI-generated work as their own; (2) Pedagogical harm — AI displacing essential learning experiences (e.g., students never developing writing skills because AI writes for them); (3) Data privacy violations — GenAI platforms collecting and processing student data in non-compliant ways; (4) Algorithmic bias — GenAI outputs that reflect and amplify societal biases in educational content; (5) Research integrity violations — AI-generated research with hallucinated citations, fabricated data, or undisclosed AI authorship. The guidance recommends different governance responses for each risk category.

**Lịch sử khái niệm:**

> The guidance was developed in direct response to the disruption caused by ChatGPT's release (November 2022) and the wave of reactive institutional AI bans in early 2023. UNESCO convened expert consultations in early 2023 and published the guidance in September 2023 — the first major intergovernmental response to GenAI in education. It builds on UNESCO's 2021 Recommendation and prior work including the 2021 AI Competency Framework for Teachers and the 2023 AI Competency Framework for Students. The guidance explicitly rejects both uncritical AI adoption and blanket prohibition as policy stances.

### Bằng chứng thực tế

**Tên trường hợp:**

> The UNESCO 2023 guidance has been adopted as a reference document by education ministries across UNESCO member states. Key applications: EU Commission referenced it in 2024 AI in Education communications; Vietnam's MOET cited UNESCO guidance in 2024 circulars [uncertain: exact circular reference]; Singapore MOE aligned its 2023 AI in Education guidelines with UNESCO principles; Australia's Tertiary Education Quality and Standards Agency (TEQSA) referenced UNESCO 2023 in its AI academic integrity guidance (2024). Several universities adopted age-limit and data privacy provisions directly from the guidance.

**Diễn biến:**

> The document's development: UNESCO's Division of Policies and Lifelong Learning Systems led the drafting with input from the Digital Learning team. Consultations were held in all UNESCO regions. The guidance was released at the UNGA 78th session in September 2023. Key policy innovations in the document: (1) Recommending minimum age of 13 for GenAI use in education (consistent with COPPA/GDPR-K); (2) Requiring data privacy impact assessments before adopting GenAI tools in schools; (3) Advocating selective banning (specific assessment types) rather than blanket prohibition; (4) Calling for regulatory sandboxes for educational AI testing.

**Kết quả xử lý:**

> By early 2024: (1) Multiple national education ministries had issued guidelines referencing UNESCO 2023; (2) EU Commission's draft AI in Education Communication aligned with UNESCO principles; (3) EdTech companies (including Google for Education, Microsoft Education) updated their privacy and transparency policies citing UNESCO guidance; (4) The guidance was cited in the EU AI Act's implementation guidance for education applications; (5) UNESCO established a GenAI in Education monitoring mechanism to track implementation.

**Con số thống kê:**

> Published September 2023; 64 pages; covers K-12, higher education, and research; recommends minimum age 13 for GenAI in education; 4 key policy areas; cited by 30+ national education ministries [uncertain: exact count]; first specific UNESCO guidance on GenAI; companion to 2021 AI Ethics Recommendation; available in all 6 UNESCO official languages.

### Tác hại & Hậu quả

**Hại cho người học:**

> UNESCO 2023 identifies two distinct harms for learners: (1) AI as a shortcut — students who use AI to bypass foundational skill development (writing, critical analysis, mathematical reasoning) will lack these competencies in professional life; (2) AI inequity — students without access to GenAI tools are disadvantaged relative to peers who use them, creating a new digital divide. The guidance recommends selective banning (protecting essential skill development contexts) while ensuring equitable access to AI tools in contexts where they are permitted.

**Hại cho nghiên cứu:**

> UNESCO 2023 devotes a specific section to research integrity risks of GenAI: (1) Hallucinated citations and fabricated references undermine scientific reliability; (2) AI-generated literature reviews may miss recent or non-English publications, introducing systematic bias; (3) Undisclosed AI authorship in research papers violates transparency norms; (4) AI-translated research may introduce errors or lose nuance; (5) GenAI used for data analysis may produce statistically plausible but incorrect results. The guidance calls for mandatory AI disclosure in research publications and updated peer review guidelines.

**Hại cho xã hội:**

> The guidance identifies three societal-level harms: (1) Credential devaluation — if degrees require no genuine cognitive work, their signaling value diminishes; (2) Amplified inequality — wealthy students with access to premium AI tools gain advantages over peers without access; (3) Cultural and linguistic homogenization — GenAI predominantly trained on English-language data may disadvantage non-English-speaking students and research traditions, including Vietnamese academic culture.

### Phát hiện & Công cụ

**Công cụ phát hiện:**

> UNESCO 2023 takes a cautious stance on AI detection tools: while acknowledging their role, the guidance warns against over-reliance, citing the false positive problem for non-native English speakers. It recommends that institutions: (1) Not rely solely on AI detectors in disciplinary proceedings; (2) Require human review of all AI detector outputs before any consequence; (3) Develop assessment designs that are intrinsically resistant to AI generation (process-based, oral, contextual) rather than depending on post-hoc detection.

**Hạn chế công cụ:**

> UNESCO 2023 explicitly acknowledges the false positive problem for ESL/EAL students, aligning with Liang et al. (Stanford, 2023). The guidance states that AI detection tools can produce discriminatory outcomes when applied to non-native English speakers and recommends that institutions conduct demographic bias audits of detection tools before institutional deployment. This is the first intergovernmental endorsement of the bias concern in AI detection tools.

**LLM Watermarking:**

> UNESCO 2023 discusses technical AI identification methods including watermarking as a potential transparency mechanism. The guidance notes that watermarking is in early development and faces technical challenges (watermark stripping, model updates). It recommends that governments monitor watermarking developments and consider regulatory requirements for watermarking AI-generated content in educational contexts — anticipating EU AI Act Article 50 (effective 2026).

### Phòng ngừa & Giải pháp

**Thiết kế bài tập:**

> UNESCO 2023 provides specific guidance on assessment redesign in the GenAI era: (1) Oral examinations and viva voce defense of written work; (2) In-class, timed assessments for core competencies that AI cannot be used for; (3) Process-based assessments requiring draft submission and reflection on revision; (4) Contextual and personal assessments (involving local, personal, or contemporary knowledge AI cannot replicate); (5) AI-inclusive assessments where students must critically evaluate AI output — moving AI from a cheating tool to an assessed competency. The guidance recommends that institutions revise their assessment portfolios to include at least one AI-resistant element per course.

**Chính sách trường học:**

> UNESCO 2023 recommends a governance structure for university AI policies: (1) An institutional AI in Education policy adopted at governance level (not just faculty rules); (2) A designated AI ethics officer or committee; (3) A faculty-level process for classifying assessment types by AI permission level (consistent with AIAS); (4) Student consultation in policy development; (5) Annual policy review as technology evolves. The guidance explicitly recommends against permanent blanket AI bans, noting that these become technologically obsolete and pedagogically counterproductive.

**Văn hóa tổ chức:**

> UNESCO 2023 emphasizes institutional culture as the foundation of effective AI governance: policies that support students and faculty in navigating AI ethically (meeting Bretag's Support and Approach elements) are more effective than punitive-only approaches. The guidance recommends staff professional development and student orientation sessions as mandatory components of institutional AI governance — transforming AI integrity from a compliance matter to a learning opportunity.

**AI Literacy:**

> AI literacy is the central recommendation of UNESCO 2023 for both students and educators. Specific components: (1) Understanding how GenAI tools work (large language models, probabilistic generation, hallucination); (2) Recognizing AI-generated content and its limitations; (3) Critical evaluation of AI output quality and accuracy; (4) Ethical use: attribution, disclosure, responsible use; (5) Technical skills: effective prompting, output verification, tool selection. UNESCO has developed companion AI competency frameworks for students and teachers that operationalize these literacy components.

### Khung pháp lý & Đạo đức

**Khung quốc tế:**

> UNESCO 2023 is the primary international reference document for GenAI in education governance. It complements: EU AI Act (2024) — whose Article 50 disclosure requirement implements UNESCO transparency principles; ACM/IEEE professional ethics codes — whose standards apply to EdTech practitioners; ICAI 2021 — whose 6 values provide the normative foundation that UNESCO operationalizes; Eaton's postplagiarism framework — which UNESCO 2023 effectively endorses without citing explicitly (support rather than prohibit, attribution not detection). Together, these instruments form a coherent international governance architecture.

**Chính sách châu Á:**

> Asian education ministries have responded to UNESCO 2023 with varying speed: Singapore MOE published AI in Education guidelines (2023) strongly aligned with UNESCO principles; South Korea's KERIS issued AI education guidelines; Japan's MEXT published ChatGPT guidance (2023) that explicitly references UNESCO; China's 2023 Provisional Administrative Measures on GenAI (ISTD) took a more regulatory approach but covers some UNESCO themes; Vietnam has not yet issued a specific GenAI in Education circular as of early 2026 [uncertain: exact status of Vietnamese MOET GenAI guidance].

### Góc nhìn EdTech

**AI tutor vs AI cheater:**

> UNESCO 2023 explicitly rejects the binary framing and provides a principled middle path: 'Rather than seek to prohibit use, students and staff need to be supported in using GenAI tools effectively, ethically and transparently.' This is the clearest intergovernmental endorsement of the 'AI as tutor' framing when properly governed. The guidance operationalizes Rose Luckin's (UCL) democratization thesis while acknowledging Neil Selwyn's (Monash) fraud concerns through selective banning and detection tool caution.

**Chính sách EdTech companies:**

> UNESCO 2023 directly addresses EdTech companies' responsibilities: (1) Age-appropriate design (minimum 13+ for GenAI tools used in education); (2) Data minimization and purpose limitation for student data; (3) Transparency about GenAI capabilities and limitations to users; (4) Provision of AI literacy resources alongside GenAI tools; (5) Cooperation with government oversight mechanisms. The guidance influenced Google for Education's and Microsoft Education's 2023-2024 policy updates. Khan Academy's Khanmigo design (Socratic, age-appropriate) pre-dates but aligns with UNESCO 2023 recommendations.

**Đề xuất nhà nghiên cứu EdTech:**

> From an EdTech researcher perspective applying UNESCO 2023: (1) Use the guidance's selective banning framework to create a decision matrix for Vietnamese universities: which assessment types should ban AI, which should require disclosure, which should integrate AI; (2) Leverage the age limit recommendation (13+) to argue for data privacy protections in Vietnamese school EdTech procurement; (3) Apply the AI literacy framework to design a Vietnamese AI literacy curriculum for teacher training (aligned with MOET's Teacher Standards update); (4) Use the research integrity provisions to propose a Vietnamese update to Decree 109 addressing GenAI disclosure in academic publications; (5) Conduct a UNESCO 2023 compliance audit of the top Vietnamese EdTech platforms used in universities.

### Nguồn tài liệu

**Bài báo khoa học:**

> UNESCO (2023). Guidance for Generative AI in Education and Research. Paris: UNESCO. ISBN: 978-92-3-100612-5. Chan, C.K.Y. (2023). A comprehensive AI policy education framework for university teaching and learning. International Journal of Educational Technology in Higher Education, 20(1), 38. Miao, F. & Holmes, W. (2023). Guidance for generative AI in education and research: Key messages. UNESCO.

**Sách tham khảo:**

> UNESCO (2021). Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence. UNESCO (2021). AI Competency Framework for Teachers. UNESCO (2022). K-12 AI Curricula: A Mapping of Government-Endorsed AI Curricula. Holmes, W. et al. (2022). Artificial Intelligence and Education: A Critical View Through the Lens of Human Rights.

## Tracey Bretag {#tracey-bretag}

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Type | researcher |
| Institution | University of South Australia (UniSA) |
| Institution | University of South Australia (UniSA) |
| Specialty | Academic integrity policy frameworks; 5 core elements of exemplary policy; Handbook of Academic Integrity editor |

### Định nghĩa & Khái niệm

**Định nghĩa:**

> Bretag defines academic integrity as a set of values, behaviors, and institutional practices that ensure the honest and responsible conduct of scholarship. Her 5-core-elements framework (Access, Approach, Responsibility, Detail, Support) provides an operational definition of what exemplary policy looks like in practice.

**Phân loại vi phạm:**

> Bretag's policy framework addresses plagiarism, contract cheating, collusion, fabrication, and — in later work — AI-assisted misconduct. The Handbook of Academic Integrity (2016) provides a comprehensive taxonomy across these categories.

**Lịch sử khái niệm:**

> Bretag led the Academic Integrity Standards Project (AISP, 2010-2012) and the Exemplary Academic Integrity Project (EAIP, 2013), which analyzed 39 Australian university policies and synthesized the 5 core elements. Her work helped shift institutional discourse from punitive enforcement to educational, values-based approaches.

### Bằng chứng thực tế

**Tên trường hợp:**

> Academic Integrity Standards Project (AISP 2010-2012): analysis of 40 Australian HE institutions' policies; Exemplary Academic Integrity Project (EAIP 2013); Springer Handbook of Academic Integrity (2016, editor Bretag); IJEI (2011) 5 core elements paper.

**Diễn biến:**

> Through systematic analysis of Australian university policies, Bretag and colleagues identified that most institutions' policies were incomplete — lacking one or more of the 5 core elements. The EAIP then developed implementation frameworks and plain-English definitions to help universities improve their policies. Her 2016 Handbook synthesized global research into a landmark reference work.

**Kết quả xử lý:**

> Bretag's frameworks have been adopted by universities across Australia, New Zealand, and internationally. The 5 core elements became a benchmark for policy evaluation in the sector. Her work is cited in regulatory guidance from TEQSA (Australia) and equivalent bodies.

**Con số thống kê:**

> AISP analyzed policies at all 39 Australian universities (at time of study). The Handbook of Academic Integrity (Springer 2016) includes contributions from over 100 international scholars across 50+ chapters. Bretag's publications have thousands of citations.

### Tác hại & Hậu quả

**Hại cho người học:**

> Bretag's framework emphasizes that students who engage in academic misconduct do not develop genuine competencies. Poor institutional support (insufficient 'Support' element) leaves students vulnerable to unintentional misconduct due to inadequate guidance.

**Hại cho nghiên cứu:**

> Institutional failure to maintain rigorous integrity standards allows fabricated or plagiarized work to enter the academic record, degrading the quality of knowledge production. Bretag's 'Approach' element addresses the importance of proactive education vs. reactive detection.

**Hại cho xã hội:**

> Systemic academic integrity failures undermine public trust in higher education credentials and professional competency. Bretag frames academic integrity as an ethical foundation for civil society and professional practice.

### Phát hiện & Công cụ

**Công cụ phát hiện:**

> Bretag's work focuses more on policy and culture than on specific detection tools. She positions detection tools (plagiarism checkers, AI detectors) as one component of the 'Detail' element — part of a comprehensive policy — but insufficient on their own.

**Hạn chế công cụ:**

> Bretag emphasizes that overreliance on detection technology without educational and cultural support (the 'Support' and 'Approach' elements) creates adversarial student-institution relationships and fails to address root causes of misconduct.

### Phòng ngừa & Giải pháp

**Thiết kế bài tập:**

> Bretag's 'Approach' element calls for assessment design that makes misconduct difficult — authentic, contextual, process-based assessments that require genuine student engagement rather than easily outsourceable tasks.

**Chính sách trường học:**

> The 5 core elements (Access, Approach, Responsibility, Detail, Support) are Bretag's primary policy contribution. 'Access' means policy is visible and available; 'Approach' means proactive education; 'Responsibility' assigns clear accountability; 'Detail' means comprehensive rules; 'Support' means resources for students and staff.

**Văn hóa tổ chức:**

> Bretag strongly advocates for values-based institutional culture over enforcement-only approaches. She argues that students internalize integrity norms when institutions model them through transparent, fair, and supportive practices.

**AI Literacy:**

> Bretag's framework implicitly supports AI literacy through the 'Access' and 'Approach' elements — students need clear, accessible information about what AI tools may and may not be used for, and proactive education to understand why.

### Khung pháp lý & Đạo đức

**Khung quốc tế:**

> Bretag's work is aligned with ICAI's Fundamental Values of Academic Integrity (honesty, trust, fairness, respect, responsibility, courage). The Handbook of Academic Integrity (2016) is the most comprehensive international reference on the subject.

### Góc nhìn EdTech

**AI tutor vs AI cheater:**

> Bretag's framework is policy-neutral on AI tools per se — her 'Approach' element calls for proactive, context-specific education about how AI may be used legitimately or illegitimately, depending on the task and institutional guidance.

**Đề xuất nhà nghiên cứu EdTech:**

> Bretag recommends that institutions: (1) ensure all 5 core elements are present in their integrity policies; (2) adopt proactive educational approaches (not just punitive detection); (3) assign clear responsibility at all levels; (4) provide genuine support to students navigating integrity challenges; (5) extend these frameworks explicitly to cover GenAI.

### Nguồn tài liệu

**Bài báo khoa học:**

> Bretag et al. (2011) International Journal for Educational Integrity — 5 core elements; Bretag (2013) EAIP implementation framework; multiple chapters in Bretag (ed.) Handbook of Academic Integrity (Springer 2016); interview in Educational Philosophy and Theory (2018).

**Sách tham khảo:**

> Bretag, T. (ed.) (2016). Handbook of Academic Integrity. Singapore: Springer. This is the landmark global reference work on academic integrity policy, practice, and research.

## Sarah Elaine Eaton {#sarah-elaine-eaton}

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Type | researcher |
| Institution | University of Calgary, Werklund School of Education — Professor (Full) and Research Chair in Leadership, Policy, and Governance; Editor-in-Chief, International Journal for Educational Integrity (IJEI, Springer Nature/BMC) |
| Institution | University of Calgary, Werklund School of Education — Professor (Full) and Research Chair in Leadership, Policy, and Governance; Editor-in-Chief, International Journal for Educational Integrity (IJEI, Springer Nature/BMC) |

### Định nghĩa & Khái niệm

**Định nghĩa:**

> Sarah Elaine Eaton is the originator of the 'postplagiarism' framework — a reconceptualization of academic integrity for the generative AI era. Postplagiarism posits that as human-AI collaborative writing becomes normative, historical definitions of plagiarism require fundamental rethinking. The framework's six tenets are: (1) human-AI hybrid writing will become the norm; (2) creativity can be enhanced by AI; (3) AI can help overcome language barriers; (4) we can outsource control of writing to AI but cannot outsource responsibility; (5) attribution remains important; (6) historical plagiarism definitions require rethinking.

**Phân loại vi phạm:**

> Eaton's research spans the full taxonomy of academic integrity violations, with particular focus on: AI-assisted dishonesty, contract cheating (ghost-writing), plagiarism policy reform, and the intersection of technology and ethics in higher education. Her postplagiarism framework reframes the category of đạo văn (plagiarism) as contextually dependent rather than absolute in the AI era.

**Lịch sử khái niệm:**

> Eaton's first major integrity publication was 'Plagiarism in the Internet Age' (2017). She developed the postplagiarism concept through 2020-2024, first publishing it in IJEI (2023) and expanding it in her 2024 Springer handbook. The framework gained international recognition with the 2025 Tracey Bretag Prize for Academic Integrity — the field's most prestigious award — and has been translated into multiple languages.

### Bằng chứng thực tế

**Tên trường hợp:**

> Researcher profile — not a specific case. Eaton's most cited case-adjacent work is her analysis of contract cheating markets, AI writing tool diffusion, and institutional policy responses at Canadian universities.

**Diễn biến:** Not applicable — researcher profile.

**Kết quả xử lý:**

> Not applicable — researcher profile. Key outputs: (1) Postplagiarism framework (IJEI 2023 + 2024, Tracey Bretag Prize 2025); (2) Second Handbook of Academic Integrity (Springer 2024, 1,991 pages) — largest academic integrity reference work ever published; (3) Editor-in-Chief of IJEI (Q1 Education journal, Springer Nature/BMC) — institutional leadership of the field's primary journal.

### Tác hại & Hậu quả

**Hại cho người học:**

> Eaton's postplagiarism framework directly addresses learner harm: if AI does the intellectual work, learners fail to develop genuine competencies. However, Eaton argues nuance — blanket AI prohibition harms learners who would benefit from AI assistance (e.g., ESL writers, students with disabilities). The framework advocates for intentional integration that preserves learning outcomes while acknowledging AI-hybrid writing reality.

**Hại cho nghiên cứu:**

> Eaton's research documents how AI tools are changing the research process itself — from literature review (hallucination risk) to writing (undisclosed AI use) to citation practices. Her framework provides researchers with ethical guidelines for legitimate AI use that preserves research integrity.

**Hại cho xã hội:**

> Eaton's policy-oriented work addresses societal concerns: credential devaluation if AI-generated work is certified as human competence, equitable access to AI tools (should AI literacy be a universal right?), and the role of higher education in preparing AI-literate citizens. Her UNESCO engagement links academic integrity to broader social values.

### Phát hiện & Công cụ

**Công cụ phát hiện:**

> Eaton is critical of detection-first approaches to academic integrity. Her research shows that detection tools (Turnitin, GPTZero) create adversarial rather than educational environments. She advocates for context-sensitive integrity policies that go beyond technological surveillance. However, she acknowledges detection tools as one component of a comprehensive integrity ecosystem.

**Hạn chế công cụ:**

> Eaton's most cited critique: AI detection tools are biased against non-native English writers (citing Liang et al., Stanford, 2023: 61.3% false positive rate for ESL writers). This critique is foundational to her postplagiarism argument — punishing students for writing that 'sounds like AI' when it reflects their linguistic background is an equity issue, not an integrity issue.

**LLM Watermarking:**

> Eaton has engaged with LLM watermarking debates in the context of policy. Her framework suggests watermarking must be balanced against privacy concerns and not deployed as a surveillance instrument that targets marginalized student populations disproportionately.

### Phòng ngừa & Giải pháp

**Thiết kế bài tập:**

> Eaton's postplagiarism framework has direct implications for assignment design: (1) Move from product-based to process-based assessment (portfolios, reflective journals, staged submissions); (2) Oral defense components verify authentic understanding; (3) AI disclosure requirements in assignment submissions; (4) Assessment design that makes AI use visible rather than penalized if declared. Her work with AIAS framework (Perkins) is central to practical implementation.

**Chính sách trường học:**

> Eaton has analyzed and compared AI policies at universities globally, including Stanford, Harvard, MIT, NUS. She advocates for the AIAS 5-tier model (Perkins/BUV): from AI-prohibited to AI-required, with contextual appropriateness determined at course/task level. Her Second Handbook includes comprehensive policy templates adopted by institutions worldwide.

**Văn hóa tổ chức:**

> Eaton extends McCabe's honor code culture findings into the AI era: she argues that institutions must build 'cultures of academic integrity' in which AI use is normalized but disclosed, rather than hiding AI use in an adversarial culture of detection. The postplagiarism framework is fundamentally a cultural framework, not a technical one.

**AI Literacy:**

> AI literacy is central to Eaton's work. She argues that AI literacy must include: understanding how AI works (and fails), knowing when AI use is appropriate vs. fraudulent, understanding attribution and disclosure obligations, and critically evaluating AI outputs. Her UNESCO and EU AI Act engagement positions AI literacy as a fundamental educational right (EU AI Act Article 4).

### Khung pháp lý & Đạo đức

**Khung quốc tế:**

> Eaton is actively involved in: UNESCO AI in Education Working Group; EU AI Act consultation (Article 4 AI literacy provisions); ICAI (ICAI board member); IEEE Educational AI Working Group. Her Second Handbook is the primary reference work for international academic integrity policy. The postplagiarism framework has been formally recognized with the Tracey Bretag Prize (2025).

**Pháp luật Việt Nam:**

> Eaton's policy frameworks are relevant to Vietnam's regulatory gaps: Nghị định 109/2022/NĐ-CP Điều 20 does not address AI disclosure; Luật KH&CN 2025 (pending) represents an opportunity. Eaton's AIAS framework could be adapted for Vietnamese higher education institutions developing AI use policies.

**Chính sách châu Á:**

> Eaton's work is cited in NUS (Singapore) academic integrity policy documentation and NTU framework documents. Her IJEI editorship gives her platform to publish Asian academic integrity research. She has presented at Asian higher education conferences.

### Góc nhìn EdTech

**AI tutor vs AI cheater:**

> Eaton explicitly engages the AI tutor vs. AI cheater tension: her postplagiarism framework resolves it not by choosing sides but by reframing — the question is not whether AI is used, but how it is used and whether that use is disclosed and pedagogically appropriate. She aligns with Rose Luckin's (UCL) democratic-AI framing more than Neil Selwyn's critical framing, while acknowledging both risks.

**Chính sách EdTech companies:**

> Eaton has analyzed and published on EdTech company policies: Turnitin's AI detection (cited her ESL false positive concerns), Khan Academy Khanmigo (which aligns with her Socratic rather than product-output AI model), and Coursera's post-submission AI dialogue approach. She has contributed to discussions at EdTech policy forums about balancing integrity tools with equity.

**Đề xuất nhà nghiên cứu EdTech:**

> Eaton's core EdTech recommendations: (1) Adopt postplagiarism framework — stop treating all AI use as plagiarism and start distinguishing disclosed, attributed AI assistance from fraud; (2) Implement AIAS 5-tier policy model — contextual, not blanket prohibition or permission; (3) Build AI literacy into curriculum as foundational competency, not add-on; (4) Center equity in AI integrity policy — detection tools that penalize ESL writers are unjust; (5) Attribution standards must evolve to require disclosure of AI assistance in all academic work, including research publications.

### Nguồn tài liệu

**Bài báo khoa học:**

> Eaton, S.E. (2023). 'Postplagiarism: Transdisciplinary ethics and integrity in the age of artificial intelligence and neurotechnology.' International Journal for Educational Integrity (IJEI). Eaton, S.E. (2025). 'Global Trends in Education: Artificial Intelligence, Postplagiarism, and Future-focused Learning for 2025 and Beyond.' IJEI (2024-2025 Werklund Distinguished Research Lecture). URL: https://profiles.ucalgary.ca/sarah-eaton.

**Sách tham khảo:**

> Eaton, S.E. (Ed.) (2024). Second Handbook of Academic Integrity. Springer Nature (1,991 pages) — largest academic integrity reference work ever published. Eaton, S.E. (Ed.) (2021). Plagiarism in Higher Education: Tackling Tough Topics in Academic Integrity. ABC-CLIO. McCabe, D.L. et al. (2012). Cheating in College. Johns Hopkins UP.

**Nguồn Việt Nam:**

> No confirmed Vietnamese translations of Eaton's work. IJEI is accessible to Vietnamese researchers with institutional access. The postplagiarism framework is relevant to Vietnamese universities developing AI integrity policies under QĐ 1705/QĐ-TTg's digital transformation mandate.

## Wayne Holmes {#wayne-holmes}

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Type | researcher |
| Institution | UCL (University College London), IOE — UCL Knowledge Lab; UNESCO — Consultant, Technology and AI in Education unit |
| Institution | UCL (University College London), IOE — UCL Knowledge Lab; UNESCO — Consultant, Technology and AI in Education unit |
| Specialty | AI in education ethics; unintended consequences of AI in education; co-author of Intelligence Unleashed with Luckin |

### Định nghĩa & Khái niệm

**Định nghĩa:**

> Holmes defines ethical AI in education as requiring attention to both 'doing ethical things' (choosing ethically appropriate AI applications) and 'doing things ethically' (implementing AI in ways that respect learner rights and dignity). He emphasizes the distinction between intended benefits and unintended consequences — a core conceptual contribution.

**Phân loại vi phạm:**

> Holmes' framework categorizes AI-in-education harms as: (1) direct harms — bias, privacy violations, manipulation; (2) indirect harms — reducing teacher agency, perpetuating inequalities; (3) unintended consequences — unforeseen effects on learning, assessment, and institutional culture. AI cheating is one manifestation of category (3).

**Lịch sử khái niệm:**

> Holmes co-authored Intelligence Unleashed (2016) with Luckin, which was optimistic about AI in education. His subsequent work shifted emphasis to the ethical challenges and unintended consequences that optimistic early framings underweighted. His 2023 Education International publication 'The Unintended Consequences of Artificial Intelligence and Education' represents this maturation of thinking.

### Bằng chứng thực tế

**Tên trường hợp:**

> Holmes & Luckin et al. (2016): Intelligence Unleashed (Pearson); Holmes & Porayska-Pomsta (eds.) (2022/2023): 'The Ethics of Artificial Intelligence in Education' (Routledge); Holmes (2023): 'The Unintended Consequences of Artificial Intelligence and Education' (Education International, Brussels); Holmes et al. (2021): 'Ethics of AI in Education: Towards a Community-Wide Framework' — International Journal of Artificial Intelligence in Education.

**Diễn biến:**

> Holmes' career arc illustrates the evolution of AIEd thinking: from the optimistic democratization vision of Intelligence Unleashed (2016), to systematic ethics frameworks (2021 IJAIED community framework), to the 2023 unintended consequences work. His UNESCO consultancy work has contributed to practical guidance for policymakers globally. His Routledge edited volume with Porayska-Pomsta is the most comprehensive academic treatment of AIEd ethics.

**Kết quả xử lý:**

> Holmes' UNESCO work produced 'AI and Education: Guidance for Policy-makers' and 'Guidance for Generative AI in Education and Research' — widely cited policy documents. His community-wide ethics framework (2021) has been adopted as a reference by AIEd researchers globally. His unintended consequences framing is now standard in responsible AI in education discourse.

**Con số thống kê:**

> Holmes is Full Professor of Critical Studies of AI and Education at UCL. Intelligence Unleashed has been downloaded thousands of times from UCL Discovery and Pearson. His 2021 ethics framework paper has hundreds of citations. UNESCO AI in education guidance documents reach millions through UNESCO's global network.

### Tác hại & Hậu quả

**Hại cho người học:**

> Holmes' unintended consequences framework identifies: AI systems that misclassify students based on biased data; over-reliance on AI tutors reducing development of self-regulation; AI assessment tools that advantage students with AI access; reduction of teacher-student relationships that are intrinsically valuable for learner wellbeing.

**Hại cho nghiên cứu:**

> Holmes documents how AI tools in research and education can introduce systematic biases, create false precision in measurement, and displace richer qualitative understandings of learning with narrow quantifiable metrics — harming the epistemic quality of educational research.

**Hại cho xã hội:**

> Holmes identifies ecological costs of AI (energy, hardware extraction), social inequality from differential AI access, loss of teacher autonomy as systemic societal harms. His 2023 unintended consequences work addresses these at institutional and societal scale.

### Phát hiện & Công cụ

**Công cụ phát hiện:**

> Holmes' work on AI ethics engages with detection tools primarily as examples of surveillance AI that require ethical scrutiny. He supports transparency requirements and human oversight of automated detection systems. He is not an advocate of detection-first approaches.

**Hạn chế công cụ:**

> Holmes' ethics framework highlights that automated detection tools carry risks of false positives, bias (especially against ESL writers), and surveillance dynamics that undermine trust. His requirement for human oversight of AI decisions applies directly to detection tool outputs.

### Phòng ngừa & Giải pháp

**Thiết kế bài tập:**

> Holmes supports assessment designs that reflect genuine learning goals rather than being primarily optimized for AI-resistance or AI-detection compatibility. He emphasizes the pedagogical dimension — assessments should develop learners' capacity to engage critically with AI, not just avoid it.

**Chính sách trường học:**

> Through UNESCO consultancy, Holmes has contributed to national-level policy frameworks for AI in education. He advocates for policies that include: transparency about AI use, human oversight of AI decisions, equity impact assessments, and ongoing monitoring for unintended consequences.

**Văn hóa tổ chức:**

> Holmes supports institutional cultures grounded in ethical AI principles — transparency, accountability, human dignity — rather than pure performance optimization. His 'doing things ethically' principle applies to institutional culture as much as to individual AI deployments.

**AI Literacy:**

> Holmes has contributed to UNESCO's AI literacy frameworks. He argues that AI literacy must include ethical and critical dimensions — understanding not just how AI works but what values it embeds, who it serves, and what harms it can cause. This is essential for both educators and students.

### Khung pháp lý & Đạo đức

**Khung quốc tế:**

> Holmes has made major contributions to international AI in education governance: IEEE EAD engagement, UNESCO AI guidance, Council of Europe consultancy, European AI Act discourse. His 2021 community ethics framework is the most influential international consensus statement on AIEd ethics.

### Góc nhìn EdTech

**AI tutor vs AI cheater:**

> Holmes co-created the foundational AI-as-tutor argument in Intelligence Unleashed (2016), but his subsequent work adds the crucial corrective: unintended consequences of AI in education require that the tutor vision be implemented ethically, with attention to equity, privacy, and human dignity. He bridges the optimist (Luckin) and critical (Selwyn) positions.

**Chính sách EdTech companies:**

> Holmes' ethics framework applies directly to EdTech company behavior. He has critiqued companies that deploy AI in education without adequate ethical review, transparency, or equity assessment. His UNESCO work includes guidelines for how EdTech companies should operate ethically in educational contexts.

**Đề xuất nhà nghiên cứu EdTech:**

> Holmes recommends: (1) develop community-wide ethics frameworks for AI in education (done — 2021); (2) produce accessible policy guidance for governments and institutions (done — UNESCO 2021/2023); (3) systematically document and monitor unintended consequences of AI deployments; (4) require transparency, human oversight, and equity impact assessment for all educational AI; (5) include AI ethics in educator professional development and student AI literacy curricula.

### Nguồn tài liệu

**Bài báo khoa học:**

> Holmes et al. (2021) International Journal of Artificial Intelligence in Education — 'Ethics of AI in Education: Towards a Community-Wide Framework'; Holmes (2023) Education International — 'The Unintended Consequences of Artificial Intelligence and Education'; Holmes, W., Porayska-Pomsta, K. (eds.) (2022) 'The Ethics of Artificial Intelligence in Education' — Routledge; Intelligence Unleashed (2016) co-authored with Luckin et al.

**Sách tham khảo:**

> Holmes, W. & Porayska-Pomsta, K. (eds.) (2022). The Ethics of Artificial Intelligence in Education: Practices, Challenges, and Debates. Routledge. Holmes, W. et al. (2016). Intelligence Unleashed. Pearson. UNESCO AI and Education: Guidance for Policy-makers (Holmes contributing author).

## John Kirchenbauer et al. {#john-kirchenbauer-et-al}

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Type | researcher |
| Institution | University of Maryland |
| Institution | University of Maryland |
| Specialty | LLM watermarking — green/red token list method |

> **Key Work:** A Watermark for Large Language Models (arXiv 2301.10226, ICML 2023) — pseudorandom green/red vocabulary split; detectable after ~200 tokens at p<0.0001

### Định nghĩa & Khái niệm

**Định nghĩa:**

> LLM watermarking is a technique to embed a statistically detectable signal into AI-generated text by biasing the token selection process, enabling downstream detection without access to the model.

**Phân loại vi phạm:**

> Technical method for AI-generated text detection; differs from stylometric detection or perplexity-based detection; operates at token probability level during decoding.

**Lịch sử khái niệm:**

> Kirchenbauer et al. published arXiv 2301.10226 in January 2023, presented at ICML 2023. Concurrent work by Scott Aaronson (UT Austin/OpenAI) using Gumbel softmax approach. Neither has been deployed in production LLMs as of 2025.

### Bằng chứng thực tế

**Tên trường hợp:** A Watermark for Large Language Models — Kirchenbauer, Geiping, Wen, Kirchenbauer, Goldstein (UMD, 2023)

**Diễn biến:**

> The method uses a pseudorandom function seeded by the preceding token to partition the vocabulary into a 'green list' (~50% of tokens) and 'red list'. During generation, the model is nudged to prefer green-list tokens via logit bias delta. Detection is purely statistical: count the fraction of green tokens; a z-score test at p<0.0001 achieves detection after ~200 tokens.

**Kết quả xử lý:**

> Published at ICML 2023 (top ML conference). Spawned extensive follow-up work. Limitation: (1) paraphrase attacks can remove watermark; (2) multilingual content reduces effectiveness; (3) quality degradation at high delta values; (4) no major LLM provider has deployed it in production.

**Con số thống kê:** Detection threshold: ~200 tokens at p<0.0001; green list = ~50% vocabulary; logit bias delta typically 1.0–2.0; ICML 2023 acceptance rate ~27.9%.

### Tác hại & Hậu quả

**Hại cho người học:** Without deployed watermarking, students who submit AI-generated work cannot be reliably detected via this method, undermining fair assessment.

**Hại cho nghiên cứu:**

> Hallucinated citations and fabricated content in AI-generated papers cannot currently be flagged by watermarking since no production LLM deploys it. The 'arms race' between watermarking and paraphrase-based evasion continues.

**Hại cho xã hội:**

> Lack of deployed watermarking means AI-generated disinformation and academic fraud are harder to attribute to specific models, reducing accountability.

### Phát hiện & Công cụ

**Công cụ phát hiện:**

> Watermark detector is a separate statistical test: compute z-score of green-token fraction. No model access needed for detection — only the pseudorandom seed function (public). Complementary to tools like GPTZero (perplexity) and Turnitin (stylometric).

**Hạn chế công cụ:**

> Key limitations: (1) paraphrase attacks (copy-paste into another LLM) remove watermark; (2) adversarial token substitution; (3) requires knowing which token-hashing scheme the generator used; (4) quality-utility tradeoff at high delta; (5) not robust across translation or heavy editing.

**LLM Watermarking:**

> Kirchenbauer et al. (UMD, ICML 2023): pseudorandom green/red token split, detectable at ~200 tokens p<0.0001. Aaronson (UT Austin/OpenAI, 2022 blog): Gumbel softmax distortion-free variant. Neither deployed in production (2025). OpenAI explored but did not ship; Google DeepMind has independent research. Main obstacle: paraphrase robustness and quality degradation.

### Phòng ngừa & Giải pháp

**Thiết kế bài tập:**

> Watermarking alone cannot replace assessment redesign. Process-based assessment (staged submissions, oral defense) is more robust than detection tools including watermarking.

**Chính sách trường học:**

> No university policy currently mandates watermark-based detection. Policies from Stanford, MIT, NUS focus on disclosure and process-based evaluation rather than technical detection.

**Văn hóa tổ chức:**

> Technical detection tools like watermarking are insufficient without an organizational culture of integrity. McCabe's honor-code research shows culture outperforms enforcement.

**AI Literacy:**

> Understanding watermarking limitations is part of AI literacy for educators: watermarking is a promising but undeployed technical solution, not a current reliable detection method.

### Khung pháp lý & Đạo đức

**Khung quốc tế:**

> EU AI Act Article 50 (effective 2026) requires disclosure of AI-generated content but does not specify watermarking as the technical mechanism. IEEE EAD and ACM Code both emphasize transparency which watermarking could support.

**Pháp luật Việt Nam:** Vietnam's Decree 109/2022 and Luật KH&CN 2025 do not mention watermarking or technical AI detection requirements.

**Chính sách châu Á:** NUS, NTU, CUHK policies rely on disclosure frameworks rather than technical watermarking detection.

### Góc nhìn EdTech

**AI tutor vs AI cheater:** Watermarking is neutral on this debate — it addresses attribution and detection, not whether AI use is educationally beneficial or harmful.

**Chính sách EdTech companies:**

> No major EdTech platform (Khan Academy, Coursera, Duolingo) currently uses watermark-based detection. Detection relies on behavioral signals and post-submission analysis.

**Đề xuất nhà nghiên cứu EdTech:**

> From an EdTech research perspective: watermarking is a technically promising long-term solution but should not be relied upon currently. Assessment redesign and AI literacy education are more actionable near-term strategies.

### Nguồn tài liệu

**Bài báo khoa học:**

> Kirchenbauer et al. (2023) arXiv:2301.10226 / ICML 2023; Aaronson (2022) blog post on Gumbel watermarking; Christ et al. (2023) 'Undetectable Watermarks for Language Models' (cryptographic approach); Zhao et al. (2023) 'Provable Robust Watermarking for AI-Generated Text'.

**Sách tham khảo:** No book-length treatment of LLM watermarking yet (2024). Referenced in Eaton (2024) Second Handbook and in IEEE/ACM technical reports.

**Nguồn Việt Nam:** No Vietnam-specific watermarking research identified. General AI policy gap in Vietnam (Decree 109/2022) extends to watermarking.

## Thomas Lancaster {#thomas-lancaster}

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Type | researcher |
| Institution | Imperial College London (Principal Teaching Fellow in Computing); formerly Birmingham City University |
| Institution | Imperial College London (Principal Teaching Fellow in Computing); formerly Birmingham City University |
| Specialty | Contract cheating, AI in academic integrity, plagiarism detection, essay mills |

### Định nghĩa & Khái niệm

**Định nghĩa:**

> Academic integrity violations include contract cheating — where a student submits work completed by a third party as their own — a term Lancaster and Clarke (2006) helped define and popularize. With the rise of generative AI, this definition now extends to AI-assisted submission where AI providers function analogously to criminal essay mills.

**Phân loại vi phạm:**

> Lancaster's work covers plagiarism, contract cheating (essay mills, file-sharing sites), AI-assisted cheating (ChatGPT and similar LLMs used to generate assessments), and fabricated citations. His 2024 work with Cotarlan frames AI providers as criminal essay mills — a new category in the taxonomy of academic misconduct.

**Lịch sử khái niệm:**

> Lancaster and Clarke coined the term 'contract cheating' in a 2006 paper identifying outsourced academic work as a distinct integrity threat. The concept evolved from simple plagiarism detection into a multi-front challenge encompassing file-sharing, essay mills, and from 2022 onward, generative AI tools.

### Bằng chứng thực tế

**Tên trường hợp:**

> Lancaster & Cotarlan (2024): AI providers as criminal essay mills (ICT Law Journal); Lancaster (2019): systemic analysis of contract cheating policies (Journal of Academic Ethics 17(2):101-122); Lancaster 2025: policies for historic contract cheating and generative AI misuse.

**Diễn biến:**

> Lancaster has documented that AI providers such as ChatGPT and similar LLMs can be used to fulfill essay-mill-style requests, bypassing traditional detection. His 2024 ICT Law paper argues these constitute criminal academic misconduct analogous to illegal essay mills. He has also catalogued the evolution from simple file-sharing cheating services (pre-2010) to sophisticated AI tools (2022+).

**Kết quả xử lý:**

> Lancaster advocates for policy frameworks that retrospectively address contract cheating and proactively govern generative AI misuse. His 2025 paper on developing policies for historic cheating and GenAI reflects ongoing institutional responses. He has been cited by lawmakers and regulatory bodies in UK discussions about banning essay mills.

**Con số thống kê:**

> Lancaster has been cited by over 3,200 scholars (Google Scholar). His research spans over 20 years and covers multiple jurisdictions. As of 2025, he has contributed to policies at Imperial College London and beyond.

### Tác hại & Hậu quả

**Hại cho người học:**

> Students who rely on contract cheating or AI-generated submissions fail to develop critical thinking, subject expertise, or professional skills. Lancaster argues this creates a hollow credential — a graduate who cannot perform the competencies their degree certifies.

**Hại cho nghiên cứu:**

> When AI-generated text is submitted as original academic work, it introduces unverified claims and potentially hallucinated citations into the knowledge base, eroding the integrity of peer review and scholarly discourse.

**Hại cho xã hội:**

> Widespread contract cheating degrades the value of academic credentials, undermines employer trust in graduates, and — in safety-critical fields (medicine, engineering, law) — poses direct public safety risks.

### Phát hiện & Công cụ

**Công cụ phát hiện:**

> Lancaster has researched detection tools including Turnitin, GPTZero, and digital watermarking. He notes that AI-generated text detection is an active arms race between detection tools and humanization/paraphrasing tools that circumvent them.

**Hạn chế công cụ:**

> Lancaster acknowledges significant limitations: AI detectors produce false positives especially for non-native English speakers, and humanized AI text is difficult to reliably detect. He argues that detection alone is insufficient — structural assessment redesign is necessary.

**LLM Watermarking:**

> Lancaster has written about LLM watermarking as a potential technical solution. He notes that cryptographic and statistical watermarking (e.g., UMD Kirchenbauer approach) is promising but not yet universally deployable, especially for open-source models.

### Phòng ngừa & Giải pháp

**Thiết kế bài tập:**

> Lancaster advocates for assessment redesign as a primary preventive measure — oral defenses, process portfolios, staged submissions, and personalized tasks that are difficult for AI or essay mills to complete authentically.

**Chính sách trường học:**

> His 2019 and 2025 papers provide frameworks for institutional policy, covering detection, deterrence, education, and retrospective handling of detected cases. He has contributed to policy at Imperial College London and to national UK discussions.

**Văn hóa tổ chức:**

> Lancaster emphasizes that deterrence-only policies are insufficient. He argues for culture-building approaches that make academic integrity a positive value rather than merely a compliance obligation.

**AI Literacy:**

> Lancaster's work implicitly supports AI literacy as a precondition for responsible use — students and educators must understand AI capabilities and limitations to distinguish legitimate AI-assisted work from AI fraud.

### Khung pháp lý & Đạo đức

**Khung quốc tế:**

> Lancaster's research aligns with ICAI (International Center for Academic Integrity) principles and has informed policy in the UK, Australia, and internationally. His ICT Law paper on AI as criminal essay mills engages with legal and regulatory frameworks.

### Góc nhìn EdTech

**AI tutor vs AI cheater:**

> Lancaster is firmly focused on the cheating dimension of AI — framing AI tools as potential criminal instruments analogous to essay mills. He does not foreground the 'AI as tutor' narrative in his work, representing the integrity-threat side of the debate.

**Chính sách EdTech companies:**

> Lancaster's work on AI as essay mills implicitly critiques the lack of safeguards in AI platforms that allow academic fraud. He calls for legal and policy accountability for providers that knowingly facilitate contract cheating.

**Đề xuất nhà nghiên cứu EdTech:**

> Lancaster recommends: (1) legal frameworks criminalizing AI-assisted essay mill services; (2) institutional policies covering both detection and prevention; (3) assessment redesign to reduce opportunities for AI fraud; (4) retrospective policies for handling historical cases; (5) international regulatory coordination.

### Nguồn tài liệu

**Bài báo khoa học:**

> Lancaster & Clarke (2006) — coined 'contract cheating' term; Lancaster (2019) J. Academic Ethics 17(2):101-122; Lancaster & Cotarlan (2024) ICT Law Journal — AI as criminal essay mills; Lancaster (2025) — policies for historic cheating and GenAI.

**Sách tham khảo:**

> Lancaster has contributed chapters to Bretag's Handbook of Academic Integrity (Springer 2016). His own website (thomaslancaster.co.uk) hosts research reports and policy briefs.

### Uncertain Fields

- phap_luat_viet_nam
- chinh_sach_chau_a
- nguon_viet_nam

## Weixin Liang et al. {#weixin-liang-et-al}

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Type | researcher |
| Institution | Stanford University (Weixin Liang, Mert Yuksekgonul, Yining Mao, Eric Wu, James Zou) |
| Institution | Stanford University (Weixin Liang, Mert Yuksekgonul, Yining Mao, Eric Wu, James Zou) |
| Specialty | AI detector bias against non-native English speakers |

> **Key Work:** Liang et al. (2023) Patterns/Cell Press doi:10.1016/j.patter.2023.100779 — 61.3% false positive on TOEFL essays; arXiv:2304.02819; triggered Vanderbilt disabling Turnitin

### Định nghĩa & Khái niệm

**Định nghĩa:**

> AI detector bias refers to the systematic tendency of AI-generated text detection tools (such as Turnitin, GPTZero, Copyleaks) to produce disproportionately high false-positive rates for texts written by non-native English speakers (ESL writers), due to lower lexical diversity, simpler syntactic structures, and higher word-frequency patterns that mimic AI output.

**Phân loại vi phạm:**

> Type of harm: false positive (Type I error) in AI detection — legitimate human-written work incorrectly flagged as AI-generated. Subcategory: algorithmic bias / disparate impact on ESL student populations.

**Lịch sử khái niệm:**

> Liang et al. submitted arXiv:2304.02819 in April 2023; published in Patterns (Cell Press) in July 2023 (doi:10.1016/j.patter.2023.100779). This was the first peer-reviewed study documenting systematic ESL bias. The findings directly caused Vanderbilt University to disable Turnitin AI detection in 2023.

### Bằng chứng thực tế

**Tên trường hợp:** GPT detectors are biased against non-native English writers — Liang, Yuksekgonul, Mao, Wu, Zou (Stanford HAI / Zou Lab, 2023)

**Diễn biến:**

> The team tested 7 popular AI detectors (including GPTZero, Turnitin, Crossplag, Writer, Copyleaks, ZeroGPT, Sapling) on: (1) English essays by Chinese college students (TOEFL test bank), (2) US 8th-grade student writing, (3) ChatGPT-generated essays. Result: 61.3% of legitimate TOEFL essays were flagged as AI-generated; only 1.5% of native-speaker essays were false-positives. Simple prompt interventions ('elevate the writing') made ChatGPT output evade detection at >90% rate.

**Kết quả xử lý:**

> Published in Patterns (Cell Press) — high-impact cross-disciplinary journal. Vanderbilt University disabled Turnitin AI detection citing this study. Northwestern and UT Austin also reconsidered AI detection policies. Turnitin issued a response acknowledging higher false-positive rates on ESL writing but disputed the 61.3% figure for their product specifically.

**Con số thống kê:**

> 61.3% false positive rate on TOEFL essays; 1.5% false positive on native-speaker writing; 7 tools tested; >90% evasion rate when ChatGPT output was prompted to 'elevate language'; published July 2023; James Zou lab (Stanford) — ~500+ citations by 2024.

### Tác hại & Hậu quả

**Hại cho người học:**

> Non-native English speaking students face disproportionate accusation of AI dishonesty for work they wrote themselves. Consequences include grade penalties (0 marks), academic misconduct proceedings, psychological harm, and chilling effect on language development (students may avoid advanced vocabulary to avoid flags).

**Hại cho nghiên cứu:**

> If AI detectors are embedded in peer review or journal submission systems, ESL researchers from non-English-speaking countries face unfair screening. This compounds existing inequity in global academic publishing.

**Hại cho xã hội:**

> Systemic false accusation of marginalized groups (international students, ESL learners) erodes institutional trust, reinforces xenophobic assumptions, and devalues international education. Institutions that over-rely on AI detection tools risk legal liability.

### Phát hiện & Công cụ

**Công cụ phát hiện:**

> Turnitin AI Detection, GPTZero, Copyleaks, Writer AI Detector, ZeroGPT, Sapling, Crossplag — all tested in the study. All showed statistically significant ESL bias. Primary detection methods: perplexity (entropy of token probabilities), burstiness (variance in perplexity), stylometric frequency analysis.

**Hạn chế công cụ:**

> Critical limitation: current AI detectors use statistical proxies (perplexity, burstiness, token frequency) that correlate with both AI generation AND with non-native speaker writing patterns. 61.3% false positive on TOEFL essays (Liang et al. 2023). Simple paraphrasing or style elevation prompts defeat detection. 'Humanizing' tools (Quillbot, Undetectable.ai) explicitly designed to evade. Detectors cannot distinguish ESL human writing from AI output.

**LLM Watermarking:**

> Liang et al.'s work motivates watermarking as a more reliable alternative to stylometric detection — if LLMs embedded provenance signals, false positives against ESL writers could be eliminated. However, no production watermarking exists (2025).

### Phòng ngừa & Giải pháp

**Thiết kế bài tập:**

> Implication for assessment design: AI detection tools cannot be the primary defense. Process-based assessment (staged submissions, oral defense, reflective journals) are equitable across ESL and native speakers and do not produce false positives.

**Chính sách trường học:**

> Vanderbilt (2023): disabled Turnitin AI detection citing Liang et al. Michigan State, Northwestern, UT Austin: suspended or limited AI detection use. Stanford's own policy emphasizes instructor judgment over automated detection. Turnitin updated guidance to warn against sole reliance on AI scores.

**Văn hóa tổ chức:**

> Over-reliance on detection tools without cultural context creates punitive, inequitable environments. McCabe's honor-code culture model — where students self-regulate — is more effective and equitable than enforcement-by-algorithm.

**AI Literacy:**

> Educators must understand AI detector limitations including ESL bias before deploying tools. AI literacy curricula should include critical evaluation of detection tool accuracy, not just AI use ethics.

### Khung pháp lý & Đạo đức

**Khung quốc tế:**

> EU AI Act Annex III flags educational assessment tools as high-risk AI systems, requiring accuracy testing and bias audits — directly relevant to AI detector deployments. UNESCO 2023 guidance warns against automated academic integrity enforcement without human oversight.

**Pháp luật Việt Nam:**

> Vietnam's Decree 109/2022 does not address AI detection tool deployment in academic integrity enforcement. No regulatory requirement to audit AI detection tools for ESL bias in Vietnamese context.

**Chính sách châu Á:**

> NUS (Singapore) explicitly warns against sole reliance on AI detection tools in its AI academic integrity framework (2023/2024). CUHK (Hong Kong) does not detail specific tool restrictions. NTU issued zero marks for AI hallucinated citations (2025) — based on content analysis, not detection tools.

### Góc nhìn EdTech

**AI tutor vs AI cheater:**

> Liang et al.'s work demonstrates that 'AI as cheater' framing disproportionately harms ESL students — reinforcing Neil Selwyn's critique that systemic inequity drives both cheating behavior and disproportionate enforcement. Rose Luckin's democratization argument is undermined if detection tools introduce new forms of discrimination.

**Chính sách EdTech companies:**

> Turnitin responded to Liang et al. by updating product guidance (not disabling the feature). GPTZero improved false-positive rates in later versions but ESL bias persists. Coursera's post-submission dialogue approach (behavioral) avoids this bias issue. Khan Academy's Khanmigo avoids detection entirely via Socratic design.

**Đề xuất nhà nghiên cứu EdTech:**

> Recommendation: (1) Institutions should immediately audit AI detection tool false-positive rates on ESL student populations before deployment. (2) No disciplinary action should be taken based solely on AI detection scores. (3) Assessment redesign (oral defense, process-based) is more equitable. (4) Advocate for deployed LLM watermarking as a bias-free alternative long-term.

### Nguồn tài liệu

**Bài báo khoa học:**

> Liang et al. (2023) 'GPT detectors are biased against non-native English writers' Patterns/Cell Press doi:10.1016/j.patter.2023.100779; arXiv:2304.02819. Perkins et al. (2023) 'Chatting and cheating' IETI. Newton & Xiromeriti (2023) Innovations in Education and Teaching International. Haider et al. (2024) Harvard Misinformation Review.

**Sách tham khảo:**

> Eaton (2024) Second Handbook of Academic Integrity (Springer) — covers AI detection bias. Eaton (2021) Plagiarism in Higher Education — ESL and cultural context chapters.

**Nguồn Việt Nam:**

> Springer 2024 study on 1,386 Vietnamese university students (doi:10.1007/s10639-024-12495-4) — discusses detection challenges for ESL Vietnamese writers. Perkins (BUV) — works with Vietnamese ESL students, directly relevant.
